## A.21. Executive summary (max: 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

### Climate change problem

(i) Tajikistan is one of the most vulnerable countries in Central Asia to climate risks.* Temperatures are increasing across the country and there is a clear shift in precipitation patterns. These changes pose a threat to food security and livelihoods. The agricultural sector is particularly vulnerable, as it is directly and negatively impacted by the higher temperatures that increase evapotranspiration and have an overall negative effect on crop yields. Climate change also has a direct impact on livestock production and animal agriculture production, such as the Climate Adaptation in Rural Development (CARD) * predicts that climate change will affect both irrigated and rain-fed production systems - and under the pessimistic risk setting at one standard deviation above the mean, Tajikistan’s agricultural sector is projected to be severely affected by 2050. Rice production will be most affected, after wheat and sunflower.* Climate change is also expected to increase the frequency of extreme weather events such as floods, droughts, heatwaves, and cold spells. This can, paradoxically, lead to overstocking as a risk mitigation measure by smaller farmers trying to sustain their livestock through periods of scarcity. The combination of these factors will have an adverse effect on animal health, welfare and productivity through emergence of new diseases, and increased temperature on animals and crops. Climate change also has a direct impact on the country’s forests, which are under threat from wildfires, landslides and droughts. Climate projections predict a worsening of the trends and events, with significant vulnerability to climate change in Tajikistan. The agricultural sector is already facing challenges due to its high level of poverty and dependence on the agricultural sector present significant challenges for future adaptation. The climate change vulnerability analyses suggest higher adaptation needs in rural mountainous areas.

### Proposed interventions

The proposed interventions build on the successful experiences of four IFAD-funded projects over the years in the most vulnerable mountainous areas of Tajikistan, specifically on the Livestock and Pasture Development Projects (phase I and II) while adding the missing focus on climate resilience and renewable energy. The project will support the Government of Tajikistan to implement a comprehensive approach for sustainable development in rural mountainous areas through:
- Improved livestock management and livelihood diversification (relevant projects referred to in Section B.3 and B.4)
- Improved water resources management
- Improved land use planning and soil conservation
- Improved forest management

Annex 30 explaining the evolution of IFAD country programmes and how the latter has evolved into the CARD approach, which is now being implemented by Tajikistan. The project will support communities and country’s capacity to plan for climate risks and modify its policy and regulatory framework to incentivise production practices that are more resilient to climate change and improve the governance of natural resources in rural mountainous areas. The project will also support the Government of Tajikistan to incorporate climate diagnostics at the local level planning frameworks. This will help to identify investments that can be made to increase the resilience of communities, households and enterprises through more resilient investments in water and irrigation infrastructure, technologies and farm equipment which have been identified as key adaptation measures for agriculture. The project will also support the Government of Tajikistan to strengthen its capacity to manage climate risks within value chains which are most vulnerable to climate risks such as livestock, dairy, meat, horticulture to increase productivity and income generation in rural mountainous areas.

The project has a seven-year duration with GCF financing and multiple co-financers, as one coordinated climate investment. CARD will be executed by three executing entities according to respective comparative advantages: (a) Ministry of Finance; (b) Ministry of Agriculture (via the State Enterprise Project Management Unit Livestock and Pasture Development (PMU)); (c) Committee on Environmental Protection (through the Center for Implementation).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V6.0 | PAGE A

## Climate results-benefits

### (a) It is expected that the proposed activities can catalyze impact beyond a one-off project investment and lead to broader impacts in key areas.

The project is designed to transform Tajikistan's approach through explicit consideration of climate change risks, vulnerabilities and adaptation measures. The project will focus on increasing agricultural productivity by improving soil fertility levels to assist households diversify their livelihoods. At the local level, the project will develop mechanisms for the identification and implementation of locally-appropriate solutions that can build a long-term legacy of local capacity to adapt to climate change.

### (b) The project is expected to have an impact on both adaptation and mitigation. Specifically, the project will contribute to the sequestration of an estimated 6,854,922 tonnes of CO2 equivalent through management of degraded forests and pastures, improved agricultural practices, and improved livestock and herd management (Annex 3.1: Carbon Sequestration Analysis; Annex 3.2: Greenhouse Gas Accounting). The project is expected to reach out to 650,000 direct individual beneficiaries or about 100,000 households in the most vulnerable districts of Tajikistan (Annex 24: Beneficiary Estimates) in 21 of the most climate vulnerable districts (Annex Z.1, Annex 30). The expected economic return is estimated at US$58 million with an internal rate of return of 23.6% and a Net Present Value of US$ 79.3 million with significant co-benefits for biodiversity conservation factors.

### (c) The project is aligned with national and international climate change commitments of Tajikistan, in particular the Nationally Determined Contributions (NDC, 2021) that identify agriculture and forestry as vehicles to achieve a progressive reduction of climate change vulnerability as well as to enhance carbon sequestration. The project will contribute to the achievement of these NDCs by providing the necessary investments for adaptation and disaster risk reduction. References to measures relevant to the project include: gender equality, poverty alleviation, food security, rural development, disaster risk reduction, and social inclusiveness, specifically focusing on gender-based development.

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B.1 Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

#### 1. Climate profile: Tajikistan has been described as one of the most vulnerable countries to climate change by the World Bank. It ranks in this regard on 98th position on the ND-Gain ranking whose indicators highlight among others a low capacity to acquire and deploy climate smart agriculture technologies. The country is located at an altitude between sea level and 7500 meters above sea level, with mountainous and roughly half of all settlements in the country are located above 3000 meters. The country has a continental climate characterized by long cold winters and short warm summers. It affects all the climatic variables (Annex 2 and Annex 6). The local weather station data analyzed shows that temperatures are rising across the country, with a sharper increase in the period 1970-2017 (Figure 1).

**Figure 1:** Average mean temperature in Kulob and in Tajikistan for the period 1901-2017.

| Year | Kulob weather station |
| --- | --- |
| 1850 | 3.4°C |
| 1860 | 3.5°C |
| ... | ... |

#### 2. The trend is higher during winter months, as registered by the local weather station in Fedchenko glacier with detected impacts on glacier and snow cover. A significant decrease in the snow extent in mountainous areas has been observed.

#### 3. At the national level, annual precipitation (rain, sleet, and snow) rose by 15-20% through the 1901-2016 period. The increase in precipitation is concentrated in the February-May season mostly in Western Tajikistan. In contrast, the precipitation has decreased significantly during the June-October season at the national level (Figure 2).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL V8.0 | PAGE 5

## Figure 2: Annual precipitation (1901-2018) (left) and Monthly rainfall trend (1981-2018) (right) in Tajikistan

There is a shift in the precipitation pattern, which has a major impact on water availability between the end of the dry season in May and the beginning of it in November–December, which, in turn, contributes to increasing the vulnerability of the ecosystems (i.e. heavy rainfall events, extreme temperatures, droughts, floods). This is also reflected by changes in the agricultural calendar as well as to the pasture productivity within the year, changing availability of pastures and grazing land for livestock. The shift in precipitation pattern has been more pronounced since 1980, with the negative trend is more evident in grassland compared to cropland. The main differences between the two can be attributed to the fact that most parts of crops are irrigated and can better withstand changes in precipitation.

The climate change vulnerability assessment for Tajikistan indicates that the country will face increased water stress, exacerbated by poor management practices and limited capacity to adapt to climate risks. Numerous research studies have shown that climate change is likely to increase the frequency and intensity of droughts on land and water management practices. A World Bank estimate puts losses due to forest degradation in Tajikistan at US$10 million per year.

In terms of adaptation, increasing irrigation efficiency, improving soil fertility through better pasture management and increased fodder conservation will be a considerable contribution to the reduction of adverse impacts.

Climate projections predict a worsening of the trends and events, with significant impacts on ecosystems, livelihoods and the economy. Crops are already experiencing reduction due to climate change and extreme weather events. There is an increase of 30 percent in irrigation demand by crops (divine) since 1985. The projected increase in temperature will have a negative impact on crop yields, especially affecting productivity. The Climate Adaptation in Rural Development (CARD) analysis concludes that climate change will reduce the yield of wheat and barley by about 20% to 30%. In addition, the area sown with crops is expected to decrease moderately in the next five years up to 2025 and more seriously from 2026 onwards. The projected increase in temperature will have a negative impact on crop yields, especially affecting productivity.

The Moderate risk setting, most of the crops' yields are projected to decrease moderately (under full irrigation) or stay stable in some cases (no irrigation) or increase (Rice, no irrigation).

### 5. Climate hazards

Climate-induced extreme weather events (such as floods, droughts, heatwaves, landslides and windstorms) have become more frequent and intense as a result of warming indicated in Figure 2 below. Heavy rainfall events (>10mm/hour) are becoming more intense at the end of summer. Beginning of fall in Gorno-Badakhshan and Dushanbe regions (rainfall, snowfall, hail, thunderstorms, floods, landslides, windstorms, heatwaves and mudflows). From 1992 to 2015, natural disasters affected 7 million people in Tajikistan (more than 80% of the population) and caused US$3.6 billion in economic losses. The average annual cost of climate-induced extreme weather events are currently estimated at $600 million, or 4.8% of GDP. The mudflows and floods in 1998, 1999, 2005 and 2010 were the most devastating.

8. Emission trends. Tajikistan is not a major emitter when assessed on emissions per capita. The latest Nationally Determined Contribution (2023) reports that after energy (60% of greenhouse gas emission), agriculture is a key source of greenhouse gas emissions (35% of greenhouse gases). The Fourth National Communication (2019) estimates that agriculture contributes 48 million tons of CO₂ equivalent to the country’s total GHG emissions. Specifically, methane (CH₄) emissions from agriculture comprise the 91% of all emissions of this gas in Tajikistan. Nitrous oxide (N₂O), which is a potent greenhouse gas, accounts for 50% of Total Oxide (NOx) from the agriculture sector representing 48.7% of these gases. Based on FCASTAT data extrapolated to 2030, it is estimated that agricultural emissions will increase by 16%. In particular, in the agriculture sector, 57% of GHG emissions were estimated to be due to enteric fermentation from livestock and 43% from manure management. The country has a large livestock population (8 million grazing) Despite being a low emitter, Tajikistan has an important potential to reduce its emissions through both traditional methods like improving feeding practices and improving digestibility of diets improved animal health and feed efficiency. Other mitigation measures include the use of biogas plants for energy production, the sequestration of carbon, through reforestation and improving pasture management (Annex 23).

### Climate projections.
Projections by the Coupled Model Intercomparison Project Phase II (CMIP4) produced by the World Climate Research Programme indicate that in the next decades temperatures will be warmer by approximately 1.0°C to 2.7°C for 2030 and 1.8°C to 4.2°C for 2050, while days hot (>30°C) are expected to increase from an average of 6 per year in the period 1980-2009 to between 10 and 27 by 2050. Annual precipitation is projected to remain stable, but the number of consecutive dry days is expected to increase. Tajikistan has experienced a significant decrease in rainfall since the mid-1960s, with severe droughts. The higher temperatures and melting glaciers are already contributing to higher risk of severe droughts. Similarly, projected hot day (>35°C) would steadily

### 10. Climate vulnerability analysis

A climate vulnerability index analysis[7] was carried out for the design of CASP, using a tool called IV – summary of results shown in Figure 5.[8] The analysis shows higher vulnerability to climate in the Eastern and Centre Khatoon, Western DRS and in the South East of Sugd. On the other hand, the North West of Sugd is less vulnerable.

Indeed, Tajikistan is expected to have very strong hydrological changes and impacts given its snow based hydrological systems, and thus, would have serious implications for its future land use (see climate data in Annex 2.3). By 2050, the main crops' yield[9] could decrease by between 5% and 35%, and pastures on which livestock are grazed will be reduced by up to 40%. This is due to a combination of increased high temperatures and increased evaporation rates.[10] Temperature increases are also likely to modify ecosystems and provide opportunities for pests and diseases to emerge and/or multiply, affecting a wide range of crops and livestock. ## Climate Change and Agriculture

### Vulnerability of Rural Areas to Climate Change

12. From a socio-economic perspective, these areas present higher vulnerability of the population, lower quality of life (access to water which will be worsened because of climate change and electricity) and poverty levels. The most vulnerable rural communities are in the regions of Sugd (30.2%) and Khaton (36.7%). These findings are in line and complement literature on vulnerability of mountainous areas, where people have limited access to services and face adaptation needs in rural mountainous area, with predominance of agroforestry and livestock related livelihoods.

13. Climate change has a direct impact on pasture areas as described in Annex 2.1. The degradation of pastures over time is evident in the country and is directly affected by climate change creating more stress for herders. In addition to this, the frequency and intensity of drought episodes have increased over time; it is the ones that are affected by more concentrated rainfall and longer dry periods - heavier daily rainfall events and cumulative 5 days rainfall events, less distributed over the year. Furthermore, the temperature has also increased in the country, which has a direct impact on pasture areas.

14. Forage production is also decreasing because of climate trends which are contributing to declining grasslands and increasing desertification. This is further exacerbated by the increase in frequency and duration of drought episodes. Projections of managed grass yields for median and pessimistic risk settings under RCP 8.5 show a decrease in grass yield over time, with a significant reduction after 2040.

15. Reductions in agriculture and pasture productivity will adversely affect the overall nutritional status and health of the population, especially women, children, and elders. Women and young girls are extremely vulnerable to the effects of climate change since they already have poor access to water and sanitation. Data from Tajikistan shows that 30% of the population is undernourished, half of the children under five suffer from iodine deficiency and a quarter from stunting (WFP, 2019). The country's food insecurity will increase

The share of women working in the agriculture sector is higher (75%) of all women employed, but they have less access to productive resources, limited decision-making, and discrimination are also characteristic of agricultural work.

The COVID-19 pandemic has exacerbated the already highly vulnerable conditions, impacting severely the livelihoods of millions of people. The crisis has had a disproportionate impact on self-employed, migrant labourers and non-registered workers. Lack of savings and the presence of loans with high interest rates make it difficult for these groups to cope with the economic shocks.

Out of five micro-small and medium enterprises (MSMEs) were negatively affected by the pandemic, with half of them having to close down their business. ### Geographic focus CASP+ has been designed to fail with ongoing projects financed by other IFIs and development partners, and its geographic targeting ensures complementarity with ongoing projects (see also Annex 21, Section 3.2). The project will be implemented in Tajikistan, Afghanistan, Pakistan, and assess how to capitalize on these initiatives in building resilience of local communities and infrastructure. What is the rationale for this selection? It is based on a combination of vulnerability assessment and ongoing interventions of a similar nature (Annex 21, §3). The participatory identification of the vulnerable areas was carried out by the Government of Tajikistan with support from IFAD, ADB, EBRD and other donor projects and CASP+. The Government and the ESF have a list of all villages in 21 districts and the selection is taking place as a primary IFAD-financed project activity. Available statistics for each village were used to identify the most vulnerable areas based on the vulnerability index (the objective selection (the procedure and criteria are described in Annex 21 Section 2.3, §28 and subsequent).

The geographic targeting was based on climate change vulnerability and the potential of the project to enhance resilience at the community level. The project will be implemented in the Gorno-Badakhshan region and 2 in the Sugd region (Figure 7). The selection was based on the IFAD developed Vulnerability Index, which is a composite index that combines several indicators related to climate change vulnerability parameters. The average vulnerability index of the project area is higher than the national average (Figure 8). The selected districts are characterized by high poverty rates and low access to basic services. The selected districts experienced heavy precipitation events, specifically in Sh Dhon, Kholgun, Balvijan, Gesor and Shakhrin in Khodjent. Most of the project districts are also situated in the area where the average precipitation is higher than the national average (Figure 9).

After selecting the districts, a water sub-catchments based approach was used to identify the most vulnerable basins within each district. The vulnerability criteria was calculated at sub-catchment level (see targeting methodology). This allows for a more detailed analysis of the project area and provides a finer sub-catchment basin level to apply a landscape approach (each village will be assigned a vulnerability index depending on its location).

### 20. Synergies with community-based ecosystem restoration (Funded Interventions)
Ongoing projects relevant to CASP+ scope include all the World Bank-financed Tajikistan Resilient Landscapes Restoration Project (RESILAND) which is designed to increase adoption of landscape restoration practices by smallholder farmers and herders in Tajikistan. The project also includes a component that supports the Climate Adaptation and Mitigation Program for the Aral Sea Basin (CAMPAS/ArS) designed to scale up the project's successful regional initiatives and climate investments and to equip and ultimately enable countries in Central Asia to implement similar projects in the future. This is being done by building on existing services, investments, and capacity-building of key stakeholders of the World Bank as also supporting the Tajikistan government’s efforts to improve livelihoods and food security through improved land management and improved rural livelihoods and food security in Tajikistan.

The World Bank funds are expected to be leveraged with other development partners including USAID, GEF, and others. The project is being implemented under support through the Climate Adaptation through Sustainable Forestry in Important River Catchment Areas in Tajikistan (CAFT 2015-2019) that supported piloting Joint Forest Management and a second phase is underway to scale up this approach across the country. The World Bank has also been supporting CAFT and has incorporated many of its lessons in the design. Overall coordination and synergies will be built through the existing mechanisms such as the National Climate Change Secretariat, the Development Coordination Council where relevant with other government and donor initiatives.

### 21. Additional synergies with and from IFAD investments
CASP+ builds on the successful experience of ongoing and past IFAD-financed and MoU-implemented projects in the country targeting livelihoods improvement through sustainable land management practices. The project will also benefit from CASP+ which complements such successful investment with interventions to enhance climate resilience and improve food security. The project will also build on lessons learned from other IFAD investments by working with other IFAD operations in the country and in similar fields. This includes synergies with the GEF programming, innovations on integrating actors on remittances and migrants’ entrepreneurship; and synergy for scaling up successful interventions.

First: On GEF: CASP+ will identify synergies with the new GEF 8 programme titled “Tajikistan Ecosystem Restoration and Climate Change Mitigation” (TERRA) which is expected to be launched in early 2017. The synergies between CASP+ and the TERRA initiative will be capitalised upon. Discussions are ongoing to consider TERKA as a potential CASP+ co-financing or parallel financing.

Second: To boost additional socio-economic

## B

### Economic priorities for resilience building. IFAD will also lease CASP with its EU-funded Action titled "Platform for Remittances, Investments and Migrants' Entrepreneurship in Central Asia" (PRIME). The project is designed to promote faster, safer and cheaper transfer of remittances, as well as leveraging these funds into investments in the agricultural sector. This will contribute to improving food security and overall well-being of migrant workers’ families in Central Asia including in Tajikistan. There is a considerable scope for collaboration between IFAD and CASP on this project.

### The programme titled "Dairy Interventions for Adaptation and Migration" (DAMA) which seeks to promote Low Carbon Dairy Development (LCD) in Tajikistan, Kyrgyzstan and Uzbekistan, is also aligned with the goals of the project. Both projects are well-aligned, with different dimensions being covered but also benefiting from the assessments carried out for the design of CASP. CASP and the DAMA Project can explore opportunities for exchange of information and experiences between these projects through visits to each other’s sites, as well as the exchange of experiences between these projects through exchange visits budget lines embedded in the respective project proposals. The two projects are also aligned with the Sustainable Land Management Triangular Cooperation (SCTC), as well as through documentation and sharing of knowledge and lessons.

### The project will capitalize on the collaboration between IFAD and FAO, and leverage technical assistance of the latter to support the implementation of the project in Tajikistan. 

#### B.2 (a) Theory of change narrative and diagram (see 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

**22. The overall goal of the project is to contribute to the country’s shift towards low emission sustainable development pathways and climate-adaptive agricultural production practices. The specific objectives are:**

- To enhance capacity for evidence-based planning, management and evaluation of natural resources at national and local levels;
- To strengthen institutional resilience at the national level; 
- Enhance capacity at the district and village level for climate adaptive planning and mitigating the impact of climate change, and building more effective links with the private sector to help improve livelihoods.

**23. The Theory of Change has been developed to address some of the key barriers in the path to improve livelihood resilience and carbon sequestration in rural areas (see Figure 8: Theory of change CASP, and Annex A).**

#### [a] Institutional barriers: limited capacity for evidence-based planning, management and evaluation of natural resources at national and local levels; translating into fragmented governance of the natural resource base and lack of coordination among different stakeholders and institutions regulating the access in different ways.

#### [b] Technical barriers: limited awareness of climate change impacts for rural populations; and low productivity of agricultural systems. Limited capacity at local level and lack of stimuli to revert the trend of unproductive livestock population growth, hampering efficient and sustainable rangeland management.

#### [c] Financial barriers: related to limited public investment, increasing but geographically still scattered and inefficient use of resources; and inadequate financial system with consequent limited financial options (products, systems) in rural areas for productive investments in forestry, livestock/rangeland management and income diversification.

#### [d] Social barriers: such as unique socio-economic structure, discriminating rural women and disadvantaged households. Limited access to information networks, especially in vulnerable contexts; barriers to human capital development and services and ultimately investment for rural populations.

The underlying theory of change of the project is that IF coordination and capacity at the national level is strengthened and enabling environment for natural resource management is enhanced and community is assisted in the formulation of climate sensitive action plans, provision of new technologies.

## Figure 8: Theory of change in CASPh

| Component | Description |
| --- | --- |
| **Component 1:** Enabling environment | The enabling environment is a set of conditions that allow or facilitate the implementation of policies, programmes and projects. It includes the institutional framework for governance and management of natural resources, including land tenure systems, legal frameworks, institutions and capacity building. |
| **Component 2:** Community investment | This component aims to enhance resilience of households in the production system by providing them with access to productive assets (e.g., seeds, tools) and services (e.g., credit). It also includes the development of common interest groups for vulnerable households and commercialisation and agribusiness development. |
| **Component 3:** Value chains | This component aims to enhance resilience of households in the production system by providing them with access to productive assets (e.g., seeds, tools) and services (e.g., credit). It also includes the development of common interest groups for vulnerable households and commercialisation and agribusiness development. |

25. One of the key postulates of CASPh theory of change is premised on the assertion that helping to shift the development pathway in a low emission and climate resilient direction requires a series of investments in the public sector at the national level to strengthen the governance and management regime for natural resources, including land tenure systems, legal frameworks, institutions and capacity building. While the National Climate Change Action Strategy (NCCAS) is being developed, the National Adaptation Plan (NAP), further legislative and policy reforms in natural resource management are important. The NCCAS will be informed by the NAP and other relevant national strategies such as the National Climate Change Strategy (NCICS) may strengthen. Besides reinforcing the enabling environment (component 1), CASPh intends to facilitate community investment (component 2) to enhance resilience of the households in the production system, and value chains (component 3). The investments will be made within selected value chains that will help to build demand for local enterprises and aid in diversification.

## The theory of change of the project is based on the understanding that with facilitation support, communities can improve their access to and use of equipment, communities can mobilize, plan and implement Community Action Plans that address some of the key issues of vulnerability to climate risks. Note: CAPS+ will not be able to provide all of these services. The role of PUS is pivotal. Adequately supported and accompanied in climate-sensitive community investment, PUSs will be able to identify and adopt appropriate adaptation measures and technologies. This will ensure that the appropriate incentives also reduce methane emission intensity. Evidence from previous IFAD investments shows that a 10% increase in livestock productivity can lead to an overall reduction of 25% in land degradation trend (summarized in Annex 18f). In addition, the assessment has also shown that the past project investment generated a decrease of livestock units owned by the beneficiaries. This approach can ultimately contribute to reducing methane emissions and GHG intensity and to an overall net incremental sequestration thanks to improved rangeland management and forestry.

## By introducing a specialization in the field of climate change in the master's programme in leading universities and by further strengthening the commitment of agricultural, ecology, etc., there can be a significant paradigm shift in how resources are managed and allocated. Annex 6* and Annex 7* provide more details on the proposed training and capacity building activities and principles. The capacity of the public sector to understand climate change issues and influence their policy and planning processes will also be enhanced. This capacity building can be facilitated through one-day sessions for civil servants at the Academy of Public Administration and Management, as well as peer-to-peer modern theoretical and practical knowledge.

## The project's theory of change is based on the evidence that farmers can transform their production systems to reduce GHG pathways and adopt production practices more adaptive to climate change and reduction in emissions with guidance and support. Livestock plays a vital role in Tajikistan’s agriculture sector but also contributes significantly to greenhouse gas emissions (see details in Annex 18a). The livestock sector is also highly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change, as evidenced by the trends of the last 5-10 years that show annual growth rates of 2% for cattle and sheep and 1% for goats are plausible. This trend is expected to continue over the next decade with a possible increase in productivity at the same time maintains or increases productivity. With the extensive grazing system largely relying on pastures, livestock production systems are highly vulnerable to climate change impacts.

The damages of climate change to the rural ecosystems (pasture and forests mostly) require communities to take adaptation and mitigation measures, including pasture management plans and husbandry practices that can reduce GHG emissions while improving productivity and resilience. This includes regeneration etc.. Investments in climate resilient pastures and agroforestry have the potential to replace extensive grazing systems with more sustainable production systems that are less vulnerable and limit risk from mudflows, landslides and soil loss. Specifically, CAPS+ will adopt a three-pronged approach for livestock investment: improving feed, breeds, health plus pasture management. Besides community investments in climate resilient pastures and agroforestry, CAPS+ will also invest in the genetic potential of smallholder farmers’ livestock and the adoption of climate resilient innovative technologies (e.g., improved feeding systems, better breeding practices, use of locally adapted breeds, feed demonstrations and FFS). Genetic improvement is expected to encourage commercial and smallholders to select more productive and climate-resilient breeds. This is also expected to encourage reduction in herd size thereby helping to reduce GHG emissions. Climate resilient technologies will be promoted by CAPS+ (comprising improved pasture management, better fodder production including by using climate


---

---

resilient systems, and fodder conservation, silage preparation techniques and better manure management), and promotion of livelihoods diversification will ultimately improve animal resilience and contribute reducing emissions from livestock practices (better pasture conditions, better productivity, reduction of inventories, lower methane emissions). The project will also address the gender barriers that have been identified as discriminators identified in previous experiences not only through affirmative actions (e.g., by setting quotas and targeting for women); dedicated resources (dedicated staff) and proper monitoring tools (details are provided above in Section 2.4 at Annex B) to each component intervention). In Armenia, it is known that Responding to the gender barriers identified, the gender-sensitive GSP+ has been developed and dedicated interventions aimed to: (i) women accessing technologies, knowledge, skills, business development / credit services, and market information; (ii) increasing women’s participation in decision-making (e.g., grants) through facilitate market linkages and value chain development initiatives, (iii) increase economic opportunities for women by providing them with access to community savings groups (CSGs), and (iv) increase decision making through enhancing leadership skills (leadership training).

30. The average farm size is small with limited household resources to invest in productive infrastructure. The physical and production infrastructure in rural areas which can help enhance the productivity of livestock, such as feedlots, silos, and other facilities are not well developed. The private sector is still failing to invest on a substantial scale and depth in them. However, communities have the commitment and incentive to organize and form groups and undertake collective action to solve these problems. In Tajikistan, the project will support the development of livestock infrastructure projects financed by IFAD (the Khaiton Livelihoods Support Project: KLSP; Livestock and Pasture Development Project: LPDP). The project will also provide technical assistance in terms of training on organizing and preparing collective action plans and implementing a range of sub-projects which can help them to improve the productivity of livestock. These projects will be implemented through the establishment of improved adaptive skills and diversification into off-farm enterprises. The projects had environmental objectives linked to the restoration of degraded pastures through pasture rotation plans implemented by the communities themselves, as well as the development of a community-based forest management plan to establish and strengthen village organizations as agents for rural development and governance with the capacity to manage their resources in an environmentally sustainable manner. A baseline survey confirmed that the village level institutions are likely to continue playing a key role as rural agents of change. Building on these, CASP will complement the success of community mobilization for transformative integration into the national economy.

31. Forests perform an essential function in regulating the water balance and providing protection against natural disasters. Also, forests play a key role in the lives of Tajikistan’s rural population. Firewood, fodder, and other forest products are important sources of income and food security as well as a source of income diversification. Following the collapse of the Soviet Union, large areas were deforested to meet the need for firewood. This now makes the country more vulnerable to climate hazards and exacerbates climate change impacts. The project will support the rehabilitation of forests in the Leskhoj District. Communities continue to lead over-exploitation and degradation of forest areas. Rehabilitating and protecting forests is therefore a priority area for the project. The project will also provide technical assistance on land use planning, resolving land use conflicts and managing the forests sustainably in a few pilot areas has helped to slow the rate of forest degradation and promote the rehabilitation of existing forests. Participatory management models based on community forestry have been successfully implemented by the Leskhoj District Administration (LDA) under which forest land is leased to local communities over the long term [1]. Those leasing the lands are responsible for their sustainable use, including protection from fire and other threats. Community conservation is also contributing to the rehabilitation and long-term stability of forests and mitigating the negative impacts of climate change and carbon sequestration. Communities are being

encouraging them to cut agreements designed to prevent conflicts of interest between forest and pastoral users. This is because forests and pastoralism are often in competition with each other. Women can supplement their incomes from the processing and marketing of non-wood forest products. While the country does not have a national carbon market, it has a strong potential for developing such markets. 32 At the household level, livestock is a key component of the mixed farming systems and contributes significantly towards GDP, food security, nutrition and household incomes. While the livestock herd size has grown from 70% to 116% for different species from 1991 to 2019[1], this growth has been uneven across regions and species. The livestock sector in the country also displays poor herd management, poor animal health, etc. The livestock sector in the country also displays poor reproductive performance. This has a major direct impact on carrying capacity and over-utilization of pastures and other resources. In addition, the large number of animals in some areas where natural resource base are unproductive (Annex 2). This proportion could be reduced to 20% to 25% with proper herd management practices. The livestock sector is also a major source of GHG emissions that do not contribute to intensifying production systems, such as improved breeding, fodder production and improved animal health. Improvements in feed quality, animal health and husbandry, coupled with stimuli to diversification into other income-generating activities can reduce the environmental impact of livestock-related emissions.[2] There is a significant opportunity to reduce GHG emissions, particularly methane from livestock production by improving feed quality, animal health and husbandry practices, improving feeding practices and digestibility of diets, improving yields through genetics, feeding practices and management. The livestock sector also has an important role in the country’s efforts for increasing animal productivity, improving the detrimental health impacts of climate change[3] and reducing greenhouse gas emissions. Reductions to herd growth are critical elements to adaptation and development strategies. This can be achieved by implementing a range of interventions such as improved techniques. Artificial insemination campaigns with large outreach but limited logistical burden, improving stock management practices, and increasing the use of feed supplements at community level and adoption of adaptation techniques including on livestock related issues in the Takiz context.

33 Private Sector Engagement: CASP envisages a key role for the private sector through helping to reduce their transaction costs in dealing with rural communities. IFAD has extensive experience of attracting the private sector into agricultural value chains, particularly in the case of smallholder producers so that their fragmented production is consolidated. The project intends to facilitate business partnerships or Producer Groups (PGs) between smallholder producers and processors (or other intermediaries or processors) for a range of value chains. The main rationale for this activity is the need to create incentives for more efficient livestock production. CASP[4] will facilitate market linkages through ensuring the private sector has access to information on the quality, quantity and location of smallholder producers with private sector input suppliers and wholesalers and retailers. The TQD is premised on the experience that these types of arrangements are most likely to be successful when they involve a range of stakeholders including partners such as producer groups and business operators. In addition, some of the producers also require technical assistance through a specialized service provider to help in the development of feasibility studies and business plans. The TQD is also premised on the experience that these types of arrangements with smallholder producer become sustainable through formal contracts and mutually beneficial partnerships. Development partners are encouraged to support such initiatives. IFAD has extensive experience which it is interested in building partnerships with CGI as long as they are assured of good quality produce and production at scale.

35. **Carbon Markets:** IFAD is currently also developing its strategy for entering carbon markets which can be a source of additional income and financing for smallholder farmers in rural areas through their forestry and agro-forestry activities. As an organization committed to the alignment of its operations with the Paris Agreement, IFAD will continue to support countries in their efforts to reduce emissions from land use on the part it can play in helping smallholders adapt to climate change and reducing Green House Gas (GHG) emissions and carbon sequestration. IFAD can increasingly capitalize on the new opportunities which have emerged for smallholder farmers through the development of a range of products that will increase their productivity, food security, incomes and employment for the primary target group, the smallholders and those most vulnerable to climate change. IFAD has already developed a number of successful projects in this area with developed successful pathways through, for example, making direct payments to farmers in many projects dealing with agroforestry, soil and water conservation, etc. Employment fair payment to farmers for payments for environmental services (PES) is another example of how IFAD can contribute to the development of sustainable change. IFAD can potential introduce some of these measures into CASP.

36. In order to bring about one of these changes, it is critical to enhance the technical capacities of national veterinary services and other relevant institutions in the areas of animal health, livestock production service provision to smallholder farmers through partnership between public and private institutions. Provision of breeding services will play a key role by allowing farmers to improve the productivity and value of animals, which will contribute directly to increasing their incomes and food security. The same applies for the dissemination of livestock sector. Dissemination of technical innovations on fodder production and conservation will support the development of sustainable livelihoods in rural areas. This will be achieved through the provision of training and extension services by public institutions and private service providers, as well as the establishment of confidence that their livestock capital will not be eroded by excess preventable mortality and that increased productivity will more than compensate for a smaller herd. This will be undertaken through the incorporation of veterinary medicine into the agricultural curriculum in schools and universities. The introduction of animal welfare, separation of powers between public and private veterinarians, creating an appropriate business environment for private practice, as well as the development of a national policy on the use of veterinary drugs and management of biological waste disposal processes. The experience of other countries has shown that the establishment of a Veterinary Council is essential to ensure the independence of the institutions responsible for the development of private veterinary services such as the Veterinary Associations and the Veterinary Statutory Body. Bringing the curriculum for veterinary education in line with international standards will allow the development of qualified veterinarians who can provide quality veterinary Medicine will allow the sustainable development of the veterinary system in general and the provision of quality services to the animals owned by graduates.

### B.3. Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

The Community-Based Agriculture Support Programme Plus (CASP+) builds on the successful experience of long IAF-funded investments in the country, comprising the Khatlon Livelihoods Support Project (KLS), implemented from 1998-2003; the Khatlon Livelihoods Support Project II (KLSP II) and III (KLSP I, 2011-2016, US$15.8m); LPDP II, 2015-2021, US$24.7m; and the Community-Based Agriculture Support Programme+ (CASP+, 2017-2024, US$34.0m). A description of IAF-funded portfolio to support agricultural development in Khatlon region is provided in Annexure B. The CASP+ project (a simplified one is reported below). The approach on ecosystem restoration and climate resilience adopted by the project was appreciated by the communities as well as other stakeholders involved in projects, operating in remote mountainous areas of Khatlon region, were particularly appreciated for their role in promoting sustainable land management practices and improving livelihoods. The IAF-funded Rural Development Project (RDP) 2015-2021 showed that a participatory approach to rural development (PARD) and the Integrated Rural Development Approach (IRDA) 2017-2024 also showed that an LPPD+ approach helped increasing beneficiaries’ income by 15 percent, in comparison with the baseline. The CASP+ project builds on the lessons learned from the previous livestock investments by 20 percent. CASP+ built on a number of lessons and evidence from its past investment in the country, including the improved livelihoods and role of women in their communities and institutions. It also builds on the experience gained through the implementation of the Community-based improvement for women headed households’ agricultural income (from crop, milk, and value of livestock

36. CASP is expected to reach resilience in at least 80% of the households in the 640 target villages and reach 100,000 targeted households as direct beneficiaries (representing about 650,000 people) and indirectly reach 2.7 million individuals (data provided in Annex 24 - Breakdown of Project Beneficiaries). In addition to the project’s focus on improving food security and nutrition through improved agricultural productivity, CASP also aims at reducing poverty and vulnerability by increasing women’s empowerment as per the Gender Action Plan (Annex 8) given their prominent role in agriculture especially livestock production, the high rates of outmigration of men from rural areas and the high proportion of women-headed households in the project area.

40. The project has three components which will be implemented at the national level with the public sector institutions; at the community level to build community resilience and management of common property resources; and at the market level to increase access to markets for smallholder farmers through markets. The three components are: (1) Strengthening public sector capacity for transformative climate change adaptation, including building institutional capacity for adaptation and resilience to climate change; and (3) Strengthening livelihoods for enhanced resilience through market-based approaches.

4.1 The project embraces various dimensions of climate resilience and decarbonization co-benefits. It requires the solid collaboration and partnership between different institutions, all involved in design and implementation of the project. The project will be implemented by a consortium led by GCF. The consortium is required to address climate vulnerability of rural mountainous population (ref to B2G), Annex 2.1, Annex 16). The approval of IFAD financing allows the project to enter into effectiveness in April 2023, but because of the time needed for the preparation and submission of the detailed budget and implementation timeframe in Annex 4 and Annex 5 respectively, GCF funding will be required until the end of March 2023. The consortium is required to address climate change related challenges and to achieve the relevant climate objectives. For the first two years, IFAD financing will therefore be used for the preparation of the detailed budget and implementation timeframe in Annexes 4 and 5 as well as for the preparation of the GCF funding proposal. ## Component 1: Strengthening public sector capacity for transformative climate-resilient management of natural resources

### Output 1.1: By year 7, capacities of relevant national institutions to implement natural resource management strengthened
4.1 National capacities to plan, manage and monitor the natural resource base at central and at lower administrative levels will be strengthened.
4.2 The capacity of the State Forest Agency (SFA) will be strengthened. A forestry curriculum recently developed for Tajikistan with the assistance of GCF will be introduced in the SFA training center. The SFA will also be responsible for pasture management (Pasture Management Trust, PMT), livestock (National Veterinary Agency, NVA), and forest resources.
4.3 National capacities to plan, manage and monitor natural resource base at central and lower administrative levels will be strengthened through capacity building of relevant national institutions in the generation of knowledge and the practical application of innovations in the project area, the project will enable the development of a training curriculum for climate change specialists in Tajikistan based on existing curricula for training of climate change specialists in key decision making positions in the government. The curriculum will be developed with the assistance of GCF and will be used to train decision makers on ecosystem services management.

## Activity 1.1.2
Implement landscape restoration and integrated Natural Resource Management. This activity is coordinated by CEP and MOA.

### Sub-Activity 1.1.2.1
Establish remote sensing capacity for natural resources as a basis for climate adaptive development and enable exchange of information between the different actors involved in the project. The output will be the establishment of a national monitoring system that will support the FAO readiness activities establishing national MRV capacity, will address the capacity building needs of the CEP and MOA, and will provide the necessary data to conduct an ex-ante evaluation of the mitigation action of the project itself. This activity is executed by CEP.

### Sub-Activity 1.1.2.2
Build capacities for public health and production services to smallholder farmers through the provision of animal health and production services, including training on climate change adaptation strategies. The output will be improved livestock management practices and increased capacities of public agencies in charge of animal health and breeding services to optimize production systems and reduce vulnerability.

### Sub-Activity 1.1.2.3
Build capacities of research and academia institutions on climate resilient ecosystem management. Streamline Climate Change and Ecosystem management activities within the National Research Council, the National Agricultural and Research Institutions and academe (under CEP and FAO execution).

## Output 1.2: By year 7, enabling environment for climate adaptive, inclusive and integrated management of natural resources will be in place.

### Sub-Activity 1.2.1
CASP has chosen strategic areas for policy engagement from the climate perspective and will focus animal health services on these areas. The project will also work with the existing system of incentives and regulation and lessons learned from the experience of joint forest management to develop a new enabling environment for addressing climate risks and is already engaged with the preparatory work for National Adaptation Plans (NAPs). To ensure that the project interacts with the main stakeholders, the project will organize regular workshops to facilitate interaction and enhance dialogue. The project will also provide natural resource management practices in close coordination with existing thematic platforms such as the Pasture Land Use Dialogue (PLUD) and the Forest Land Use Dialogue (FLUD), which are already established and prepared to deal with growing climate risks. The dialogue will also include the integration of the Pasture Land Use Dialogue (PLUD) and the Forest Land Use Dialogue (FLUD). The project will ensure that entry points to address this issue from a policy and regulatory point of view would be included in the law, providing an enabling environment for the implementation of climate change adaptation strategies. If sufficient capacities are observed and that stock accumulation is discouraged. If these types of measures were framed in the context of a national strategy, it could have a significant impact on the livestock sector.

Given the centrality of an integrated ecosystem approach for a transformational and climate resilient development pathway, the project will also support the implementation of the Ecosystem Services, the project will also provide assistance in the use of carbon assessment decision tools such as the EX-Ante Carbon Balance Tool (Ex-ACET), the Global LiveStock Environmental Assessment Model (GLEAM) and the Forest Landscape Analysis (FLA). The project will also contribute to the development leading to enhanced capacities for national accounting, and will intend (through FAO as co-executing entity) work with the Government of Ethiopia to develop a National Ecosystem Services Strategy. In this output, GCF proceeds will also finance stock taking of policy development and mainstreaming of gender considerations in the livestock sector. The project will also support the implementation of existing capacities towards implementing the Article 6 of the Paris Agreement, within the approved Green Economy strategy for Ethiopia. This will include capacity building on carbon accounting and reporting to support country access to deploy climate finance.

### Sub-Activity 1.2.3
The project will focus specifically on youth, as an empowerment measure and to ensure that the policy and regulatory framework changes embed youth and gender perspectives in its execution. The project will also provide training for young youth on livestock management at different levels formed and receiving public and private self-sustained support.

The output will comprise the following activities:

## Activity 1.2.1: Promote an inclusive and integrated policy dialogue.
Enhance the mainstreaming of climate adaptive management practices of natural resources in all national institutions (including government, civil society, and private sector) to ensure the effectiveness of policies on the ground. The activity is implemented by CEP and FAO.

## Activity 1.2.2: Technical assistance for review of livestock-related regulatory frameworks.
Review existing livestock-related regulatory frameworks and develop a new framework to ensure compliance with international guidelines and streamline climate mainstreaming in the sector. This will be coordinated by CEP, MDA, and FAO.

## Activity 1.2.3: Support government's capacity to coordinate and monitor Green Investments.
Support the development of an enabling environment for investment in climate change adaptation and mitigation through a regulatory framework related to the Green Economy, including assessing potential for public-private partnerships (PPPs) and supporting the implementation of the PPP action list on that regard. The activity is coordinated by CEP, with MDA and FAO support.

## Component 2: Investments in community capacity for adaptation and resilience to climate change

48. This component will develop and implement 400 Climate Sensitive Community Action Plans (CSCAPs) in the selected districts. The project will encourage a collaborative and participatory diagnostic approach, involving all stakeholders at district level. The Diagnostic Climate Risk and Vulnerability Diagnostic (DCRD), defining the main challenges related to climate change and the main avenues for intervention, will be developed with the participation of all stakeholders. This will include identifying improved pasture management, afforestation and forest rehabilitation, climate resilient infrastructures, and livelihood diversification.

49. Social inclusion dimension: Women will represent 50 percent of the overall beneficiaries and youth at least 30 percent in each district. The project will ensure that women and youth are involved as active participants during the community planning process for CSCAPs. As such, they will be targeted specifically for 2,400 women and 1,800 youth to be capacitated. The objective is to project women members of community organizations to be leaders and change agents in their organizations. They will be trained on climate change adaptation and mitigation, gender equality, conflict resolution, good governance, accountability, negotiation and conflict management, advocacy, lobbying and effective communication (capacity building). This will be done through the establishment of Women’s Groups. Climate change management contracts will be provided to youth, seying them to remain in rural areas and develop livelihoods that are climate resilient.

Output 2.1: By year 3, 400 Climate-sensitive Community Action Plans (CSCAP) based on 21 district level climate diagnostics are developed

A capacity building approach will be used to support the development of a participatory diagnostic analysis of climate risk and vulnerability. A map-based profile of each district will be created to indicate the geographic areas where the effects of climate change pose the greatest threat to the safety and livelihood of communities, as well as the most effective adaptation strategies for these areas. The profiles will include control, conservation and topography in disaster reduction and climate change resilience, each district will have a profile that will guide the development of the Climate Change Action Plan (CCAP) approach.

The District level Climate Resilience Diagnostic (DCRD) will be regarded as a discrete diagnostic tool to support the development of the CCAPs. The DCRD will be developed in collaboration with JAMCAT in the selected 21 districts, benefiting the entire population of each district, in the frame of CLIMATE CHANGE ACTION PLANS (CCAP). The DCRD will provide an overview of climate change and outline the climate-related changes that can be expected and the best strategies to cope with them including adaptation measures. The DCRD will also identify the institutional capacity gaps at national and subnational institutions will be assessed, thus defining actions to strengthen local capacities to plan and monitor climate sensitive community action plans (CSCAP), the impact on the economy and on the issues emerging from the implementation of the CCAPs in each district. This will guide the response and adaptation strategies in designing CSCAPs screened against social and environmental safeguards,” and in establishing Common Interest Groups within the selected villages. These will be self-selecting groups of up to 10 households per

### Output 2. By year 7, 400 Climate-sensitive Community Action Plans (CiCAP) implemented in 21 districts with benefits for 100,000 rural households.
52. This will be achieved by ensuring that the VAPs are a mix of investment activities, and that they properly capture the need for specific interventions on climate change adaptation, mitigation and disaster risk reduction (DRR). The VAPs will also include an assessment of the vulnerability of communities to climate change based on climate diagnostic analysis and village level priorities. The CiCAPs will include ecosystem resilience and adaptation strategies.

#### Pasture Management investments identified through the forum of JUH (where VOIs do not exist) or PLUG (where VOIs exist). The pasture plans will include pasture rotation and protection, pasture restoration, or pastoral diversification. In some cases, the plan may include the establishment of livestock shelters and night fences and shelters for animals (e.g., cattle crates for treatment). In some cases, a sub-class of conservation investments is included in the VAPs, such as the creation of wildlife corridors or areas for example where plans are made for transhumance routes. Overall, pasture investments will represent around 20% of total investment.

#### Forestry investment, operated in collaboration with leskouz (Forest enterprises depending on the State government). The forestry investments will be part of a broader ecosystem plan which includes the restoration of ecosystems and the protection of areas vulnerable to climate hazards (disaster risk reduction), at the same time as providing livelihoods for communities. The forest enterprise model is based on where investment will be part of a broad ecosystem plan, which will be monitored by the communities and Leskouz. Specifically, investments will include:
- Joint Forest Management Plans (JFMP) - where a contract is established between the community and the forest enterprise to define the area of land where the yield from the plot is split between each party to the contract.
  * Direct Leskouz Forestry: Where the forest enterprise manages the entire process, including tree planting, maintenance, and harvesting. The investment will be limited to Sh. Shohdin district only.
- Forest investments in buffer zones (where the community has no land rights) - where the forest enterprise is established by the community itself. Investment includes concessionary plot support for site works, seedlings, and other inputs. The investment will be limited to Sh. Shohdin district only.

#### Climate Resilient infrastructure investments, related to addressing water stress and the need to adapt agricultural practices in the face of climate change.
- Water infrastructure investments: This includes a range of activities such as rainwater harvesting, including a plan to finance it. Water investments aim to support diversification activities (backyard gardening, livestock, etc.), improve access to drinking water, increase irrigation water availability, and ensure meeting basic livelihood requirements in isolated areas. Soil and water conservation structures to prevent soil erosion, mudslides and floods will be considered where

approaches. This could include the plantation of bushes and trees, small hydrological works that slow down torrential water flows, where there is potential for aquifer recharge and enhanced infiltration, reduce impacts on downstream communities, improve soil fertility and enhance erosion reduction, water points and irrigation schemes, etc. The project will ensure that these measures are implemented to reduce risks caused by more frequent torrential precipitation events.

- **Component 2: Climate-smart Agriculture (CSaC) Investments:** This component includes the procurement of agricultural equipment such as tractors, bailers, etc., that will contribute to support transformation of production systems and practices. The CSaC investments will be implemented in a participatory manner with the community institutions, and will be rented to individual community members, generating financial income for the project. The equipment will be used by farmers to improve their livelihoods. Examples of types of equipment investment will be on fodder harvesting and conservation material, in order to help farmers to cope with climate change impacts. Other examples include: ploughs, seed drills, fertilizers, etc. A list of category of mechanization equipment that could be considered are those that can be used both for hay/straw production (such as mowers, rakes), tractors, image equipment (e.g., ploughs, cultivators, trailers, planters, fertilizer spreader (unless they are not in private practices). Equipment that are only for non-fodder crops (e.g. combine harvester) will be need to be justified in the CSaC according to specific climate change impacts.

### 3.5 CsCa Monitoring

The monitoring and evaluation of the CsCaPs will be an intrinsic part of the approach, with a focus on the use of mobile technology. The project will provide each community with Source+ smartphone tools for project planning, monitoring and control to minimize supervision costs and improve efficiency. The Project Manager will be responsible for coordinating all technical investments; the Engineer recruited by the MOA and technical works Supervisor will be responsible for performing the necessary activities in line with the approved plan. A mobile application will be developed between the diagnostic maps and citizen reporting to provide a means of tracking the achievement of objectives, as well as the identification of new opportunities. The project will also use digital tools to georeference this. This will allow communities to better monitor all CsCaP investments using digital tools.

Innovative methods will be employed to engage and incentivize stakeholders, including youth, to use services provided by the project.

Implementation of the activity will be carried out through a participatory approach in 30 villages, comprising the multiple dimensions of improved pasture management (including conservation and rehabilitation), climate-resilient infrastructure development (in the proximity of Lesokho), climate resilient infrastructures, agricultural productivity improvement (implemented by MOA, with CEP response for forest investments).

### Component 3: Strengthening livelihoods for enhanced resilience through market based

50. The project will support the development of a private sector (both public and private sector actors), building smallholders' capacities in climate resilient production practices and creating opportunities for market access.

#### Funds and flow
The GCF co-financing was approved at the December 2012 IFAD Executive Board and the Financing Agreement signed on 09 January 2013, and then IFAD entered into a Financing Agreement with the MoF. The total amount of the project is USD $750 million (of which USD $750m and USD $24.38m are for a project duration of 7 years). As per the above said agreement executed between IFAD and MoF, the direct fund flow from IFAD to MoF will be arranged subject to the formal confirmation by the MoF.

#### GCF Proceeds
For the GCF proceeds, IFAD will enter into a Financing Agreement, which will serve as the Subsidiary Agreement with the Republic of Tajikistan, represented by the Ministry of Finance and the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry (MAF) for the purposes of the agreement. The financing arrangement is USD $750 million (one for the loan portion and one for the grant portion) and the CEP (one for the grant portion) for a project duration of 7 years. As per the above said agreement executed between IFAD and MoF, the direct fund flow from IFAD to MoF will be arranged subject to the formal confirmation by the MoF. The fund flow arrangements to three EEs will be described in the Financing Agreement.

The Ministry of Economic Development and Trade (MEDT) is responsible for leading the national government’s climate change policy and strategy development. It is also responsible for developing a national action plan on climate change, which will be submitted to the UNFCCC in coordination with key ministries and agencies as well as the preparation of GEF Inventions. However, it should be noted that the Ministry of Economic Development and Trade is not the lead agency for climate change for the general public in Tajikistan. The Climate Change Center (CCC) within Hydromet is responsible for mainstreaming climate change into all relevant sectors and providing technical support to the government on climate change adaptation and mitigation. The National Biodiversity and Biosafety Centre is responsible for biological diversity issues, which are key areas of concern when it comes to project thematic impact.

The terms and conditions for the highly concessional loan provided to Tajikistan as co-financing of the GCF Loan are as follows: i) The loan amount is 48% of fixed service charge as determined, the service charge rate applicable at the date of approval of the Loan by the Fund's Executive Board. ii) The terms and conditions for the loan are as follows: a) Repayment will be made in equal annual installments over a grace period of ten (10) years. b) The principal of the Loan will be repaid at four and half per cent (4.5%) interest rate, with no interest charged on the first installment due until the end of the grace period. c) The loan principal for years thirty-first (31) to forty (40). Thus, to clarify, IFAD does not charge any interest to Tajikistan on the loan principal during this period. d) The percentages are the proportions of the total repayments of the loan principle over the years from 11 to 40.
