**(including livestock and leguminous crops, technical crops, vegetables and horticulture and viticulture) and livestock subsectors. Lack of appropriate financing is reducing the potential of the country for effective allocation of resources to achieve these results and to achieve the fair and ambitious development objectives set out in the NDCs. In addition, the financial system is inadequate to support the private sector and market mechanisms to attract carbon finance are missing.**

80. The CASP+ project requires GCF financing as the Government does not have the resources to make investments in agriculture and food security. Tajikistan is the poorest country in Central Asia and considered as low-income country (World Bank, 2021). The COVID-19 pandemic has had a significant impact on the economy of Tajikistan. GDP growth was negative for three consecutive months of 2020 compared to 7.2 percent a year earlier. Restrictions on labour mobility and economic activity have led to a decline in employment, income and remittances, which are the main source of foreign investments. The external current account has faced a deterioration due to weak exports and remittances and an increase in imports. The government has been forced to implement austerity measures (see also Sustainability Analysis). (1) A growing share of the population reported reducing their food intake and overall consumption to cope with the changed circumstances. To ameliorate the economic fallout, the authorities decided to provide subsidies for basic goods and services. However, this measure is not sustainable as financing needs were forecasted to increase from 3.8% of GDP in 2019 to 7.7% by the end of 2020. (2) The government has also been forced to borrow from international financial institutions. On one hand, which on the other hand is reluctant to take additional loans and on the other hand, needs additional fiscal space for its development priorities. This situation requires a comprehensive approach that provides much needed investments in areas which are being increasingly threatened by a host of challenges including climate change, desertification, land degradation, soil erosion, water scarcity and biodiversity loss. It is essential to ensure the transition towards a wide use of sustainable, climate-resilient and low-emission agricultural practices.

81. During 2014-2017, public debt nearly doubled as a share of GDP from 27.7% to 53%, while total external debt (public and private) jumped from 45.6% of GDP to 72.0%—more than twice the average for lower-middle income countries. Bank recapitalization in 2016 and the issuance of a $500 million sovereign export credit insurance facility by the World Bank Group have helped reduce the debt-to-GDP ratio, but debt sustainability analysis (2021 Article IV report) by the International Monetary Fund (IMF) highlights that Tajikistan’s public debt is projected to reach 84% of GDP in 2030 and 95% of GDP in 2040, with limited fiscal space. The debt-to-GDP ratio is projected to decline to 40.0 percent by 2026. However, since Tajikistan’s public debt is expected to remain high for the next decade, it has breached its threshold at Eurobond payment due dates (2025-27), contributing to a higher external risk rating.

82. Climate change is one of the main challenges to Tajikistan's agricultural development and food security. The country faces significant risks from climate change in terms of water availability and achieving country’s strategic objectives as defined in the National Development Strategy for 2016-2030, which includes ensuring food security and access to quality nutrition by 2030. Many models, including IFAD's Climate Change Vulnerability Assessment (CCVA) for Tajikistan, indicate that climate change will affect both irrigated and rain-fed systems, and under the pessimistic risk setting shows that all crop yields are projected to decline significantly in the future. The CCVA also indicates that climate change will reduce grasslands, in line with what is already seen in certain areas of the country under high temperature and drought conditions. (3) In addition, the impact of climate change on livestock production systems show that yields of major crops will decline significantly and projects an overall negative effect of climate on crop yields by 2050. The CCVA also indicates that climate change is likely to exacerbate existing main challenges for food security, leading to increasing risk of hunger, malnourishment, especially among children and other vulnerable groups, and insufficient per capita calorie intake.

83. Tajikistan has a large number of forest resources in the country's territory, particularly the water sector, pastures and forests (Annex 2). Chapter 1: Most of the forest and pasture resources are owned by the State. Dushan farms, agriculture enterprises and state farms and Individuals can obtain use rights (privileges) to manage these resources. The management of these resources is carried out in accordance with the laws of Tajikistan. The use of these public goods provide little incentives for the private sector to invest in them. Community members who


---
**Page 36**
---

use these resources tend to overuse them because of the current production practices and limited other options. This leads to over-grazing and degradation of the pasture resources of the country on which many communities depend. Since the government also allocates a small amount of its budget for agriculture, there are sometimes no competition with each other. The public sector tries to regulate use rights but does not have strong enforcement mechanisms. Markets do not generate an efficient allocation of the forest or pastoral land resources because they are often underutilized and overused. This leads to inefficient allocation of resources. Thus, the ownership, use and management of these resources represents a fundamental challenge for the country. The government has been trying to address this issue by providing individual household level and private sector financing is unlikely to be available for investments in the natural resource base for sustainable use and protection against climate and other risks. Government support is therefore needed to provide financial assistance to communities, households, and groups adapting to changing climate conditions and to guide further climate adaptation investments in rural areas.

The project envisages the use of both grants and senior loans. The total loan for the project from GCF is USD 9 million, and the grant amount is USD 30 million. The loan amount therefore represents 25% of the total cost of the project. This loan will be used to address the climate change sustainability stress of the country (RISF, 2012) in agreement with the Government, specifically the Ministry of Environment and Forestry (MoEF). The grant component is USD 30 million. A portion of the external financing from IFAD and GCF are used as follows: a) grant-financed portion of the project to be implemented by the MoEF at national level; b) loan-financed portion of the project for technical development, helping communities to defray the costs of planning and implementing climate sensitive community projects at the household, group, and community level. The project will also provide support to part of the investments in implementing the community plans in component 2 (both IFAD GCF loans as well as for investments in livestock strengthening in component 3 (only IFAD loan).

GCF funds have been able to leverage third party financing from both internal and external sources in a manner that is consistent with the project design. In agreement with the Government (from MoA, CEP and SFA) and FAO (Annex 13), IFAD will be providing the larger co-financing loan amount of 37.85M USD. The loan will be provided to the MoEF at national level for technical development purposes as a commitment to the project's objective and an indicator of national ownership in achieving the desired performance outcomes. This loan will be used by the MoEF to finance the community plans at household, group and community level. It is expected that this loan will be co-financed with GCF funds. As part of the demonstration of ownership, commitment and their expectation of the benefits in helping them enhance their capacity to manage natural resources sustainably, communities will contribute 10% of the total cost as complementary technical assistance. IFAD co-financing will be managed by the MoA, CEP and the TNC. The loan will be provided under the terms of the International Financial Institutions (IFIs) and GCF.

Overall GCF co-financing is in 51.1% with IFAD @ 93.0% of total co-financing coming from total country allocations under IFAD/ITF and IFAD 12 repayment processes). The design has estimated total project resources required to be USD 84 million, which includes the GCF loan component (USD 9M) and the performance-based allocation system (PBSA) with allocation at county level approved by IFAD Board every three years. This will ensure that the PBSA is in line with the national development priorities.

The high level of concessional financing requested to GCF including a loan of USD 9 million and a grant of USD 30 million) is motivated by the difficulty of Tajikistan in maintaining its debt sustainability, especially given the country's low export earnings. The combination of concessional loans and indebtedness is a key factor in determining the level of concession being requested for the country. Under the terms of the GCF agreement with IFAD, the overall financing to be provided by GCF to the country with grant financing (IFAD has also moved to full concessionality since its IFAD 12 financing) will be USD 30 million. The overall loan component is USD 9 million (including account GCF and FAO resources). This represents 25% of GCF's 30M IFAD loan component.

The financial structure in this project has been very carefully balanced in order to allow the project to be affordable to the Government, while also ensuring that it can be implemented at a scale that is commensurate with country levels (annex 2.1, annex 16), and to allow it to introduce innovative investment for climate resilience (at

### B

community-based component 2), and through market driven approaches in component 3) that incur additional costs while ensuring the long-term financial sustainability of the investment. This is the minimum level of commitment required to ensure that the project exit strategy and the repayment period is expected to be much longer than a business-as-usual project. The Government does not have the resources to invest in institutional strengthening, capacity building, and training for communities and households to deal with climate risks and impacts, nor has it the resources for financing climate resilient action plans, infrastructure, assets, equipment and training to help them deal with climate risks and impacts. This is a critical gap that needs to be addressed through this project. The Government and households impacted by climate change in the selected project districts most vulnerable to climate risks (Afghanistan) need additional financial resources for adaptation measures to reduce their vulnerability towards sustainable development. Financing will be tailored to cover the additional costs of the investment necessary to make the project financially viable.

### B

The investments being allocated are incremental costs and will not displace any investments that would otherwise have occurred. At the level of the households and common interest groups, the grants will deliver part of the household investment and will cover the risk premium required to make the investments financially viable. The GCF funding will be used for two purposes: (i) to provide grants to vulnerable communities threatened by climate risks and households to diversify their livelihoods. Given the high costs associated with these investments, the project will not be able to finance all of them. The matching grants for investments at the household level in building the individual household assets are a necessary condition for making such investments financially viable. (ii) To provide grants to support the investments. Moreover, the households do not have access to commercial loan financing because of the high risk associated with these investments and the lack of collateral from either the sector or through MFIs, and the general lack of adequate financial services for agriculture and in rural areas.

### B

88. One of Tajikistan's biggest challenges in diversifying its economy has been its inability to increase private investment levels in the country. Domestic credit to the private Sector was reported at 10.6% in 2015, which is a very low level compared with other countries in the region. The lack of access to finance such as this financing and the concessional loan required are critical to make the investment. GCF funding will be used for two purposes: (i) To provide grants to vulnerable communities threatened by climate risks and households to diversify their livelihoods. Given the high costs associated with these investments, the project will not be able to finance all of them. The matching grants for investments at the household level in building the individual household assets are a necessary condition for making such investments financially viable. (ii) To provide grants to support the investments. Moreover, the households do not have access to commercial loan financing because of the high risk associated with these investments and the lack of collateral from either the sector or through MFIs, and the general lack of adequate financial services for agriculture and in rural areas.

### B

89. There are several approaches for ensuring the project exit strategy at various levels. These include (i) building capacity to manage climate risks and impacts; (ii) strengthening the institutional framework by building of institutions and individuals; (iii) strengthening of policy and regulatory frameworks in a manner that adds to sustainability of natural resource through improved management and governance, (iv)


---
**Page 38**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V 6.0 | PAGE 34

## Introduction of system tools and technologies to strengthen institutional capacity; (v) use of participatory approaches to ensure beneficiary ownership; and (vi) a clear plan for operation and maintenance of all infrastructure, including equity in the management of the project, etc.

### At the national level:

- The investments are expected to continue beyond the project period because of the range of public sector institutions dealing with natural resources such as the Ministry of Economic Development, Planning and Local Government (MEDPLG), the National Environment Management Authority (NEMA), the Kenya Forest Service (KFS), the Kenya Wildlife Service (KWS), the Kenya Forest Research Institute, the Land and Geodesy Department, the Pasture Meliorative Trust (PMT), several national environmental institutions, and the Ministry of Water and Irrigation. The investments will be directed at introducing modern and efficient techniques and technologies such as remote sensing and carbon accounting tools such as the use of Ex-ACT and GLEAM that will enhance the capacity of the institutions for climate change adaptation.
- Care will be taken to invest in only those activities which either have no implications for increase in their budget allocations or are already included in their current budget allocations. The specific staff members involved in these investments will be trained on the use of the new tools and technologies, and since they are currently posted for long, they can be expected to contribute within the institution during their service.

### At the expert level:

- The expertise for understanding climate risks will be strengthened by supporting research and building capacity at the University of Nairobi (UON) and other universities in Kenya. The UON is already a leader on the subject.
- The institutions involved like the Tana Agrarian University, Tana Agricultural University, and others will continue to benefit from the project through training and upgrading courses as part of their regular curriculum.

### At the community level:

- The strengthening of community institutions particularly the use of the broad base Village Organizations and within that the formation of Pasture User Groups (PUG) that consist of pastoralists who are directly involved in the management of the land will be a key strategy for sustaining the investments.
- Many agencies have been investing in these institutions as well as the Ministry of Water and Irrigation, the Kenya Forest Service, and the National Environment Management Authority. These institutions will become more sustainable by providing them powers for generating revenue by collecting user fees from their members. The project will also support the formation of PUGs at the sub-county level across the region like Kyambura.
- All investments that are included in the climate sensitive action plans (CLAPs) will be defined through participation of the local governments, local stakeholders and community members owning or managing the resources.


---
**Page 39**
---

or continuous operating and maintaining them after hand-over. The experience with previous IFAD projects has shown that communities are generally able to operate and maintain infrastructure investments well beyond project completion. The physical commitment on the ground at community level in terms of forestry, and other land productivity activities generated by the project will help them adapt to climate risks and challenges that they currently face and add to the sustainability of their livelihoods.

94. For investments at the household level, the project places strong emphasis on making livelihoods more resilient through increased access to markets for products produced by households. The markets are those which are already being used by the households. The environmental sustainability of the investments will be ensured since only green investment opportunities will be considered and only projects with a clear private sector orientation and will work backwards from the markets in trying to identify opportunities in which the project can make an impact. This will include groups of smallholder producers (e.g., dairy processors, feed lots of operators, poultry processors) and groups of farmers for increased access to markets will strengthen their linkages. The underlying mutual interest of the smallholder producers and the private sector will ensure the sustainability of the arrangements beyond the project period.

### (b) Other financing arrangements and contributions (in other words, approximately 0.5 page)

IFAD is the Accredited Entity and the main co-financing partner. The Government will be providing additional co-financing for the project activities through the Ministry of Agriculture (for agricultural equipment), financed by own resources. This will include financing from the Ministry of Agriculture (for agricultural equipment) and the Ministry of Finance (for other investments). In addition, FAID will provide additional co-financing (a dedicated Technical Cooperation Project, TCP), to be completed with technical assistance according to its comparative advantages.

The project is expected to generate a total of USD 4.5 million in co-financing contributions from beneficiaries, for an estimated amount of USD 4.5 million. The contribution is expected to come as follows: (Output 1.2: Smallholders producers and processors: 30% for agricultural investments; 10% for agricultural equipment investment); (Output 3.2: Smallholders producers and processors: 70% for agricultural investments, 15% for agricultural equipment investment); (Output 3.3: Members of the Common Agricultural Policy Committee: 80% for agricultural investments, 20% for matching grants financing under Window 1; and 20% for the matching grants financing under Window 2).

### D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This section refers to the performance of the project/proposal against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF’s Total Investment Framework.

#### D.1 Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

97. The project is expected to have an impact on both mitigation and adaptation and lead to reduced emissions and increased resilience. With respect to mitigation, the project is expected to help in a shift towards more sustainable land use practices, including improved agricultural management, better soil use, reforestation, reduced deforestation, and through sustainable management, conservation and enhancement of forest resources. The project will also contribute to the reduction of carbon emissions from removed/acquired land according to the new Integrated Results Management Framework. Overall results are expected to be measured in terms of avoided emissions (CO2-eq) over a 100-year horizon with a total carbon balance of -4,854,822 CO2-eq over 27 years* or (-233,882 CO2-eq per year [Annex 23] which presents also a disaggregation of greenhouse net incremental sequestration and emission reduction, showing that the project will have an overall positive balance in terms of carbon emissions. The project is estimated to cost USD $105 million (or USD $79.58 million considering GCF financing). The net incremental contribution to carbon sequestration has been calculated based on the avoided deforestation and forest degradation, improved land use practices, better land use, land use change and forestry and livestock production systems. The outcome associated with this project is the reduction of emissions from land use change and forestry and livestock production systems contributing to emissions reductions.* The outcome will be measured through tracking the hectares of land under conservation and/or reforestation and the hectares of land under improved land use practices. Specifically, the project will improve the status of 3,650 ha of forests, 6,190 ha under perennial crops and 180 ha under livestock production systems. The project will also contribute to the improvement of the ground truthing system developed by the project. Training on the use of carbon accounting tools will be provided to the institutions involved in NRM, to encourage the tracking of the CO2-eq reduction.

#### D.2 Potential full potential (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

This project is expected to lead to a paradigm shift in important policy areas, implementation mechanisms and in household-based production decisions. CASHP will be the first major initiative of its kind in Tajikistan, with the aim of providing a model for the private sector. Farm modeling has shown that improving pasture quality and livestock efficiency can increase milk yields by 10% to 25%, which is equivalent to an additional 40 kg per cow per year. IFAD investments show that livestock farmers in the treatment group are more likely to use pastures to feed their livestock especially PPDU managed pastures compared to the control group and are more likely to follow recommended practices for pasture management, such as grazing rotation and fertilization. This is likely to reduce methane emissions and the greenhouse gas intensity of animal products. Reducing the number of animals per cow by 20% would also lead to a reduction in livestock-related GHG emissions. If productivity increases through nutritional and breeding strategies, the number of livestock can be reduced without compromising milk production. For example, if the average age at first calving for raising beef cattle, slaughter weights can be reached at a younger age, with reduced lifetime emissions per cow.

100. Experience from previous IFAD projects has demonstrated the transformational nature of some of the proposed activities in Tajikistan such as the LUPR-III. Focus on improved animal health and productivity led to an increase in milk production by 25% (140%) for cows that were treated compared with the size of their herds by 29%. The impact assessment shows an average income increase associated with rise in the productivity of cattle among LUPR-II beneficiaries. This was attributed to breed and milk type selection, improved nutrition, better health care and increased use of artificial insemination compared to the control group, which is equivalent to around 44 kg per animal. Similarly, cattle kept by households participating in the project had a higher average weight at slaughter than those in the control group. The results of the impact assessment also suggest that the project succeeded in enhancing livestock productivity and improving the quality of milk production through better use of feed resources, housing, and water points. These were all activities promoted by both phases of the project. The current project will build on these experiences to ensure that the proposed activities have a significant impact on reduction in GHG emissions. The results of the Glean-i analysis undertaken for CASHP shows the potential for reducing GHG emissions from livestock under the With Project (WP) scenario compared to the Without Project (WJP) scenario and the baseline. This result is based on the assumption that the hard sire seed in evidence in LUPR-II is not realized.

101. CASHP is expected to assist in diversifying rural livelihoods which is a slow process given that Tajikistan has been isolated from a market economy after the dissolution of the Soviet Union in 1991 has been one of the slowest among the Commonwealth of Independent States countries. Private investment in agriculture and livestock production is essential for increasing agricultural productivity, growth and job creation. If family incomes today are strongly dependent on a single source, with 50-70% deriving their income from livestock, then diversification will be difficult to achieve without a significant diversification of income sources, which requires strengthening farm occupations, wage employment, and entrepreneurial activities. CASHP is expected to assist in rural livelihood through its provision of matching grants, establishment of CIGs and building links with the private sector along key value chains.

102. The recent analysis of the agriculture sector for the update of the NDCs noted that adaptation and mitigation were not currently explicitly integrated into agriculture sector planning in Tajikistan. The project is expected to address this gap by integrating climate change considerations into agricultural policies, programmes at the national and local level to target resources and production systems where they are most needed. This will be achieved through a multi-stakeholder approach with strong government orientation in close collaboration with MEDT, CEP, MoA, SFA and other national level institutions. At the district level, this will involve working closely with farmers’ associations and local communities to promote an approach that focuses on catchments and protecting downstream areas through a range of green infrastructure measures such as agroforestry systems, improved water management practices, sustainable pasture governance, increasing bio-diversity and plantation of forest trees and orchards and more efficient use of irrigation. The project will also support the development of climate-resilient agricultural land-use shift have been identified below. Each of these is expected to make an overall contribution to the climate- resilient development pathways which are consistent with the national climate change adaptation strategies and plans.

103. Some of the key measures for improving the country capacity for transformative in adoption include the following: (i) introducing climate risk awareness and analysis as a part of the curriculum of academic and post-academic institutions; (ii) developing tools and methodologies to support climate risk assessment in allocative, design and planning decisions in key sectors by experts and in the public sector through workshops and training sessions; (iii) strengthening the capacity for climate-resilient investment planning through technical capacity building of MEDT and other national level institutions; (iv) engaging with private sector actors such as farmers’ associations, agricultural cooperatives and agribusinesses through policy and knowledge sharing platforms; (v) use of improved technology for decision-making such as surveillance and remote sensing of pastures and livestock; (vi) and crop production systems that are more climate-resilient encouraging the adoption of low productivity animal breeds on smallholder livestock and to help farmers embark on a low emissions trajectory; (vii) strengthen the provision of private veterinary services, including improved disease control measures for reducing livestock morbidity and mortality; (viii) establish and strengthen pasture user unions for improved pasture governance and protection against overgrazing and degradation; (ix) improve land tenure security to reduce deforestation and closures, etc.; (x) applying participatory management models of joint forest management that entail lessening the dependence on government resources and increasing the capacity of communities to manage their forests with respect to climate sensitive actions to build resilience and ensuring upstream; (xi) linking private sector with public sector instruments suitable to handle the required context-sensitive approaches, technologies and innovations.

107\. Environmental co-benefits. The main environmental benefits that the project is expected to be from combined investments at national level are improved land use management practices on degraded lands and forestry and reduced CO2 emissions due to improved animal husbandry practices that help to improve animal diets and digestibility and promote better feed conversion ratios. Improved livestock health will also reduce the need for veterinary services, which in turn will reduce GHG emissions from enteric fermentation. The project is expected to contribute towards increased biodiversity of trees and shrubs as well as increase in biodiversity in pastures and forest areas. The degradation of pastures is threatening biodiversity of the region. The project will work with Village Organizations and Pasture User Unions which are expected to prepare pasture management plans, introduce improved grazing practices and improve livestock health. A temporary closure of pastures is expected to lead to their regeneration and increase plant diversity and foster nectar for pollinators and beneficial insects. The National Biodiversity and Biosecurity Centre (NBBC) in collaboration with the project will conduct biodiversity assessments at the pilot sites, which are expected to protect threatened species in the pastures and forests. Joint Forest management also performs an essential function of protecting forest resources from encroachment and illegal logging as well as preventing soil erosion. At the farm level, the project will introduce crop and livestock production practices through farmer field schools that will help improve soil fertility, reduce chemical inputs, increase water use efficiency, improve pollination potential, and landscape restoration, etc.

### Economic Co-benefits

While the project will generate direct economic benefits from many of the activities that it will finance in order to enhance the resilience of communities and households to climate risks, there are also a number of co-benefits that can be achieved through the implementation of Climate-sensitive Community Action Plans (CCAPs) and the support that will be provided to communities to implement these plans. These include the creation of new livelihood opportunities using technology, assets and the links with the private sector. There are several ways in which the project is expected to contribute to this:

1. **Economic Co-benefits from CCAP Implementation**: The economic co-benefits envisaged through the approach envisaged in component 3 of strengthening the link between rural producers form the project area (Urozgan, Khost and Wardak) with the private sector will be achieved by the implementation of the CCAPs instruments. In the project will also contribute to increase financial inclusion as well as the enabling environment for microfinance institutions and the provision of the services that will use to offer these services. The creation of productive assets and the maintenance equipment provided will generate jobs for the young people. It is estimated that the labour productivity in the project area will be increased by 20% due to the implementation of CCAPs, and that the forest plantations will lead to an additional 2,280 jobs (Annex 2). The management and processing of the products from the forest plantations will also generate employment opportunities such as fodder, tree seeds, medicinal plants, fruit and nuts which can be sold locally and may also open up new markets for these products. This will also lead to increased income generation through the provision of inputs and technologies for building climate adaptive practices and for increased off-take of farm produce from the project area.

2. **Economic Co-benefits from Productive Alliances**: The creation of productive alliances with the private sector and contribute to reduction in wastage especially of perishable products such as fruits, vegetables and legumes that is likely to extend beyond the value chains that the project will be directly engaged in.

### Gender Sensitive Development Impact

CASPs builds on a number of lessons and evidence accrued in the various IFAD-financed projects in the country, including on the improved livelihoods and role of women in rural communities. The project will build upon these experiences to ensure significant improvements for women headed households’ agricultural income (from crop, milk, value of livestock) and access to markets. However, there are a number of gender-related challenges detected in the gender assessment (Annex 8), such as insufficient leadership participation in policy, access to resources and services, and lack of information on climate change adaptation strategies. For each component measure access to products and services with disaggregation by sex and respective target groups will be ensured. The project will also ensure that women are involved in the design of CASPs has placed significant emphasis on women inclusion, including at community level, to improve women empowerment and their participation in decision making processes. Making the project more gender sensitive is a key challenge for the project as it will address the climate change vulnerability and risks. One of the key challenges that women face is because migration has become an important coping strategy for many households in response to shocks and stresses related to working abroad. This leaves women facing additional challenges, including dealing with a disproportionate

## D.4. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

112. Tajikistan is a transition country with a per-capita gross national income oscillating between low-income and lower-middle-income countries, depending on the year. It has the lowest GDP in Central Asia (confirmed for the recent years as low-middle income country – WBS, 2021) and one of the highest ranked among the countries of Eastern Europe and Central Asia (EECA) in terms of poverty rate (UNDP, 2021). Tajikistan is a landlocked country due to its low adaptive capacity. Climate-induced extreme weather events recurrently destroy livestock, crops, and infrastructure, leading to significant economic losses. Agriculture accounts for 35% of the GDP, accounting for about 19% of the country's GDP and 51% of its employment in 2018. Of the permanent cropland, only 47% is irrigated (FAO, 2016). Tajikistan has a population of approximately 9 million people with an average population ratio. Cotton, the country's most important cash crop, as well as other primary agricultural products such as wheat and barley are grown in the irrigated areas. The country's economy is heavily dependent on remittances from its citizens working abroad, which account for about 40% of GDP in 2018. These remittances make Tajikistan one of the countries most economically dependent on external factors and vulnerable to global market fluctuations. Another factor which makes Tajikistan particularly vulnerable is its low adaptive capacity due to its geographical location, high seismicity, drought-prone and hydrologic variability and the effects of climate change. Extreme weather and climate risks in Tajikistan have been identified as a major challenge for sustainable development. The country's infrastructure is poorly developed with limited access to basic services such as water supply and sanitation, electricity, roads, and other types of infrastructure. A study from 2019 identified mudflows, droughts, high temperatures, landslides, and floods as the most significant climate risks in Tajikistan. The cost of environmental degradation and climate change will rise if the GDP per capita is up to 54% by 2030.

113. Tajikistan is a landlocked country in Central Asia, and has a low private sector presence. According to World Bank and Asian Development Bank analyses, public investment has been the principal form of capital formation, as the share of private investment has been extremely low. Private investment is estimated to have accounted for only 10% of total investment during this period (IMF, 2021). The government of the Commonwealth of Independent States during this period. This partly reflects the private sector's small share of employment in Tajikistan. According to the World Bank, agriculture and services are the two largest sectors, employing 24% of the labor force as of 2018, dominate many economic sectors (IMF, 2021)). The presence of a formal financial system is limited with only 35% of the population having access to it. Microfinance institutions and community institutions are active. The entry point of the project is Village Organizations and other member-based organizations in Tajikistan. These organizations are formed by women and youth, who are among the most vulnerable population with appropriate instruments. The financial system has a limited penetration in the country, and there is a need to improve access to finance for women and youth. The project will support the financial sector in the country (20), and to bring a wide set of reforms to "enhance resource allocation and mobilization, improving labour productivity through human capital development, and fostering better

Livelihoods are expanding in the land-linked economy (1). In order to strengthen the link between project stakeholders and finance service providers, the project will work through common interest groups, facilitating dialogue among them and with representatives of private sector actors. This is necessary to ensure the progressive commercialization of on- and off-farm rural market activities in the target areas.

## 114. Tajikistan's relatively low level of socio-economic development, its inadequate infrastructure, as well as its high dependency on climate-sensitive sectors make the country extremely vulnerable to the negative impacts of climate change (2). The projected increase in temperature by 3°C and precipitation changes will affect the agriculture sector and the rural poor who have limited adaptive capacity. In addition, the projected increase in temperature is expected to cause forest areas will increase by 77%, and that some parts of Tajikistan may experience a fall in agricultural yields by 2050 (3). The climate change vulnerability assessment conducted for Tajikistan using the CCAFS tool predicts that without irrigation, yields are more volatile and rely entirely on climatic variability and hence are more vulnerable to climate change. In addition, the projected increase in temperature when setting all crop yields is projected to decrease. The main factors for decreasing yields are expected to include reduced runoff during the critical growing season and heat stress negatively impacting yields of key crops such as wheat, barley, maize, and cotton (4). Furthermore, the projected increase in temperature combined with higher levels of flood-related water contamination are projected to also increase the risk of vector-borne diseases. The increased frequency and intensity of droughts will lead to deteriorating water availability and quality due to increasing glacial melt and loss of biodiversity, and ultimately result in a decline in livestock productivity (5). In addition, the projected change in the hydrological cycle is leading to land degradation, erosion of fertile top soil and reducing agricultural productivity which impacts the food and nutrition security(6) and contributes to biodiversity loss.

## 115. The climate change vulnerability analyses suggest higher adaptation needs in rural mountainous areas where the majority of Tajikistan's population lives (7). These areas are already vulnerable through increasing pressure on pastures which are already subject to overgrazing and degradation as well as by effects such as increased mortality from high temperatures and reduced livestock mobility and utilization. Rather than focusing here on the small size and low land size of rural communities, it is necessary to diversify their risk. In order to adapt to these increasing challenges, rural communities require support for a range of adaptation measures including improved water management systems (8). The projected higher temperatures combined with higher levels of flood-related water contamination are projected to also increase the risk of vector-borne diseases. The increased frequency and intensity of droughts will lead to deteriorating water availability and quality due to increasing glacial melt and loss of biodiversity, and ultimately result in a decline in livestock productivity (9). In addition, the projected change in the hydrological cycle is leading to land degradation, erosion of fertile top soil and reducing agricultural productivity which impacts the food and nutrition security(10) and contributes to biodiversity loss.

## 116. The ongoing conflict between Russia and Ukraine is expected to have consequences on Tajik economy, especially for the poorest segments of the rural and urban populations. This impact is even stronger in Tajikistan where the majority of the population lives in rural areas (11). In addition, wheat (in particular) and fertilizers are expected to have a negative impact on the industrial economy of Tajikistan(12). In such context, CASP+ approach will not have the potential to restore the economic development of Tajikistan. The project is designed to support rural communities in Tajikistan which has a significant potential to absorb major negative impacts due to its investment in rural livelihoods, especially for women and youth (13). The project will also contribute to the overall objective of CASP+ to support climate resilience in remote rural communities targeted by the project. These areas have been chosen because they are characterized by high poverty levels, low access to basic services, limited economic activities and absorb the expected increased labour supply deriving from the return if migrant workers. Specifically, the project will use the Support to Common Interest Groups (CIGs) is expected to generate a positive spin on the economy.

## 117. Economic and social development level of the country and the affected population. Over the past decade Tajikistan has experienced significant economic growth, reducing poverty levels from about 50 percent in 2000 and 2002, the poverty rate fell to less than 83 percent of the population (World Bank), while the economy grew at an average rate of 7 percent per year. There is substantial spatial and seasonal variation in economic development across Tajikistan with rural areas being more vulnerable. Poverty rates are higher during winter and spring(14). The poor are concentrated in rural areas (81%) with some areas showing


---

**GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V 6.0 PAGE 45**

much higher than others. Non-monetary poverty indicators in rural areas remain high, and only 30 percent of the population in rural regions has access to safe drinking water.18 While the official literacy rate is 97%, the actual level of education among Tajikistan’s people has raised the level of young people. Although education is compulsory, many children fall to attend because of economic and security concerns in some regions. Even those who have graduated find it difficult to secure employment. The lack of job opportunities in rural areas forces many young people to leave their homes in search of work outside the country, leaving the economy vulnerable to external shocks. The private sector is also underdeveloped, with only 10 percent of total investments.

### Absence of alternative sources of financing

The Government does not have the resources to address all the challenges that are critical for its long-term development. In addition, the fiscal space of the country is limited, and IFIs already investing in rural areas are addressing rural development and related climate change adaptation issues. The Green Climate Fund (GCF) and other forms of GCF financing represents a unique opportunity to complement any other external (or domestic) financing to address climate-vulnerable rural communities in mountainous areas. The COVID-19 pandemic has had a significant impact on the country’s economy, with GDP growth falling from 5 percent during the first two months of 2020, compared to 7.2 percent a year earlier. The external current account has faced a decline in foreign direct investment (FDI) inflows and remittances, which have fallen by 18% for Tajikistan remains high. A growing share of the population reported reducing their food intake and overall consumption levels during the pandemic. In response to these challenges, the Government introduced tax reforms, boosted health and social spending, and eased monetary policy. In January 2021, the World Bank approved a $50 million loan for Tajikistan’s COVID-19 Response Program.

### The public debt nearly doubled as a share of GDP

The public debt nearly doubled as a share of GDP from 27% to 53%, while total external debt (public and private) jumped from 45.4% of GDP to 72%.—more than twice the average for lower-middle-income countries. The increase in debt ratios was mainly due to the large foreign borrowing undertaken in 2017 to support the Rogun project were main reasons for the rise in debt ratios. The latest debt service ratio is 35% of GDP, which is significantly higher than the 24% target set by the World Bank. Tajikistan’s debt is currently assessed as sustainable, but there is a high risk of debt distress in the context of the current economic situation and the projected increase in debt ratios over the next five years. Under the baseline, the external debt service-to-exports ratio breaches its threshold at Eurobond payment due dates (2025-37), contributing to a higher external risk rating. This highlights also the reason for a high growth rate of public debt as well as the negative impact on the country’s credit ratings, typically long payback periods.

### Need for strengthening institutions and capacity building

Tajikistan lacks an effective institutional framework or a legal framework that could incentivize authorities to pursue climate adaptation-related initiatives. In addition, as part of the development of the National Climate Action Plan (NCAP), Tajikistan has identified several challenges in terms of: (i) limited awareness of adaptation benefits among the public and experts; (ii) insufficient institutional flexibility and capacity for planning and implementing adaptation measures; (iii) limited financial resources; (iv) limited sectoral access and institutional capacity to collect, process and disseminate hydrological and meteorological data; (v) limited technical knowledge and skills of government employees who work on climate change issues; and vi) limited experience in the design, implementation, monitoring, and evaluation of adaptation programmes. The GoT has received assistance from UNDP and has developed an initial roadmap of the NCAP. However, there is a need to strengthen institutional capacity at all levels, including some of the institutions at the national level for enhanced coordination for the management of natural


---

resources, and adaptation, the current CASP is also designed to further strengthen the coordination among institutions at the national and district level as well as identify areas for policy reform and build the technical capacity of government officials in climate change mitigation and adaptation measures and green economy interests. There is also a need to strengthen the capacity of distantly and Jaamot governments to better integrate climate vulnerability into their development planning.

121. The above elements require a steady process of inclusive involvement, engagement, monitoring and technical support for the implementation of the CASP in all districts and at the national level. ### D.5 Country ownership (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

#### Existing national climate strategy

122. Tajikistan has been active in submitting its Intended National Determined Contributions (INDCs) to the UNFCCC, having submitted its first INDC in 2014 and updated it in 2021 with the updated NDC. The country has submitted three National Communications under the UNFCCC: 2002, 2008 and in 2014. The fourth National Communication is under development. The preparatory documents include inventories of greenhouse gas emissions, a vulnerability assessment, adaptation strategies, sector-specific adaptation recommendations, and mitigation analysis. The National Climate Change Adaptation Strategy (NCCAS) was adopted by the Government of Tajikistan in 2013 as part of the NDC process to guide the country over the next decade for adaptation and developing projects that address the country’s adaptation needs. This strategy is currently being updated with a focus on climate change vulnerability, identifying adaptation practices in light of climate change. The Government of the Republic of Tajikistan is also finalising its National Adaptation Plan (NAP) under the UNFCCC. The NAP will provide a framework for addressing the government’s need to align climate change adaptation with its disaster risk reduction and development priorities. The NAP will be developed in close coordination with the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) with UNDP assistance with GCF readiness support to complete the NAP.

#### Ongoing and upcoming Climate Finance projects in the country (including GCF, GEF, IFIs)

123. GCF presents a number of synergies with existing climate finance portfolio in the country; in this context, CASPs have been designed as an important element for supporting adaptation efforts in mountainous communities (as shown in Annex 2 and Annex 16, both on climate scenarios), largely suffering from lack of access to climate information and climate services. The GCF projects are expected to provide timely interventions, while detailed information is provided in Annex 27 (Synergies with Climate Finance in Tajikistan).

CASPs has been designed in close coordination with all institutions implementing the GCF-funded projects. It is in 13 countries across Southern and Eastern Europe, the Middle East and North Africa, Western Asia, and Central Asia. This programme will support readiness investment, with special focus on companies and households, energy efficiency and renewable energy

### Synergies with GEF projects

Key asset for CESP is in the pivotal role played by the CEO in NDA as well as executing Entity. CEP is currently supervising and implementing projects co-financed by GEF, which are expected to generate significant synergies with the NDA. These include: (i) the affected agricultural production lands in Central Asia and Turkey (CALCIM/FA) (the central role of CALCIM and FAO in this project [both CESP + ADB] is an asset to generate synergies and build on CALCIM results to support the NDA); (ii) the World Bank’s Land Restoration and Agricultural Resilience (LARRA) programme, expected to contribute to the NDA in line with the ones of Tajikistan; (iii) the USAID-funded project “Enhancing Climate Change Adaptation and Water Resilience Investment” (output 2.2). Detailed information on CESP+ complements to these interventions is provided below.

### Synergies with other IFM portfolios

The World Bank and ADB are implementing and planning additional climate finance projects (additional details in Annex 27). These include: ADB’s “Resilient Livelihoods for Women” project, which aims at improving the livelihoods of women and enhancing the livelihoods of rural women in the Khyber province; “Enhancing climate resilience in the Pyasai River Basin”, aimed to support irrigation schemes and improve the livelihoods of women in the agriculture sector. Coordination will be strengthened at village level, with a focus on women’s participation.

### Synergies with other GEF executing entities

The coordination at country level through the NDA and the other CESP+ executing entities will ensure complementarity and coherence. Moreover, the village selection criteria of CESP (detailed in Annex 21 CESP Project implementation manual) include complementarity with other projects implemented by ADB, World Bank, and World Bank as well as other development partners.

### Alignment with existing policies such as NDCs, NAMAs, and MRPs

The GCF project is aligned with the existing policies and priorities of the country given that the GoT priorities of reducing the country’s vulnerability to climate change via adaptation and increased resilience are in line with the national strategy for climate change. The project will contribute to a reduction of climate change vulnerability as well as to enhance the carbon sequestration potential of the country, which is aligned with the NDCs, NAMAs, and MRPs. It also aligns with disaster risk reduction and disaster risk reduction. Tajikistan has developed a National Development Strategy, which defines socio-economic development priorities for the country until 2030. This strategy integrates climate change adaptation as one of its main pillars.

The GCF project is also aligned with the Country Programme for the GCF (2019 - 2021) in order to define short-term and long-term strategies for Tajikistan. The GCF project will be integrated into the country programme, which is the Country Programme where GCF investments are needed: i) Agriculture and Forests; ii) Transport; iii)


---
**Page 52**
---

Energy (V), water resources, V) Cross-sectoral areas of activity (Education, Public Health Services, and Migration). CASP focuses on the first pillar and indirectly on the water resources. The project will focus on the energy sector as part of its overall strategy for development. Given that the country has a policy law, it needs assistance in building technical capacity at national level for green growth, including adaptation policy and planning, improved capacity for disaster risk reduction and improved pasture and livestock management. The project will also contribute to the achievement of the country’s climate resilient and sustainable management, contributing to climate change mitigation. However, while there is an up-to-date forestry law in place, it has not been fully implemented yet. The main objectives are to maintain biodiversity, improve the livelihoods of local people and leverage public and private investments.

129 CASP investments provide incentives for the government to take action and implement a range of policies and strategies designed to reform and strengthen natural resource management. Responding to our country’s need to build capacity in climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction, projects are designed to mobilize climate finance (GCF and IFAD) to address the national climate change adaptation and mitigation goals in line with the national commitments and strategic framework on climate change formulated by the government. The project will also contribute to the achievement of the country’s Nationally Determined Contribution2030 and the National Disaster Risk Reduction Strategy 2019-2030, and foster a conducive environment for private sector investments in sustainable agriculture and forestry and strengthen the execution of key government priorities outlined above.

• The Water Sector Development Programme (2016–2025) to promote increased access to resources and ensuring that water is managed sustainably. In this regard, IFAD has recently formulated the National Agricultural Investment Plan (NIP) for Sustainable Agriculture and Food Security (SAFS), which includes a number of investments for public and development partners as well as measures to unlock private sector investments for food and nutrition security and sustainable agriculture. These include investments in forestry, pasture and livestock.

• The Water Sector Reform Programme (2016–2025). Building on the ongoing efforts of the review of the water resources management law, CASP will support the implementation of the principles of Integrated Water Resources Management (IWRM), which take into account social, economic and environmental aspects in the planning, development and management and development of water resources.

• The Draft National Forest Policy Programme of the Forestry Sector (2016–2030). currently under final revision, outlines efforts for sustainable forest management and integrated development of the forest area. In this regard, CASP will support the government in addressing the challenges associated with unsustainable livestock grazing. CASP will provide support in enhancing natural forest regeneration potential.

• The Livestock Sector Programme for the Development of the Livestock Sector (2018–2032). In its efforts to promote pasture improvement, productivity enhancement and rational use of sustainable land resources, CASP will contribute to the development of a livestock sector that contributes to the country’s food security due to the importance of pasture for livestock feeding. In the same domain, CASP will support the government in addressing the challenges associated with unsustainable grazing. This programme aims at supporting the rolling out of the institutional mechanisms of the Pasture User Associations (PUA), Pasture Commissions and Pasture Management Plans.

Capacity of Accredited Entities or Executing Entities to deliver

## 131. CEP is the NDA and an Executing Entity for the project. Its Center for Implementation of Investment Projects (CEP-CIP) is currently the executing entity for the implementation of the GCF-funded Climate Adaptation Project in Uganda. The CIP has been working with the Ministry of Water, Environment and Natural Resources (MoWENR) to implement the GCF-funded “Building climate resilience of vulnerable and food insecure communities” project. The CIP also works closely with the National Hydrological Services Authority (NHSUA) for Hydrometeorology USD10.0m).” and has close collaboration with UNDP and FAO on the GCF readiness process. The CIP is currently implementing the “Building climate resilience of vulnerable and food insecure communities” project, which is a joint implementation between MoWENR and NHSUA. The CIP is also the implementing agency for all forestry management projects of UNDP, WFP, and WFP. CIP is a key executing entity in Uganda for the GCF readiness process. It has been working closely with the NDA to develop its technical expertise for identifying priority villages based on climate vulnerability analysis. CEP has district units with permanent staff at each district of Tadpoles. CEP also has departments at the provincial level: the Provincial Unit and a CIP office in Kampala, and 12 sub-district offices in the country’s capital city and 30 sub-district levels. At the sub-district level regulate and monitor environmentally damaging activities with the support of the NDA.

## 132. The PMU was established within the MoW in 2012 to support the implementation of investment projects in the agriculture sector. PMU has been managing IFAD-financed investment projects valued at USD45 million, including those for the Uganda Smallholder Agricultural Productivity Improvement Project (SAPI) and CASP. It has solid experience in rural areas where the population face challenges similar to those of CAF’s portfolio. The PMU is responsible for project implementation, monitoring and evaluation, risk management, financial, procurement functions as well as technical expertise in pasture, animal husbandry and health, agriculture, climate change adaptation, community mobilization, gender and social inclusion. It has a strong presence at the district level with 12 permanent staff at each district of Tadpoles. PMU will be further strengthened for the implementation of CAF.

## 133. FAO has a solid record of technical assistance in the country on climate change, including on policy and institutional strengthening, capacity building and project implementation. Its assistance has evolved from providing emergency assistance to deal with locust attacks to a more comprehensive approach to build a sustainable and resilient food system that can withstand the impacts of climate change and other forms of environmental change. Currently, FAO is engaged with CAF in the Readiness Programme, including the update of the NDA, GCF readiness process, and the development of the country’s climate adaptation portfolio, including GCF-funded FP 116, Kyrgyzstan: Carbon-sequestration through climate investment in Forests and Rangelands (USD 49.9 m) and SFA 814, Armenia: Forest resilience of Armenia, enhancing forest carbon stocks and green growth via mitigation (USD 207.7 m).

### Role of National Designated Authority

## 134. In 2014, the GoU appointed CEP as the NDA for issues related to the GCF. In its capacity as the NDA, CEP has demonstrated good capacity to lead the climate investment programming in the country.

The NAP's project is further strengthening the capacity of CEP.

## Engagement with civil society organizations and other relevant stakeholders, including indigenous peoples and local communities

Civil society in Tajikistan represents a wide spectrum of organizations, ranging from community and religious groups to professional associations and nongovernmental organizations (NGOs). Community-based organizations (CSOs) in Tajikistan are engaged in a wide range of activities. The impact of civil society on Tajikistan has been significant over the past decade. Civil society has played an important role in mobilizing communities, particularly women's groups, to participate in decision-making processes and to advocate for their rights. The role of these organizations in mobilizing communities: Civil society has become a crucial bridge between the government and the people. In 2015, there were approximately 3,685 public associations registered in Tajikistan. There were also 1,400 legally registered Village Organizations (VOs), some of which have formed higher level organizations such as Social Union Groups (SUGs). The VO/SUGs are the most important community-based organizations in Tajikistan and are made up of community members with roles and responsibilities specified by legislation which includes the Law on Local Self-Government. These VO/SUGs are also the main actors for implementing action plans. The inclusion of women in the current project will be facilitated through an approach that will build on the experience gained from previous projects, such as the National Action Plan (NAP) and the Committee for Women's and Family Affairs. The project will also be aligned with the National Strategy for Development of Women and Children 2015-2030 and the National Strategy for Equal Rights and Opportunities for Men and Women. Section G.2 below outlines the gender strategy of this project and G.8 outlines a more detailed Gender Action Plan.

## The financial structure of the project has been designed to ensure that the proposal's objectives are achieved in an efficient, effective manner

The financial structure of the project is designed to ensure that the proposal's objectives are achieved in an efficient, effective manner which reduces CO2 equivalent emissions and assists in climate risk adaptation in an effective manner. The level of income and indebtedness is a key factor in determining the level of concession being required for each component. The project will be financed through a combination of concessional external and internal resources in a ratio of 1:0.47%. The grant elements are being allocated for incremental costs, which are defined as those that would not have been incurred without the project. For households and common interest groups, the grants will defray part of the household investment and will contribute to their ability to access other sources of financing. In addition, the project will provide resources that are being developed (pastures, forests, agricultural land, and ecosystem services) (be- low referred to as "resources") to smallholders in order to assist them in accessing finance from smallholders to help them enhance their adaptive capacity due to the high risks and transaction costs of working with larger farmers.

## An analysis of the Values for Money Metrics of the project shows that the project investment is highly justified based on both financial and economic analysis (Annex 3). CESP builds on a broader portfolio of projects in Tajikistan, including the National Action Plan (NAP) and the Climate Change Adaptation Resilience diagnostics as entry point to define investment priorities. The project is expected to complement efforts already underway by other donors and agencies. A financial analysis was carried out to present the with- and without-project intervention scenarios using the indicative investment models that would be most likely supported by the project (see Annex 3) – using both CFP B.5 as well as a more detailed model for each component. The results of this analysis show that the net present values (NPVs) discounted at 11.07% and financial internal rate of return (FIRR) is above 20%. The project is also expected to generate significant co-benefits, including increased resilience (results presented in Table 4 and Table 5 below) and compared to a scenario without concessional financing (using own resources for Component 2 investments and accessing loans for Component 3).

investment condition, the project shows overall efficiency in the achievement of its mitigation targets with a cost of about 11.68 USDtCO2e in line with similar operations in the country[39].

### Economic analysis
The illustrative models used in the financial analysis were used for the calculation of the net present value (NPV), internal rate of return (IRR) and removing taxes and subsidies. The overall benefit stream has been generated based on the phasing of O&M costs, which are expected to be covered by the project’s own resources, including strengthening livelihoods and enhanced resilience through market-based approaches (1020 grants through workshops and 711 grants through training). The economic analysis was supported by financial projections.

The economic returns extracted from COSTAB application were used for the analysis. The discounted cash flow analysis resulted in ENV of US$1878.3 million (discounted at 6%) and EIR of 23.05%. Economic returns were tested against different levels of concessionality and in a more moderate climate scenario (RCP 4.5). As a result, the component is expected to be financially viable even under worst-case scenarios.

### Additional sensitivity analysis
The sensitivity of project investment was also tested against different levels of concessionality and in a more moderate climate scenario (RCP 4.5). As a result, the component is expected to be financially viable even under worst-case scenarios.
a. help beneficiaries to overcome the lack of adapted and accessible financial products;
b. help them in coping with the impacts of climate change by providing them with access to finance for adaptation investment (provided-in-kind, as labor or local material), as well as d. maximize their ownership on component activities and outputs.

The sensitivity analysis was tested against different levels of concessionality and in a more moderate climate scenario (RCP 4.5), which presented more solid results, shows adequacy of the project to strengthen resilience even under a worst-case scenario.

E.7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### 140. M&E Structure: A monitoring and evaluation system will be established for the CSEP+ project in accordance with the terms of reference provided by the IFAD and GCF.

The logframe indicators will be monitored and coordinated by the MoK (via PMU) as lead implementing agency responsible for the major share of the work. Three M&E Units will be established, one in the PMU, one in FAO and one in CEP/CIFP to monitor and report on the progress towards achieving the project’s components. FAO and CIF/CIP will be responsible for monitoring and reporting on the sub-activities under their respective components. The MoK (via PMU) will prepare a quarterly progress report and regular reports to the PJMC which will consolidate these and send them to its IFAD and GCF for their review.

An Annual Performance Report (APR) will be prepared by the PMU, FAO and CIF/CIP on all activities and sub-activities as described in the Project Implementation Manual. Section 4 (Annex 21).

### 141. Key M&E Responsibilities: All executing agencies will be responsible for ensuring that the agencies working under their supervision provide them quarterly progress reports. These reports will be used to prepare the Annual Performance Report (APR) and submitted to IFAD and GCF.

The PMU will submit an Annual Performance Report to IFAD and GCF. The responsibility for reporting on the progress under each component will be assigned by the MoK (via PMU) to either the executing agency or its implementing partner through supervision by another implementing partner. All contracts or MOUs with implementing partners will specify their reporting responsibilities, the frequency of reports to be produced and provide them with the templates for the required reporting formats.

All implementing partners will submit reports to the PMU, FAO or CIF/CIP depending upon which agency is overall responsible for the component. The PMU, FAO and CIF/CIP will prepare an APR on all activities and sub-activities as described in the Project Implementation Manual Section 4 (Annex 21).

### 142. Types of Reports: CEP, FAO (via PMU) and FAO will formulate an Annual Work Plan & Budget based on the approved project proposal and submit it to IFAD and GCF for approval. The APRs will be prepared by the PSIC and approved by both IFAD and GCF. Consistent with the AWMPB, reporting formats will be developed in consultation with the executing agencies and implementing partners. The PMU will prepare the reports and the Annual Performance Reports (APRs) by the M&E staff of each of the executing agencies as well as at the level of the MoK (via PMU) into one comprehensive APR.

The Annual Performance Reports (APR) will document the progress made in achieving the indicators (both quantitative and qualitative progress, respectively) in GCF’s Performance Managed Framework and any additional project level indicators. The APRs will be prepared based on the logical framework attached to this Funding Proposal. The PMU will specify their reporting responsibilities, the frequency of the reports to be produced and provide them with the templates for the required reporting formats.

The APRs will be submitted by the PMU to IFAD and GCF within six (6) months after the end of each relevant period as part of the proposal and report on the implementation and indication of specific aspects related to gender. All data will be disaggregated by sex to enable an assessment of the progress in inclusion of women in the project.

The key reports that shall be submitted have been identified in the M&E Reporting Matrix given below (Annex 21, Table 12: Types of report, timeline and responsibility). MoK (via PMU), CEP and FAO are the main implementing agencies responsible for preparing the APRs. The PSIC will prepare the templates for reporting formats. All implementing partners will be required to provide information on the core indicators, impact, outcome and output level indicators specified in the PMF. The APRs will include the status of each function and indicator as well as the progress made towards achieving the project’s components based on the logical framework attached to this Funding Proposal. The APR will be submitted to IFAD and GCF within six (6) months after the end of relevant period, with the first APR required to be submitted following the completion of the first relevant period. The second APR is due 120 days after the end of the relevant period and the third APR should be submitted within six (6) months of the end of the relevant Reporting Period.


---
**Page 71**
---

144. **MIS System**: An MIS system will be developed for the project to record key information of all beneficiary households. The M&E Unit in PMU will coordinate and produce a consolidated MIS report for the project. This will include data from the field visits, remote sensing applications, and other sources once sufficient coverage has been achieved and the M&E data base begins to get populated. thematic maps will be generated by the project and will be monitored through consolidated remote sensing applications or ground truthing. The MIS system will also provide a platform for analysis of the collected data. This analysis makes more meaningful in understanding the relationship between climate parameters and the pattern of agricultural production, as well as the impact of the project on the beneficiaries. The M&E Unit will use the Remote Sensing application- Earth Map. The MIS system will also record beneficiary phone numbers for feedback collection, tracking beneficiaries over time, tracking beneficiaries over time, and assessing impact.

145. **CaSe Monitoring**: Participatory methods will be employed to assess the project and incorporate findings into the MIS system. Beneficiaries' telephones for field reporting and ground truthing of remote sensing data will be established between the diagnostic output and citizen reporting to provide a means of tracking progress in the project. The results from these assessments will be compiled, analyzed, and summarized into updated formats of the annual reports submitted by these bodies. These will also appear as georeferenced maps on the Earth Map. This will allow for an assessment of the project's impact at the level of status of NRM. The project will also introduce and pilot test some innovative approaches to assessing bio-diversity impacts by agro-ecological types (see companion document).

146. **Survey Methods**: A baseline survey will be conducted in a third party in year one against which subsequent changes and impact will be measured. To measure attributable changes, the evaluations at mid-term and completion will use both quantitative and qualitative methods (e.g., focus group discussions, key informant interviews, etc.) in combination with counterfactual analysis (e.g. quasi-experimental designs). The baseline survey will include a pre-project assessment of project design and completion surveys, which will be confirmed during project inception). All surveys and impact assessments will be sex-disaggregated and gender-sensitive indicators both quantitative and qualitative data will be collected. A mid-term review and completion report will be conducted by the GCF Secretariat in addition to the annual supervision missions, a mid-term review and a project completion report. The project will undertake surveys at mid-term and at completion to assess the performance of the project, draw important lessons and contribute to the development of future projects. The results from these assessments will be used for the development potential as a three-point scale scored that is being developed by the GCF Secretariat. The assessment will also include an evaluation of the impact on the targeted households up to that period and will be incorporated in PMU's Mid-Term Review Report. The mid-term review report will also assess the project's progress towards achieving the impact of the project on the beneficiaries. The mid-term and final impact will compare project results with the baseline survey, which will be conducted at inception. The mid-term review will also assess whether a paradigm shift outlined in the project log-frame and the indicators of resilience outlined at the impact level. The completion report will provide an assessment of the project's performance against the GCF Secretariat Fund's higher-level goal of achieving a paradigm shift in adaptation to climate change at the national level and in the selected project districts in Tajikistan. The AE Unit will also hold participatory workshops at the interim and final stages.

148. **Quality control and quality assurance measures**: Each agency will establish a system for a periodic review of its implementation approach, including the use of feedback mechanisms. In addition to the M&E Units at PMU and CEP will also deploy its staff in the field to fact check and verify 5% to 10% of the reports submitted by the implementing agencies or on its own staff. Regular field visits will be undertaken by the AE Unit to monitor progress, identify problems, and provide feedback to the project team. The AE Unit will also hold workshops with the beneficiary representatives in the process and recording how the process and consultations were conducted.

A beneficiary feedback will also be prepared by each implementing partner on an annual basis and will form
