# F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1. Risk Factors and Mitigation Measures (see 2 pages)

### Selected Risk Factor 1: Lack of Institutional Coordination and Capacity

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Source/Owner | Description |

#### Lack of Inter-Ministerial Coordination and Ineffectiveness There is limited capacity and coordination at the inter-ministerial level between MDA, CER, MoH, MoWR, Committee of Emergency Situations and Civil Defence (CES) and other relevant institutions in the country. There is also limited expertise in understanding and awareness about climate change and climate risks as well as how to address these challenges. Capacity gaps and lack of institutional arrangements have hindered coordination between different stakeholders at all levels. The level of commitment towards addressing climate change adaptation and planning implementation are inclusive processes that encourage active participation from all stakeholders, including the most vulnerable groups. There is also a lack of knowledge about the needs of the most vulnerable. Inability to integrate climate change adaptation considerations into the national development plans and policies has resulted in ineffective coordination and synergy between disaster risk reduction and climate change adaptation. Ineffective coordination and knowledge sharing decreases effectiveness of adaptation measures. Lack of institutional arrangements for monitoring and evaluation of adaptation. There is also lack of coordination and synergy between disaster risk reduction and climate change adaptation. Ineffective coordination and knowledge sharing decreases effectiveness of adaptation measures.


---
**Page 73**
---

# Mitigation Measure(s)

There are several GCF supported readiness grants and projects for strengthening the capacity of CEP and the overall institutional coordination in the country. One main result of the first GCF Readiness Grant was the issuance of a Decree by the President of Tajikistan to establish the Coordination Council on Green Climate Fund under the GoT. The decree has been submitted to the government for approval. The focus of this decree is to coordinate activities, interventions, and projects that are currently being implemented in the country through the GCF. However, the roles and responsibilities of the NDA and the Coordination Council are not clarified in the draft decree. The current NAP Readiness Project (NAP-PR) will support the development of a new NAP which will include a capacity building component aimed at strengthening human resources in the CEP and priority sector agencies. These capacity building activities have been designed to respond to specific gaps and weaknesses identified by the GCF, including the need for better coordination among key agencies dealing with issues related to climate change adaptation to improve vertical and horizontal coordination, facilitate the integration of climate change considerations into development planning processes, and build and strengthen data and information capabilities, which support evidence-based policy-making and project design and strength the capacity for inclusion of green economy investments. The current CASP+ project will be implemented in close coordination with key government agencies dealing with mitigation and adaptation measures. Further, CASP+ is designed to integrate climate change considerations into development planning processes by initiating a participatory process which will involve all key agencies at the district level. This approach is expected to enhance coordination among the key agencies dealing with various key sectors that are impacted by climate change. The project will also be further enhanced through implementation as implementing agencies establish their implementation arrangements and then initiate procurement of goods and services.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V 8.0 | PAGE 70

## Financial risks are another factor which pose a risk and contribute to persistent delays in projects.
### Lack of strong procurement capacity is a key challenge as procurement plans are not developed in a timely manner and procedures are not always strictly followed. The lead time required for procurement is seldom predicted with accuracy. The indelicate delay has an impact on project implementation, leading to increased costs and potential loss of donor funds.

The ineffectiveness of implementing partners with the procurement processes of the financing agencies also contributes to delays in projects. In addition, the financial management capacity of the Government does not have strong systems for financial management and there is no well-defined framework or process for audits and monitoring. Tajikistan has a score of high at the country level for financial risk (ranked 162 out of 180 countries) according to Transparency International’s Corruption Perceptions Index (CPI) for 2023 with a score of 20/100. Tajikistan's CPI is in the bottom quartile and at the bottom of the Eastern Europe and Central Asia region with an average score of 35 for the region.

## Mitigation Measure(s)
At CEP, a dedicated CIF has been established based on the successful experience of CIF implementing a large range of CIF proposals and its strengthening due to the UNDP’s technical support as part of the first replenishment process. Both CIFs have strengthened their financial management because of their strong donor proposals.
In addition, strong procurement and financial management arrangements will be put in place with a detailed overall plan since the effectiveness of IFAD-funded portion of CASP+ (April 2023), IFAD has started to implement its own project management system. The project will have a standardized accounting software and proper internal control measures including flow of funds and recipient reporting.
The project will also have strong implementation support provided by the Financial Management Services Division (FMD) within the CIF, which is responsible for financial management and procurement staff for both CEP and M&As. The project will use a standardized accounting software and proper internal control measures including flow of funds and recipient reporting.

## Selected Risk Factor 4 Limited engagement of the private sector
### Cause
- Project implementation
- Technical and operational

### Description
Limited engagement of the private sector: Engaging the private sector in adaptation in Tajikistan is an on-going challenge. The private sector is small with limited capacity working in a relatively fragmented manner. In addition, there are no specific proposals or projects under the National Programme for Tajikistan, the NDA has limited ideas or proposals related to the private sector.
When looking at the potential areas of engagement such as water harvesting, irrigation, energy, capacity building, or transport. Without private sector engagement, it will be difficult to establish sustainable climate resilient inputs and technologies provision. Lack of collective action by the private sector also limits the ability for the government to establish partnerships between smallholders and the private sector.
Lack of private sector involvement also limits the potential for the private sector to participate in the implementation of adaptation projects in Tajikistan as well. There is a risk that the private sector may not fully participate due to the fragmented nature of production, i.e., many smallholders, who cannot guarantee product quality and quantity at any given time. Consequently, there will be limited participation of the private sector where possible.

### Mitigation Measure(s)


---
**Page 75**
---

Part of the NDA’s NAP and readiness plan is to support the private sector by identifying potential partners. There are a few projects (Section D.5) that currently involve the private sector such as FP025 which is private sector focused and is already working in the country to engage local financial institutions, microfinance institutions, and other relevant stakeholders to provide credit lines and information to customers in the private sector. One of the key outcomes expected from the NAP readiness plan is to increase the capacity of the private sector for climate change adaptation and participation in advancing climate change adaptation in Tajikistan. The CASPs include several activities designed to engage the private sector in direct partnerships with small producers in the agricultural sector, including providing technical assistance and training.

A strategy for building productive alliances is being relied upon to encourage the private sector to play a more active role in supporting rural communities and increasing their resilience to climate change. The private sector will be encouraged through improved infrastructure, equipment and arrangements for storage, transport, processing and trade and providing grants to smallholders which will help them to increase their productivity and competitiveness on the market.

Selected Risk Factor 5 COVID-19 pandemics and emerging influx of refugees

| Category | Impact | Other | Description |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Medium | High | Medium | Description |

COVID-19 pandemics and the influx of Afghan refugees into Tajikistan affects the operations: The COVID-19 pandemic has had a severe impact on the physical and psychological and economic well-being of rural communities in Tajikistan. It has also had an adverse effect on remittances and government revenues and created urgent balance of payments issues and fiscal pressures. The pandemic has led to increased demand for food, medicine and other consolidation measures that can be implemented over the medium term. While the development of how the pandemic will continue to affect rural areas is unknown, the impact on rural economies has been significant. The Government of Tajikistan has taken several measures in response which continues to maintain measures imposed to control the spread of COVID-19, as of mid-April 2021. The world situation with respect to the pandemic and its effects will continue to evolve over time and it is not possible at this moment how long the restrictions will continue and how they will impact project implementation.

The influx of Afghan refugees that began in the immediate aftermath of the US troop withdrawal and the Taliban takeover has placed a significant additional pressure on the natural resources and public sector infrastructure in the country.


The overall implementation will factor-in procedures and protocols to proactively ensure high standards of minimization of health risks related to the on-going pandemic. Specifically, this will entail ensuring that all project staff are fully vaccinated against COVID-19 before commencing work. If necessary, the project will encourage the adoption and use of SOPs and turn to virtual e-communications as far as possible. The design mission and the consultations for the current proposal were organized in a hybrid format with a small number of participants physically present. The level of refugees was reported lower than initial expectations. In case of emerging influx of refugees in the project area, the Government will inform via Project Steering Committee the relevant authorities at regional and national levels.

## G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

### 152. The project is categorized as a Category B based on an evaluation procedure and a standardized questionnaire that assessed the likelihood, frequency, intensity, manageability, duration and reversibility of adverse environmental and social risks associated with the project.

The assessment was conducted in accordance with the methodology described in Annex A and given in Annex 6 and a detailed Social, Environmental and Climate Assessment Procedures (SECAP)2012 document. The SECAP2012 document provides guidance on how to conduct an environmental and social screening of the CESP as was conducted on a preliminary basis at the project concept stage for consideration at the Board meeting in June 2014. A detailed environmental and social risk assessment will be carried out in advance of the quality enhancement review. The consultations that took place as part of the design process confirmed the need to conduct an enhanced environmental and social screening, a review and approval of project documentation by FAD’s Executive Board, the MoA (via PMU) and the CEF/IFAD Joint Technical Committee prior to approval process. In all steps, a number of internal and external evaluators provided inputs and peer reviews.

### 153. CASP is designed to introduce a series of measures that will strengthen the development and governance of natural resource management. Pasture management and joint forestry management plans are being developed in close collaboration with local communities and stakeholders. The project aims at the improvement of vegetation cover and the restoration of upstream degraded areas. The project will enhance capacity building for the implementation of the CESP, including the establishment of a network to enhance technical capacities of national livestock institutions to ensure efficient provision of public animal health services. The project will also support the development of private farms through efficient partnership between the private institutions and the CEF/IFAD.

The project will build capacity in the design and implementation of resilient ecosystem management system.

### 154. The design team carried out extensive stakeholder consultations—not only to assess the ESE risks and opportunities, but also to ensure compliance with GCF’s ESE and IFAD’s SECAP standards—both to also maximize the potential of environmental and social benefits. As outlined in Annex 7, the design team discussed the project with a wide range of stakeholders including local communities, women groups, youth, environmental and social risks in the target areas and related to the planned activities. Focus group discussions were held with representatives from the private sector, civil society organizations, and children. The project does not envisage any adverse social impact. Prior to implementation, a qualitative assessment of the aspirations of women and men of various age groups, especially the most vulnerable

# Grievance and Redress Mechanism (GRM): IFAD’s GRM policies will be implemented and can be accessed through the project website. The GRM is a key component of the project and is managed by the project's Executing Entity. The outline for GRM procedures and guidelines have been developed based on IFAD's corporate complaints procedure to receive and facilitate resolution of complaints and were reviewed in accordance with the United Nations' policies and mandatory aspects of its Social, Environmental and Climate Assessment Procedures. During implementation they will be further fine-tuned by the project’s Executing Entity and/or the National Grievance Mechanism (NGM) or the government or executing entity. However, if the complainants feel that they might be subject to retaliation, they may choose to file their complaints with the NGM or the government or executing entity. During the building program and the project will organize consultations to determine the most suitable channel for bringing forward grievances and complaints. The project will also be responsible for ensuring that the safeguard's performance monitoring, the project will also be responsible for documenting and reporting any grievances and complaints received through its GRM. The National Grievance Redress Mechanism will be available for assistance at any stage, including before a claim has been made.

## Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### G.2. Women suffer disproportionately from the insecurity caused by climate change because they are the ones predominantly responsible for securing food and income from agriculture plots and providing for their families. In Tajikistan, women make up about 73% of the agricultural labor force, compared to only 40% of those registered as extremely poor. Women have limited access to decision-making making, productive resources, and information on climate change adaptation strategies. The majority of Tajikistan’s population, or 65%, is engaged in the agricultural sector, with women being 61% of the total. Despite the high proportion of women involved in agriculture, they are underrepresented in decision-making bodies: (i) only 32% of women headed HHs compared to 80% men headed HHs own land and livestock; (ii) only 45% of women have access to irrigation water as compared to 71% for men; and (iii) compared to 87% for men, (iv) women face greater difficulties accessing knowledge, services and credit. Women are also underrepresented in forest management: (i) only 20% of Tajikistan’s population is from Forest Management Committees. However, they have a critical role to play as practitioners, and agents of change in climate resilience and biodiversity conservation as reflected in key documents such as the National Strategy for Climate Change Adaptation (Ministry of Agriculture, 2010); (ii) the National Development Strategy of the Republic of Tajikistan (2030); (iii) the Sixth National


---
**Page 78**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V 6.0 | PAGE 74

158. In line with GCF’s Gender policy, the project design is based on the understanding that gender relations, roles and responsibilities exercise important influences on women's and men's access to and control over decisions, assets and resources, information, and knowledge. This in turn influences their capacity to adapt to climate change and other environmental changes. The project will build upon a consultation held in the project area. Physical and virtual dialogues were held with both women and men from the project district and surrounding areas. These dialogues included representatives of local government bodies and civil society organizations working on gender issues such as the Committees for Women Affairs and the Ministry of Gender, Family and Social Development.

159. Gender aspects are mainstreamed in each of the three main components of the project as well as the project management arrangements. In Component 1, the GAP ensures that (i) the gender dimension is included in evidence based studies and visibility of women's role, needs and priorities in CC adaptation is reflected; (ii) women’s organizations and other groups representing women are involved in decision-making processes; (iii) women are involved as decision-makers in relevant committees; (iv) their view is captured in development of COPACs & implementation plans; (v) the project will ensure that women have access to resources and services, including land tenure, credit facilities, etc. In Component 2, the GAP ensures that (i) women will benefit equally from (a) training on climate smart agricultural practices (FFs); (ii) formation of Climate Change Adaptation Committees; (iii) provision of financial support for women’s groups to implement investment projects aimed at strengthening household's resilience. Women and Women Headed Households are also targeted in Component 3.

160. A Gender Assessment and a Gender Action Plan (GAP) has been developed for the project and is elaborated in Annexes 8. The Gender Action Plan of the project aims to have a transformative impact on women’s role, how they change through their engagement in climate change adaptation processes leading to natural resource management. The GAP ensures that 50% of all direct and indirect beneficiaries of the project will be women and that they will hold at least 30% of all leadership positions. The GAP also aims to (i) increase women’s access to resources, information and knowledge for climate change adaptation as part of the policy development process; (ii) increasing women's voice and representation in decision-making processes on climate change issues; (iii) increasing women’s opportunities to adopt climate adaptive practices to reduce their vulnerability.

161. The responsibility for mainstreaming gender will be included in the ToR of the PMU Gender Expert. Data collected during project implementation, including disaggregated and project reports will include sex-disaggregated findings, analysis and recommendations.

### Financial Management: IFAD will be responsible for the overall financial management of GCF resources allocated to the Project. The PMU will be responsible for the implementation of the financial and procurement procedures and guidelines which will be adopted. Disbursement, administration and processing of GCF procurements by IFAD shall be in accordance with GCF’s rules, policies, procedures and guidelines as well as those established under this Agreement. All financial management and procurement arrangements and records required to comply with the Funded Activities Agreement (FAA) and the Financial Management System (FMS) will be maintained. The Funded Activities Agreement (FAA) and will be aligned with the process and method agreed in the Annexes of the Agreement. The PMU will ensure that an independent external audit is conducted annually to maintain a financial management system, with separate statements, accounts and records of its own and GCF procurements. ### Procurement

As part of the detailed design for the CASPI mission assistance the public procurement process will be reviewed and updated in accordance with the GCF Mission Assistance Guidelines. The Government of Tajikistan is currently in the process of strengthening its procurement regulations. However, the Government of Tajikistan has not yet adopted any new or revised procurement regulations. ADB's Country Procurement Status Review, the EBRD's procurement assessment, as well as the Public Expenditure and Financial Accountability Assessment completed in Tajikistan's public procurement system indicate that there are critical gaps in compliance with IFAD's procurement policies and procedures. Despite significant progress, there is a lack of clear strategy and sufficient technical skills to ensure that all procurements are carried out in accordance with IFAD's procurement policies and procedures.

Externally financed projects undertake their work based on their own procurement rules and guidelines. In very few cases, the Government of Tajikistan may be involved in the project's procurement process or by a third party, shall be done in accordance with the laws, policies and procedures of IFAD to the extent that they are not inconsistent with the laws, policies and procedures of the Government of Tajikistan.

Handbooks and provisions stipulated in the Financing Agreement/letter to the Borrower will be followed.

### 165. Fao will use its own procurement rules for those activities under its responsibility regardless of the source of financing. The implementation arrangement for any activity financed by IFAD's financing or IFAD's financing which FAO receives would be in accordance with the FAO Procurement procedures and guidelines. This implementation arrangement is based on an assessment done by FAO of the HUDSON project, which concluded that the current procurement arrangements under the HUDSON project procurement would be carried out in accordance with the Financing Agreement and any duly agreed amendments thereto. The HUDSON project procurement arrangements are consistent with the rules in the financing agreement; (c) the cost of the procurement is not to exceed the availability of duly allocated funds for such purpose as approved by the revised IFAD Policy on Preventing Fraud and Corruption in its Activities and Operations as to be respected.

The PRU Procurement consultant will provide technical assistance for both PNU and CIP-5. The PNU and CIP will should apply the IFAD Guidelines in their activities and adopt procurement documentation in line with the standard forms agreed with IFAD.

## Climate Change and Food Security in Tajikistan: A Review of Evidence and Priorities for Adaptation Strategies April 2017 WFP

### Key Findings

- **Water Scarcity:** Tajikistan is one of the most water-scarce countries in Central Asia, with a per capita renewable freshwater availability of only 350 m³/year (UNDP, 2014). The country's climate is characterized by high evaporation rates and low precipitation levels.
- **Water Scarcity:** Tajikistan is one of the most water-scarce countries in Central Asia, with a per capita renewable freshwater availability of only 350 m³/year (UNDP, 2014). The country's climate is characterized by high evaporation rates and low precipitation levels.

## In 2015, during YPFP implementation, the country approved the Pansu Law that decentralized戒指and livestock management. It authorized the central government to transfer responsibilities for the development of agricultural and livestock policies to provinces and municipalities. The law also expressed its willingness to implement a policy of land reform and fodder crop production. UPRF supports the PMU's purchase of agricultural equipment and the use of local resources.

### With support from the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and co-funding from the World Bank, GCF will provide technical assistance with P&Q and on American financing through IFC. GCF projects will complete commitments will provide the required additional address to climate change mitigation and adaptation in the country.

### For the Agriculture, Forestry and Other Land Uses (AFOLU) sector, the project will support the development of a national forest inventory and account for the ecological and biodiversity sensitivity of project sites. The project will also support the implementation of the National Forest Policy and the National Biodiversity Strategy and Action Plan (NBSAP). The project will also include climate change policy, climate vulnerability, climate disaster preparedness and early warning systems, climate information system development and design, and capacity building.

Proposal name: Community-based Agriculture Support Programme "plus" (CASP+)

Project/programme size: Medium

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| Locally led adaptation: CASP+ supports locally led adaptation by building capacities of local agencies to assess climate risks and implement appropriate measures, thereby building a long-term legacy of local capacity for climate change adaptation. | Gender: The recent livestock project undertaken by IFAD had scope to make greater efforts in relation to gender. CASP+ should learn from the lessons learned from the IFAD project by implementing various measures, including more and diverse gender-sensitive activities and gender-specific results targets. Nevertheless, regular supervision of the project's progress in gender is required. |
| Enabling environment for green economic development and national capacity for emissions accounting: CASP+ will support refining existing laws, enhancing technical capacity of relevant institutions, and providing policy monitoring tools like georeferencing and remote sensing to facilitate sustainable management of natural resources and agriculture management. | Fiduciary capacity: The fiduciary risk rating of the project is high. |
| In addition, the project will enhance capacities for carbon-monitors accounting by providing support for the adoption of carbon accounting tools such as those developed by the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) - EX-Ante Carbon Balance Tool (EX-ACt), the Global Livestock Environmental Assessment | In response, CASP+ now considers a number of risk mitigation measures, including strengthening the project monitoring system, capacity-building of the fiduciary team of the State Enterprise Project Management Division, and starting and significant handholding from the Financial Management Division of IFAD during implementation. Nevertheless, regular monitoring of fiduciary compliance and re-evaluation of risk with adequate mitigation measures are critical. |


---
**Page 93**
---

Model – interactive (GLEAN-i) and the Biodiversity Integrated Assessment and Computational Tool (B-INTACT). It will also pilot B-INTACT.

Innovative: CASP+ promotes the adoption of innovative technologies and solutions (digital, technical, technological and policy) to mainstream climate change adaptation at both policy and user levels. It also supports research institutions to generate and disseminate knowledge.

Cross-learning and knowledge exchange: CASP+ has a provision to support cross-learning through a pilot project named 'Dairy Interventions for Adaptation and Mitigation' that will showcase and climate-resilient livestock in East Africa through exchange visits, including through China (South-South) and triangular cooperation.

Green credit line: CASP+ does not consider the provision of green credits for financing. However, it will facilitate access to existing credit lines that support green investments.

The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal in accordance with the terms shown above, subject to the terms agreed by the accredited entity, and if considered appropriate, subject to the conditions set out in Annex II of document GCF/JB.39/02.


## Summary of the Secretariat's assessment

### Project background

1. Tajikistan faces increasing climate risks, including rising temperatures, shifting precipitation patterns, and changing natural disasters, particularly in rural mountainous areas, posing threats to agriculture, pasture productivity and livelihoods.
2. To address these challenges, the international community has supported Tajikistan with a number of successful projects and focusing on carbon sequestration, improved livestock management and diversification of livelihoods.
3. Based on lessons learned from the Community-based Agriculture Support Programme initiative in Tajikistan, the proposed project, Community-based Agriculture Support Programme (CASP), aims to support the development of climate-resilient, sustainable, inclusive and integrated agriculture and forestry management in 21 vulnerable districts.
4. The project is incorporated into both the country programme of Tajikistan and the entity work programme of GCF.
5. The project's goals will be achieved through three main components:


---
**Page 94**
---

### Strengthening public sector capacity for transformative climate-resilient management of natural resources

#### Component 1: Strengthening public sector capacity for transformative climate-resilient management of natural resources (total cost: USD 2.5 million; core cost: USD 1.9 million)

- **Output 1.1**: Focuses on bolstering the public sector's ability to manage natural resources in a climate-resilient manner, integrating climate change adaptation and mitigation strategies.
- **Output 1.2**: Concentrates on creating an environment conducive to adaptive management of natural resources.

#### Component 2: Investing in community capacity for adaptation and resilience to climate change

#### Component 3: Strengthening livelihoods for enhanced resilience through market-based approaches

### Financing for the project amounts to USD 79,690,370 (of which USD 39,000,000 will be from GCF). The project is expected to benefit 650,000 people and indirectly reach over 2.9 million people, encompassing 24 per cent of the country's total population in vulnerable areas.

#### Component-by-component analysis

- **Component 1**: Focuses on bolstering the public sector’s ability to manage natural resources in a climate-resilient manner.
- **Component 2**: Invests in community capacity for adaptation and resilience to climate change.
- **Component 3**: Strengthens livelihoods through market-based approaches.

#### Output 1.1: Strengthening national institutions' capacity to oversee climate-resilient management of natural resources
- Policy engagement will prioritize areas such as animal husbandry, agroforestry, and water resource management.
- GCF funding will support activities related to policy development, gender-sensitive practices, and green investment coordination.

#### Output 1.2: Creating an environment conducive to adaptive management of natural resources
- Activities under output 1.2 will promote inclusive policy dialogue and provide technical assistance for regulatory reforms.


---
**Page 95**
---

monitor the implementation of the country’s green economy strategy. Through these efforts, component aims to bolster Tajikistan’s capacity for mainstreaming climate resilience in the management of natural resources and sustainable development.

### Component 2: Investments in community capacity for adaptation and resilience to climate change (total cost USD 19 million; co-cost USD 502 thousand)

#### Output 2.1: Support the development of CSAPs based on district-level climate diagnostics, ensuring a comprehensive understanding of climate risks and vulnerabilities.

This includes conducting diagnostic studies at the district level, developing strategies specific to each district to guide investment strategies. Activities under this output include conducting diagnostic studies, preparing district-specific adaptation plans, managing CSCPAs, and strengthening local capacities for monitoring and evaluation.

#### Output 2.2: Implement the CSAPs in the selected districts, benefiting 100,000 rural households through investments in climate-smart agriculture, livestock production, forestry, climate-resilient infrastructure, and agricultural productivity improvement.

Monitoring and evaluation will be conducted using a participatory approach involving farmers, forest users, and community representatives. The project will provide training tools and citizen reporting to track progress and outcomes. Funding for CSCPAs comes from GCF and IFAD, targeting investments with high potential for carbon sequestration and climate adaptation.

The CSCPAs will feature ecosystem-based solutions and a balanced investment mix, covering the full range of livelihoods and services necessary for enhancing community resilience. However, capacity gaps, including technical skills and infrastructure maintenance challenges, pose significant weaknesses. To address these, targeted capacity-building, technical assistance, and institutional strengthening are proposed to be further strengthened as remedial measures, aiming to enhance project success and sustainability.

### Component 3: Strengthening livelihoods for enhanced resilience through market-based approaches (total cost USD 19 million; co-cost USD 502 thousand)

#### Output 3.1: Promote climate-resilient agricultural production by investing in climate-resilient and diversified production systems connected to local and national value chains.

It will address the current limitations of production systems, which are not adapted to climate change impacts and lack long-term sustainability. The project aims to promote climate-smart agriculture through artificial insemination services, animal health interventions, and training, aiming to improve breeding practices and dissemination of technical innovations. Output 3.2 facilitates business partnerships between smallholder farmers and agribusinesses, enabling them to access markets for their products, secure market access and reduce environmental degradation. Output 3.3 supports the establishment of community-based enterprises through capacity-building activities, including training on linkages, with a focus on capacity-building and financial support.


---
**Page 96**
---

### Assessment against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High

20. To address challenges such as capacity gaps, infrastructure maintenance issues and coordination challenges among stakeholders, component X fosters economic inclusion, reduce environmental impact and enhance resilience through market-based approaches.

The targeted outcomes include financial assistance, partnership strengthening, community engagement and innovative monitoring tools will be improved with a view to enhancing project effectiveness and sustainability.

21. The project holds significant potential for both mitigation and adaptation efforts, aiming to shift towards low-emission sustainable development pathways while increasing resilience to climate change. Through sustainable land-use management, reforestation and conservation efforts, the project aims to reduce emissions by 654,872 tCO2e per year over a period of 27 years.

The estimated carbon balance suggests a net reduction of 654,872 tCO2e (per year) over 27 years, leading to an overall decrease in emissions. This impact will be tracked through improved land and forest management practices, covering 3,650 hectares of forests, 6,190 hectares of degraded lands, and 14,800 hectares of agricultural lands. The project will use remote sensing and by comparing remote sensing images with the reality on the ground through ground truthing.

22. Additionally, the project aims to reach over 2.9 million individuals, both direct and indirect beneficiaries (see the table below), with a focus on enhancing the adaptability of physical infrastructure and livelihood diversification in vulnerable communities living in natural resource areas. At the national, community and household levels, investments will bolster climate resilience by reducing vulnerability to climate risks. Through household surveys conducted at various project stages, the project's impact on increased resilience and reduced vulnerability will be assessed, ensuring effective outcomes.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

24. The proposed activities outlined in the project have a high potential to catalyse a significant paradigm shift across various policy areas, implementation mechanisms and household livelihoods. By promoting sustainable land-use management practices and livelihood diversification, coupled with increased private sector involvement, the project aims to enhance productivity and reduce emissions per unit of product. Previous experience, such as that from similar interventions in Tajikistan, has demonstrated the transformative impact of similar interventions, showcasing increased livestock productivity and


---
**Page 97**
---

### Sustainable development potential

**Scale: High**

The project aims to address climate change challenges while contributing to key Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), namely SDG1 (No poverty), SDG2 (Zero hunger), SDG5 (Gender equality), SDG6 (Clean water and sanitation), SDG13 (Climate action), SDG15 (Life on land), and SDG17 (Partnership for the goals).

It anticipates a number of environmental, social and economic co-benefits. The environmental co-benefits include improved soil fertility, biodiversity enhancement in pastures and forests. It also seeks to resolve land-use conflicts, reduce migration pressure, and improve rural livelihoods by creating new business opportunities to bring about social co-benefits. Economically, the project anticipates job creation, enhanced market access and increased productivity in agriculture and livestock practices through improved infrastructure and technology adoption. Additionally, it places significant emphasis on the inclusion of women, including at the community level, to improve the empowerment of women and their participation in the decision-making processes of institutions.

Overall, it presents extensive sustainable development impact by conserving the environment, promoting social inclusion, empowering communities and advancing gender equality in Tajikistan.


### Needs of the recipient

**Scale: Medium to high**

Tajikistan, a country vulnerable to climate change, faces significant socioeconomic challenges including poverty, limited private sector presence and inadequate infrastructure. The country's reliance on agriculture for its economy, particularly extensive irrigation crops and limited irrigation, exacerbates its vulnerability to climate impacts.

Additionally, reliance on remittances and external factors such as an influx of refugees from Afghanistan and the lack of access to international markets due to political instability, Tajikistan requires investments in climate-resilient infrastructure and sustainable agriculture.


---
**Page 98**
---

3.5 Country ownership Scale: High

Tajikistan has actively engaged in climate action by submitting detailed national deterministic contributions (NDCs) to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change, with ongoing efforts to update strategies and plans. The national climate change adaptation strategy and forthcoming national adaptation plan highlight priority areas such as water resources management, agriculture, and disaster risk reduction. This is consistent with priorities, and it is solidly placed in Tajikistan's country programme, which is in the final stages of formulation. The project has a strong potential for replication in the Eastern Europe Central Asia region, and specifically, in the Central Asian countries.

Several climate finance projects, including those funded by GCF and the Global Environment Facility, complement Tajikistan’s climate efforts, focusing on areas such as agriculture, water resources management, and disaster risk reduction. The project aligns with Tajikistan's existing policies, including its national development strategy and sectoral strategies for agriculture and water resources.

The CASP project also creates synergies with existing climate initiatives in Tajikistan by coordinating with institutions implementing GCF-funded projects, leveraging the expertise and ongoing activities of these institutions. By complementing existing World Bank projects and ensuring complementary to maximize the impact of climate finance interventions, the project will play a crucial role in mobilizing communities and ensuring inclusive participation in climate action.

To ensure national ownership for GCF investments, the Government of Tajikistan nominated the Center for Implementation of Investment Projects (CIP) to pursue the institutionalization of the project. The CIP is now the only national entity in this project category and increase direct access entry programming to GCF climate investments in the future. Currently, the CIP is the only nominated national entity from Tajikistan.

3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium to high

The overall assessment of this project's efficiency and effectiveness is medium to high. The accredited entity (AE) developed a detailed and robust economic and financial analysis with clear assumptions and scenarios for both baseline and counterfactual scenarios. Limited public investment and financial options in rural areas are identified as constraints, but the project has been able to leverage private sector investments. In 2021, 8.5 per cent of firms in Tajikistan were using banks to finance the purchase of fixed assets. The project has successfully engaged with both the public and the private sector market, including through matching grant windows. By addressing market failure and incentivizing private sector engagement with smallholders, the project fosters productive alliances to overcome constraints. Value for money metrics analysis demonstrates the project's


---
**Page 99**
---

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards

4.1 Programme brief: The programme intends to strengthen the public sector capacity for transformative climate-resilient management of natural resources, provide investments in community-based adaptation projects, and support the development of market mechanisms for enhanced resilience through market-based approaches.

Among the environmental and social co-benefits included improved biodiversity in pastures and forest areas given that the project aims at increasing the number of trees per hectare in the target areas. The programme is also expected to improve slope stability, minimize soil erosion, improve water quality, and reduce greenhouse gas emissions. Social co-benefits include more sustainable and resilient households able to better manage effects of labor migration on family communities.

#### Environmental (E&S) risk category and safeguards framework

4.1 Environmental and social (E&S) risk category and safeguards framework: The AE has categorized the programme as Category B based on a comprehensive screening questionnaire. The programme is expected to have minimal environmental risks, including construction and operation of support infrastructure, and processing facilities, which are small scale and located in areas with low population density. These activities may cause localized environmental and social risks and/or impacts which are reversible and can be managed through the implementation of appropriate mitigation measures. The programme is within the AE's E&S risk accreditation level. Since the types, scale, locations, and host communities and the specific interventions will only be identified and developed with


---
**Page 100**
---

beneficiary communities during the programme implementation phase, a framework instrument called Social Environment and Climate Assessment (SECA) was prepared for this programme.

Compliance with GCF's Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) Standards. The following paragraphs describe the programme’s compliance with GCF’s interim ESS standards.


### ES1: Assessment and Management of Environmental and Social Risks and Impacts

The SECAP described and analyzed the programme activities in terms of their potential environmental, social, economic, and institutional risks and impacts. The environmental and social conditions of the programme areas and the key regulatory frameworks in the country were also assessed. The SECAP provided a comprehensive assessment and management of E&S impacts and risks of the subprojects. The SECAP also provided an environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA) for each of the components of the programme which could have potential risks and impacts and their recommended mitigation measures. A summary of the ESIA findings, recommendations, and social and impact assessment (SSA) and the development of an ESMAP is also provided.


### ES2: Labor and Working Conditions

The labor code of the country regulates the labor and working conditions in the programme areas. The SECAP indicated that the programme activities will not require workers to work more than 40 hours per week nor require migrant workers or seasonal workers. The AEU ESE, however, should ensure that if there is a need for workers in the implementation of the activities of the programme, labor and working conditions are fully respected so that the rights of workers would be fully addressed and adequately mitigated.


### ES3: Pollution Prevention

Based on the description of the programme activities, subprojects to be supported may include small-scale infrastructure (roads, water harvesting, small irrigation systems), livestock production and veterinary services, and agroforestry. These activities could potentially cause environmental or small amounts of waste. Road and irrigation construction may cause temporary sedimentation in water bodies. Livestock production can lead to the release into the environment which may contaminate surface water, soil, and groundwater. Veterinary services may involve the use of chemicals that may have an impact on the environment. Mitigation measures such as manure management, water management, and composting, remote sensing on erosion and pollution monitoring, and avoidance of use of agrochemicals in forest plantations. More detailed mitigation measures for road construction, livestock production, water use, and waste generation will be undertaken at subproject identification during implementation.


### ES4: Community Health, Safety and Security

Community health and safety issues for this programme will be mainly on the hazards related to infrastructure such as on road safety, agricultural machinery accidents, fire hazards in rural areas, animal bites, livestock theft and rangeland rehabilitation, and small-scale processing. The SECAP indicated the conduct of road safety awareness campaigns, provision of first aid kits, fire extinguishers, and accident-related to such implement measures to address them. Measures such as ensuring national standards for construction of roads and bridges will be undertaken at subproject level (accompanying warning signs) in highly populated areas such as villages, schools, markets and other public places. The provision of first aid kits and fire extinguishers, safety signage will also be part of the capacity building intervention under the programme.

The SECAP indicated that "No physical resettlement or economic displacement will be expected." As far as road rehabilitation

in concerned, the programme foresees a "consultative design of road sites using local knowledge and safety measures will be included in the design of road rehabilitation activities according to national law." ### 10.2 Indigenous Peoples and Local Communities Living Natural Resources

The activities under the programme are expected to positively contribute to biodiversity conservation and sustainable use of natural resources. It is noted that the planter will support practices such as: increasing capacities for range rehabilitation, reforestation, and afforestation; restoring degraded rangelands with native and exotic grasses, shrubs and trees through assisted natural regeneration; maintaining sustainable grazables by avoiding overgrazing and keeping adequate grazing pressure on livestock; promoting agroforestry systems; using and other soil water storage techniques, among others. The programme will also promote participatory approaches to enhance the capacity of indigenous peoples and local communities in the protection of forests from illegal activities. Nevertheless, subprojects will be further developed with a view to minimizing any potential negative direct and indirect impacts and ensure that biodiversity issues are adequately managed.

### 16.8 ES08: Cultural Heritage, The Social, Environmental and Climate Adaptation Provisions indicate that the project activities will not lead to the conversion and/or loss of physical cultural resources.

### Sexual exploitation, sexual abuse and sexual and sexual abuse (SEAM). The SEAM safeguarding measure identified by the AE is considered by the Secretariat to be compliant with the SEAM provisions pursuant to the GCF Environmental and Social Policy. The project includes specific measures for preventing and responding to sexual harassment and violence as part of the SECAP. The AE has identified the project's potential SEAM risks with regards to the increasing number of women and children in the project area, and has accordingly incorporated SEAM risk mitigation measures into the Environment and Social Management Plan and/or other relevant documents. The Secretariat will monitor and evaluate whether the project is meeting its obligations under the GCF Environmental and Social Policy and related policies and regulations. The AE will conduct regular monitoring and evaluation on the progress regarding SEAM prevention and response activities. It will also ensure that all parties involved (including implementing entities, financial intermediaries and other entities) will work collaboratively and with local authorities to provide SEAM-related training and capacity building as needed.

The AE will provide technical assistance and implementation support missions. There will be three EEs: the State Enterprise "Project Management Unit" (PMU) will be responsible for overall coordination, while the PMU has responsibility for implementation, coordination, oversight and reporting to RAP and the government including liaison closely with the other EEAs and parties; the Committee on Environmental and Social Aspects (CESA) will provide advice and guidance to the Project Implementation Group and the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO).

### Gender policy

The fund has provided a gender assessment and a gender action plan therefore complies with the requirements of the gender policy of the fund.

The gender assessment indicates that the need for the project to address various aspects of general gender inequality in the country. The assessment demonstrates the role of women as agents of change. With a view to addressing gender inequality, and in alignment with the priorities identified by the government, the project will address multiple dimensions of inequality in relation to the climate change impacts policy, institutional arrangements, and access to resources.

The barriers to equality are embedded in prohibitive socio-cultural norms, unequal reproductive responsibilities and pay gaps between women and men. Access to land is a significant obstacle for women as they have limited access to land and often do not own it; those who do own land have limited access to inputs and far away from their homes. The discussion of gender equality will be addressed through the project with targeted interventions and actions to address this problem. The gender assessment has also identified that women are disproportionately affected by food-limited households and youth, but also recognizes their role as agents of change.

The project's interventions will address the underlying causes of the gender inequality experienced by women in the country. This is achieved through awareness-raising and sensitization activities. It will address barriers that prevent women taking part in decision-making processes and respond to their needs with regard to having access to inputs and resources. The interventions are targeted toward the agriculture sector and natural resource management, with the aim of increasing women's participation in these sectors. This is achieved through capacity-building and skill-related interventions as well as their engagement in forestry, biodiversity and natural resource

## 4.3 Risks

### 4.3.1 Overall project assessment (medium risk)

**58** This funding proposal is a cross-cutting project (52 per cent adaptation and 43 per cent mitigation) to strengthen the capacity of the public sector for the climate-resilient management of natural resources in Tajikistan. The project will contribute to increasing the economic and environmental resilience of livelihoods using a market-based approach. GCF is expected to provide a total grant of USD 90 million, and a loan of USD 75 million as a grant. Co-financing is expected from IFAD of USD 31.8 million as a grant and USD 62.75 million as a loan. The project will also be able to utilize USD 2.84 million of kind contribution from the Tajik Government and from United Nations agencies, which represents approximately 1.0% (USD 900 thousand) or 3.1 per cent of total financing.

#### 4.3.1.1 Accredited entity/executing agency capacity to execute current project (medium risk)

**59** IFAD is an international financial mission to improve global agriculture and food security. Since its accreditation in 2014, IFAD has provided over USD 2 billion in grants and concessional loans to fund livelihoods in fragile and developing countries. In addition to the USD 39.3 million CAS investment in Tajikistan, including a USD 39.3 million CAS investment. In addition, FAO as one of the EEs is a known institution with extensive experience in implementing projects in Tajikistan. Since its last accreditation, FAO has had 20 funding proposals approved by the Board worth over USD 600 million.

The other project EE is SEMPUI under the country’s Ministry of Agriculture, GCF-SEP (also referred to as SEPUMI). Both SEMPUI and SEPUMI have extensive experience with GCF and other development financial institutions. CEF partnered with GCF for the “Climate Change Adaptation Project” in Tajikistan, which was implemented in 2013–2016 and Uzbekistan, and worked with other development institutions such as the World Food Programme (WFP) and the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD), focusing on their forest management projects. As a unit of the Ministry of Agriculture, SEMPUI has been supporting the government in implementing various agricultural projects over the years, having expertise for agricultural subsectors and community engagement. While both government agencies have years of project implementation experience, there is concern with the general lack of coordination between these two entities at the national level.

### 4.3.1 Project-specific execution risks (medium risk)

**60** The funding proposal shows high credit risk given the B country rating of Tajikistan. The low country rating is due to challenges related to its low per capita income, narrowly diversified economy, weak institutions and governance, and exposure to foreign currency risk.


---
**Page 104**
---

Even with considerably young population profile, the lack of employment opportunities and low wages have contributed to the economy’s reliance on overseas remittances. Tajikistan also ranks low according to the worldwide governance indicators on government effectiveness, rule of law, regulatory quality, political stability, and voice and accountability. The country has been downgraded by contingent liability from state-owned enterprises. To help manage credit risk resulting from the low sovereign credit rating, the government has decided that it should avoid highly concessional loans with terms of a 40-year maturity period, 10-year grace period or below 2% interest rate.

The weak institutions and governance in Tajikistan also lead to a lack of poor institutional coordination and capacity. Among different ministries and institutions in the country there is no clear division of responsibilities for climate change adaptation risk. Even at the national government level, a consensus on how to address the challenges of climate change is lacking. As such, the project risks experiencing delays in approval and implementation. To mitigate this risk, the Government of Tajikistan (GoT) has requested GCF provide several grants under the Readiness and Preparatory Support Programme and implement them as soon as possible.

The GoT also requests that the project will benefit from a national adaptation plan which is being developed with the assistance of the United Nations Development Programme.


### 5.1 Project viability and concessionality

The project seeks to work at both institutional institutions and at the community level. At the national level, it aims to strengthen capacity for natural resource management in order to enhance climate adaptive and integrated management of agri/forestry resources. The project team will also support the GoT in developing a national adaptation plan (NAP) that incorporates action plans and strengthens livelihoods through a market-based approach. All these initiatives are critical to ensuring the long-term sustainability of the project, and their impact on project viability is expected to be manageable.

The requested participation from GCF will represent approximately 90 per cent by the total project cost. The funding will be funded partly by loan and partly by grant. The concessionality in terms of financing share between GCF and the AE is evenly distributed. Funding for the project will be provided through a combination of grant and concessional loan on highly concessional terms. The concessional funding is justified by the weak country rating and low per capita income. The combination of grant and concessional loan with both the grant and the concessional terms will help achieve climate impact without affecting the country’s high risk of debt distress.


### 5.1 Compliance risk (medium risk)

The compliance risk assessment reveals a medium level of risk associated with the project implementation such as an anti-money laundering and countering the financing of terrorism programme, sanctions compliance and counterparty due diligence are in place, reflecting the current regulatory framework.

However, it is important to note potential vulnerabilities, such as the project’s reliance on automated screening and ongoing monitoring for identifying entities subject to integrity due diligence. The risk assessment does not capture nuanced or emerging risks that require human judgment or deeper investigation. Ensuring that the project team has a robust understanding of these risks, along with periodic reviews, could fortify the project's resilience against compliance vulnerabilities.

Additionally, while the project has outlined robust internal controls and grievance redress mechanisms, ensuring their effective implementation will be critical in mitigating any


---
**Page 105**
---

potential compliance challenges. Overall, continued vigilance and proactive measures will be essential to maintain compliance throughout the project life cycle.

# Results monitoring and reporting

The funding proposal is for a cross-cutting project focusing on five results areas: four adaptation results areas and one mitigation result area of the Integrated Resilience Management Framework (i) Most vulnerable people and communities; (ii) Health and well-being, food and water security; (iii) Ecosystem services and biodiversity conservation; and (iv) Climate-resilient ecosystem services; and (v) land use. The project aims to benefit a total of over 2.95 million beneficiaries with 0.61 million direct and 2.27 million indirect beneficiaries and deliver 6.85 Mt CO₂ equivalent emissions reductions.

The funding proposal uses the templates and provides content in line with the requirements of the Integrated Resilience Management Framework. The sections on the lines of change and land management (logframe) have been aligned to the template structure and format.

The proposal also contains all the elements of the logframe in terms of identification of results areas, indicators, means of verification, targets, assumptions, etc. These are adequate and are expected to allow successful monitoring and reporting of project results. The information provided on each element of the logframe will help the communities concerned adopt the adaptation and mitigation mechanisms that have been identified.

The assessment of adaptation benefits and beneficiaries as well as the mitigation impact is a strength of the funding proposal. Following the Secretariat’s guidance, separate annexes on the methodology for estimating adaptation benefits and the methodology for estimating the mitigation impact have been prepared. The adaptation impact estimation methodology provides a description of the assumptions made in the project design and the expected adaptation-related beneficiaries. Similarly, the annex on mitigation impact provides assumptions and methodologies to estimate the emissions reductions that will be achieved by the project. These methodologies have allowed a systematic estimation of expected adaptation and mitigation impact which will help in developing approaches to achieve them during implementation, as well as monitoring and reporting.

### Implementation timetable: The implementation timetable has been provided in the request for proposal (RFP). The key milestones and deliverables at each stage of implementation are identified. Adherence to the implementation timetable will facilitate the smooth implementation of the project as well as the timely delivery of the results planned intervals.


# Legal assessment

The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 24 September 2018 (the “AMA”) and it became effective on 9 November 2018. The Accredited Entity’s five-year accreditation term lapsed on 6 November 2023 but was extended by Decision R37/18, which is currently in effect until 5 November 2028. The next scheduled revision of the accreditation term or the date on which a revised accreditation framework is adopted by the Board (the “Accreditation Term”) will be determined at the time of adoption of Decision R37/18, and negotiation of the Amended and Restated Accreditation Master Agreement is ongoing.

no The Accredited Entity has not provided a legal opinion or certificate confirming that it has obtained all final approvals and that it has the capacity and authority to implement the project.

no The proposed project will be implemented in the Republic of Tajikistan, a country in which GCF is not precluded from privileges and immunities. This means that, amongst other things, GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks need to be factored into the project design. The GCF has provided appropriate privileges and immunities to the Minister of Finance of Tajikistan on 31 March 2016. The latest commissioning letter was issued by the Ministry of Finance and National Bank of Tajikistan on 20 May 2024. Negotiations on the agreement are ongoing.

no The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity Unit (IIU) have been informed that the GCF has not provided appropriate privileges and immunities to the Republic of Tajikistan for any activities and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where the GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. The Heads of IRM and IIU have advised that payments to the GCF are made only after the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

no The Republic of Tajikistan is a signatory party to the Convention on Biological Diversity (CBD) and the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC). The project will be implemented in accordance with the Committee for Environmental Protection (CEP). Although it has been agreed that the RoI and CEP are Executing Entities for this project, under the Accredited Entity's proposed contract with the Republic of Tajikistan, the GCF Secretariat is required to enter into a MoU with CEP which is responsible for project implementation. For the Accredited Entity to pass down and inform the Board that it has obtained all final approvals, including the RoI from CEP, the Accredited Entity has agreed in the term sheet that will ensure that RoI passes down, monitors and reports on the status of the MoU with CEP. The GCF Secretariat will require the Accredited Entity to comply with the AMA and FIA requirements as if it had a direct contractual relationship with CEP.

no To address the matters raised in this section, it is recommended that any approval by the Board be made subject to the following conditions:

(i) Submission by the Accredited Entity to the Fund of a certificate or legal opinion, in form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, within 120 days after Board approval, confirming that the Accredited Entity has obtained all final internal approvals necessary for implementation of the project and that it is prepared to implement the proposed project;

(ii) Signature of the FAA in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat within 30 days from the date of Board approval, or the date the Accredited Entity has provided a certificate or legal opinion confirming that it has obtained all final internal approvals, whichever is later; and

(iii) Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP233

## Proposal name:
Community-based Agriculture Support Programme 'plus' (CASP+)

## Accredited entity:
International Fund for Agricultural Development

## Country:
Tajikistan

## Project/programme size:
Medium

### I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

#### 1.1 Overview
The funding proposal is for an investment in adaptation (57 per cent) and mitigation (43 per cent) targeting the public sector to support its capacity needs to strengthen climate resilience, enhance livelihoods, increase agricultural productivity, improve rural communities' resilience to climate change and enhancing the climate resilience of livelihoods using a market-based approach.

The geographic selection approach starts from the identification of water sub-catchment basins to identify the most vulnerable villages. This approach facilitates the grouping of villages into clusters based on their vulnerability scores. The selection process for village clusters and village selection is based on the Climate Vulnerability Index (at the district and village level) which rely on multivariable data sets, including as variables, inter alia, a simple formula for measuring poverty, land use, soil quality, water availability, climate change impacts, and considering the climate vulnerability index with socioeconomic variables such as poverty incidence. Moreover, the selection process will consider other relevant criteria, such as the type of natural resources (e.g., rangelands and forests), the proximity of urban markets for agro-value chain activities and the adjacency to other villages in the cluster. The final list of villages included in each village is defined at the implementation stage.* and will ensure that no village with other relevant criteria, such as the type of natural resources (e.g., rangelands and forests), the proximity of urban markets for agro-value chain activities and the adjacency to other villages in the cluster. The final list of villages included in each village is defined at the implementation stage.* While protecting to be objective and fair, using the Climate Vulnerability Index for village selection might also obscure the actual criteria, for example, the adjacency and/or presence of other relevant criteria used for selection or deselection.

#### 1.2 Impact potential
The project estimates reaching 2.0 million beneficiaries, with 650,000 direct (approximately 7 per cent of the total population, or 31 per cent of the project area population) and 2.3 million indirect beneficiaries (approximately 24 per cent of the total population).* Over 7 million livestock units will be supported through improved feed resources, animal health or training services to increase the productivity of their livestock.*


---
**Page 109**
---

4. The total lifespan of the project is 20 years, in which a total of 6,853,612 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (t CO2 eq) (annual average of 340,000 t CO2 eq) will be mitigated from pasture and forest management, afforestation and improved herd management across 5,900 ha of land. The project will also contribute to the mitigation of climate change adaptation components which will also arise along the agricultural production and business value chains, including livestock farming. These include the use of solar-powered water pumps for the disinfection of shelters and stabling structures using straw, which was successfully tested in previous projects, as well as energy efficiencies in the irrigation and drying systems or reduced use of fuel.

5. Impact on the fiscal resilience of the Tajikistan government. Another potential climate change impact is the increased cost to the Tajikistan government for livestock feed rations. The introduction of district climate resilience diagnostics (DCRD) should lead to the integration of climate measures in the local development and investment plans of the government and the private sector. This will ensure that the Tajikistan government can respond effectively through efficient and effective planning and budgeting of expenditures based on priority climate change adaptation projects, as well as increased awareness among stakeholders for rehabilitation-expenditures due to impacts of climate change in the project areas. This will potentially reduce the fiscal burden on the Tajikistan government.

6. Risks to potential climate impact. Effecting behavioural change among the targeted livestock farmers will be a challenge. It may be difficult to convince the targeted farmer beneficiaries (commercial and smallholder) that they need to make changes (e.g., increasing herd size and flock size, pasture grazing, buying good-quality bulls) to the new methods of cattle rearing. The experience of the Tajikistan government in the field of livestock farming have enabled them to survive through the years, and letting this go overnight to a new approach will be difficult for many farmers. It is therefore important to focus on increased awareness and understanding of the new approaches to herd management will be crucial. Moreover, considering that most of the farmers are exposed, and possible competitors who may not be better off, it requires more effort.

7. Genetic improvement is expected to encourage commercial farmers and smallholders to select for traits such as heat tolerance and disease resistance in their livestock by encouraging a reduction in herd size and helping to reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions. Indeed, the funding proposal states that livestock production is currently low and limited by the poor quality of animals. It also notes that the current genetic improvement efforts are changing the genetic pool also need to be discussed. Similarly, risks and knock-on effects of a transition from traditional to modern livestock farming practices will also occur.

8. The monitoring and reporting of GHG emissions post project implementation needs to be assured. Such monitoring and reporting are clear during project implementation but not assured for the period after project completion when the new technologies will occur.

9. The funding proposal's impact potential is assessed as medium to high.

13 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium to high

16. The funding proposal's multi-pronged approach to mainstreaming climate is laudable as this is likely to trigger the potential paradigm shifts.

18. Mainstreaming in higher education. The funding proposal will streamline climate change and ecosystem management approaches in the training curricula and research


---
**Page 110**
---

programmes of national research institutions and academia.* The integration of climate change issues into the curricula of universities and training institutions will establish best practice for potential scaling up and replication across educational institutions in the country, targeting agricultural extension officers, public administrators, and other stakeholders such as Tajik Agrarian University and Tajik Academy of Agricultural Sciences. Climate change will also be mainstreamed through the development of a national climate change strategy by the Public Administration. These students, especially public administrators, will eventually work for, or own, the private sector, which will be better equipped to integrate climate change into government policies, projects and other activities with respect to integrating climate change adaptation and/or mitigation elements.

14. The role of research institutes and the private sector to produce evidence on natural resource management and climate change for policy dialogue and climate-sensitive technical assistance is also important in order to provide a new knowledge base that can be used by planners and decision makers in pursuit of their development objectives, particularly along a low-carbon and climate-resilient pathway.

15. Mainstreaming in the private sector. There are many interventions activities in this area that contribute to potential paradigm shifts. These include enhancing the mainstreaming of climate adaptive management practices of natural resources in all national institutions and organizations; strengthening the capacity of the Ministry of Agriculture for the legislation and regulatory framework related to the Strategy for the Development of the Green Economy; providing technical assistance to the Ministry of Agriculture for the development of training to government officials and strengthening of the Ministry of Finance to enable it to monitor and evaluate the impact of climate change on agriculture, forestry and livestock; mainstreaming of gender-sensitive climate adaptive agricultural practices; training on the utilization of the Climate Change Adaptation Assessment Tool (CCAT), the Green Economy Model, Ex-Ante Carbon Balance Tool, and Biodiversity Integrated Assessment and Computation Tool, which directly captures the government’s commitment to the implementation of the development of the above-named Green Economy Strategy as well as the capacity-building of the Ministry of Economic Development and Trade and the Ministry of Finance. These activities are supported by the Ministry of Agriculture, the Ministry of Economic Development and Trade and Ministry of Finance staff, on how to potentially integrate climate change into the performance assessment framework.

16. Mainstreaming in policy. The funding proposal indicates that the animal health and breeding regulatory and strategic frameworks to be reviewed and updated to ensure compliance with international standards and best practices for livestock production supporting climate resilience and environmental protection. A learning by doing approach would ensure that climate change is mainstreamed into the existing national strategy on sustainable husbandry and livestock management subsector. This initiative can potentially lead to either voluntary or mandatory adoption of the new framework. The proposed activities in this component have a huge potential for replication across other agriculture subsectors in interventions that will eventually lead to mainstreaming climate in public finance management (i.e. planning, budgeting).

17. The very ambitious component on strengthening public sector capacity for transforming agricultural production and management of natural resources is reinforced by the proposed project’s choice to work principally through the Ministry of Agriculture and the Center
