## Mainstreaming in community-level planning: **D30**. Based on the opportunities and constraints identified by the communities themselves, a participatory approach to community planning in 400 GSP* target villages. Ultimately, it will form the basis for defining the climate-sensitive development plans of these communities. These plans will include land use improvement/restoration (pasture management plans); (ii) climate-resilient infrastructures strengthening/rehabilitation; (iii) provision of public services; and (iv) forestry investments.

### Partnerships or private alliances: The interventions are focused on new productive activities, such as livestock products groups and agro-ecosystem aggregators**. These are based on localized public-private partnership models, distinct from the typical national flagship public-private partnerships. They will be led by local communities, including women’s groups and farmers, funded by the government via grants from windows 1 and 2 with some co-finance from window 3. The aim is to create a new development model that breaks the paradigm shift in the way livestock, local farmers associations and the government could come together to address the much-needed local economic development.

### Integration of climate change adaptation into existing solutions: The deployment of modern technologies to address traditional issues and challenges in livestock care**. Leveraging on the existing public-private partnership models for funding, monitoring and reporting of developmental activities*** and linkages with the wider agriculture business value chains**** enables the local communities to transition into more low-carbon and climate-resilient development pathways.

### Over all mainstreaming of climate in both public and private sector planning is the main priority. The integration of climate change adaptation into existing solutions will eventually lead to greening of the components of the public financial management systems, the effect of which is to enhance the fiscal resilience and sustainability of the government. From this approach, it can be seen that the government has a strong incentive to integrate climate to manage their potential losses and damage due to the impacts of climate change.

### The key parameters for an appropriate approach to mainstreaming climate in policy, planning and investment activities at the community, district and central level, weak buy-in from the other activities may delay but will not stop the integration of climate into the decision-making process.

Overall, the paradigm shift potential is high.

20. Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The funding proposal (cuts across several SDGs). It will also contribute towards the following specific SDGs: SDG 2 (Zero hunger), SDG 5 (Gender equality), SDG 8 (Decent work and economic growth) and SDG 17 (Partnerships for the Goals).
21. Economic co-benefits. The establishment of youth and veterinary training groups and the matching grant facilities will enhance the inflow of private sector investments in the target sectors, including agribusinesses. The creation of farm stations and service stations (e.g., artificial insemination) [31] will provide long-term confidence among entrepreneurs and investors in carrying out more local business activities.
22. Environmental co-benefits. The project will generate environmental co-benefit due to the initiatives on management of forests and pasturlands. Another is the potential increase in biodiversity, which will be achieved through the creation of wildlife corridors for beneficial insects. The proposed project will also lead to improvements in soil fertility and increased water retention.
23. Social co-benefits. The potential social co-benefits, enhancing the socioeconomic conditions of the communities involved, are as follows: (i) employment generation via labour in forest plantations, animal husbandry, and other related activities; (ii) improved climate-proofing of agricultural infrastructures and animal care services; (iii) enhanced community resilience via improved erosion and flood control measures versus extreme climate events; (iv) training and capacity building for the youth and veterinary practitioners; (v) climate-smart agricultural practices; (vi) increased access to markets through farm stations and service stations, with improved livestock and agriculture production techniques and adapting practices to changing climatic conditions. The project will also benefit vulnerable groups and proactive alliances.
24. Gender-sensitive development impact. Women and female-headed households are active participants in this funding proposal. Women's capacity will be enhanced through increased access to climate knowledge and technology solutions to manage communities' resources, especially in the mountainous regions[32]. Gender is also taken into consideration in the mainstreaming in climate-smart agricultural practices.
25. The risk to sustainable development is low given the multi-pronged, integrated and holistic approach of CASP.
26. Overall, the sustainable development potential is assessed as high.

29\. The funding proposal endeavours to address the needs of the people, community, critical ecosystems, investors and entrepreneurs, and government stakeholders through several interventions, as discussed in paragraphs below.

30\. Need for bottom-up approach in community planning: The use of DCR in developing the climate-sensitive community action plan shows a strong bottom-up approach and community linkages. DCRD is based on the assessment of risks and opportunities emerging in each district, which will be used to develop the climate-sensitive community action plans (CCAPs) for the targeted CAGS villages. DCRD will form the basis for defining the climate-sensitive community action plan (CS-CAP), which will include interventions such as land use planning, pasture management (pasture management plan), strengthening and rehabilitation of climate-resilient infrastructure, procurement of agricultural machinery and diversity and agriculture business value chain investments.

31\. Need for climate-related technical capacity and knowledge of government stakeholders: The project will provide training to government officials on the sustainable use of natural resources such as pastures, forests and agricultural land, as well as optimize ecosystem services (biodiversity conservation, carbon sequestration, water management) through the implementation of the planned Green Economy Program, and the implementation of DCRD, which is the main intervention that will enhance the capacity of village communities and at the district level to develop climate-sensitive community action plans for sustainable land use.

32\. Need for farmers' sustainable livelihoods and investments: The proposed project will address the need for sustainable livelihoods by providing improved pasture management and production to access to markets. On production, it will provide improved pasture management schemes, climate-resilient infrastructure, agriculture assets, a grant window facility for bee farming and livestock, and an agricultural extension service. In addition to improving the post-production side, the interventions will open a grant window facility for the use of cold storage facilities, which will be used by farmers to store their produce. Through this project, the project will help to set up alliances such as the producers alliances and common interest groups (CIGs) that will provide support to smallholder farmers in accessing credit through the agriculture business value chain. It will also facilitate access to investors via the Platform for Remittance Investors (PRI), which is a platform established by the Ministry of Finance to increase access to formal remittances and expand the digital financial inclusion of migrants and remittance recipient families and returnees, with a focus on women and youth in rural areas.

33\. Overall, the trends of the recipient are assessed as high.

### 1.6 Country ownership

34\. Alignment with national climate strategy and priorities: CAGS is aligned with climate change adaptation under Tajikistan's intended nationally determined contribution, the National Disaster Risk Reduction Strategy (2019–2030) and the National Climate Change Adaptation Strategy 2030. It will also support the national development plans, such as the National Land Use Plan (2016-2030), the National Water Resources Management Plan (land and water) the National Agricultural Investment Plan for Sustainable Agriculture Development and Food Security (2017–2030), the National Forest Strategy 2015–2030, the National Strategy for Sustainable agriculture, including investments in forestry, pasture and livestock intensification), the Water Sector Reform Program (2016-2030) and the National Strategy for the Development of the Forestry Sector (2016–2030) (forest, livestock, grazing).

### Efficiency and effectiveness: Scale: Medium to high

#### Strong project foundation:
The proposal is informed by the successful experience of the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) over the last 15 years (2008–2023) in vulnerable mountainous areas of the Khatlon region. This experience includes projects such as those listed in paragraphs [I] and [II]. This provides strong confidence that GCF will be relying on the robust evidence-based data and lessons learned from these projects.

#### Strong technical and institutional partners for project implementation:
The choice of accredited entities (AEs) with a proven track record, such as IFAD, will guide the effective and efficient implementation of the proposed project. IFAD, an accredited entity, has strong experience in Tajikistan (e.g., Khatlon Livelihood Support Project, Livestock and Pasture Development Project). The AE is also well positioned to lead this project because it has substantial global experience in designing and supervising rural development investments, with a strong focus on climate change. IFAD's portfolio of projects in the region includes projects that include climate fund management, climate and environmental finance and GCF climate programmatic support (e.g., GCF Tajikistan Project 102, Niger FP135, Brazil FP182, West Africa FP183, West Africa, FP272, East Africa).

#### The Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations:
As an executing entity, it has extensive experience in climate change adaptation projects in Tajikistan, which shows its capability in providing comprehensive climate change assistance. The relevant GCF climate projects that have been involved include MIF116, Kyrgyzstan, and SAP014, Armenia.

#### Concessional cost of GCF funds:
Tajikistan has a B minus country rating which signifies its high credit risk and thus the concessional cost of GCF funds is very high (up to 25% in low capacity*).

#### If the funding proposal is approved, GCF will provide USD 39.0 million in the form of grant (USD 18.0 million) and loans (USD 60.0 million). The co-financing commitment from IFAD is in the form of grant (USD 31.0 million) and senior loans (USD 67.5 million), and there will also be a contribution from Tajikistan through its national budget and other multilateral agreements. The co-financing ratio is at 1:1.4 and accounts for 51 per cent of total financing.

#### The requested participation from GCF will represent approximately 90 per cent of total programmatic costs, with the balance being covered by IFAD and Tajikistan. The structure of funding share between GCF and the accredited entity is evenly distributed. For component 1, the GCF contribution is USD 35 million (87% of total cost), which is for a transformative climate-resilient natural resource management; this is co-financed by IFAD, the national budget, and other multilateral agreements.

# Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The ITAP recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal.

To strengthen further the submitted funding proposal, the following recommendations are presented:

(a) Develop a plan to incorporate the potential future distribution of species into a revised version of the biodiversity management plan.
(b) The ITAP recommends further that the following be made part of the Project Implementation Manual, specifically:
(i) a pilot project at the community or district level government at the end of the implementation phase, and provide budget for the monitoring - reporting and evaluation (MRE) component to cover the costs associated with land management components, and GHGs from livestock and pasteland management; and
(ii) a data collection format for the target farmer beneficiaries covering (i) the potential risks associated with the adoption of new agricultural and livestock management practices, including but not limited to: [a] the risk of genetic loss due to the genetic pool-knock-on effects of the transition from natural pastures to fodder feeding; [ii] a caveat that the proposed interventions need to be undertaken as a whole package in order for them to be effective in terms of productivity and incomes; (iii) an indication that selecting interventions according to the farmers' preferences is not sufficient, but rather that they should be selected with safety net for farmers that do not become better off even after following the programme in its totality.

## Country: Tajikistan
## Project/programme size: Medium

### Impact potential
IFAD takes note of the positive assessment and thanks ITAP for identifying the risks and challenges associated with the proposed changes introduced by the project. The challenge of a drastic behavioural change in livestock management patterns is a key pre-identified risk in the project TOC, whereby in addition to awareness pull factors such as improved genetics, feeding, and health services, there will be a need to address the push factors that may lead to unproductive practices. In addition, Annex 2.3a details Farmers' Users' Unions-managed specific inputs for livestock production.

IFAD also takes note of the need to assure the monitoring and reporting of GHG emissions post project implementation. Training to CEP and other related entities will be provided in order to ensure that national entities mainstreaming GHG sequestration monitoring is government institutions.

### Paradigm shift potential
IFAD takes note of the high paradigm shift potential and equally thanks ITAP for stressing the high potential of the project to contribute to climate change mitigation efforts leading to mainstreaming climate in public finance management, by recommending a learning-by-doing approach for the review and update of the regulatory and strategic framework.

### Demography

1. According to the National Statistical Agency, the total population of Tajikistan accounts for 9 Million. As of July 2020, the regional population distribution was as follows, showing higher percentage in Khatlon Region (3.3 Million) followed by Sugd (2.7Million):

2. Women account for 51.5% of the population. Households are larger on average in rural areas (6-5 persons) than urban areas (4-8 persons). Overall men head the majority of households (79%), with only 21% headed by women (being 84% male-headed and 16% female-headed). The median age is 30 years old. Newborns (25%) are the population under age 25, and 38% are younger than 15. Four percent of the population is age 65 and older [TajStat, 2019].

### Poverty

3. Extreme poverty, measured by the international poverty line of US$1.90 per day (WB data from 2003 to 2014), fell markedly from 27 percent in 2003 to 4 percent in 2014. According to the Government's own calculations, using a national poverty line, extreme poverty was at the same level as before: 8 percent in 2013 and 3 percent in 2014 [TajStat, 2015].

4. The most recent poverty data available at national level is for the register further reducing the number of people living below US$2.7 (TajStat, 2020). The thresholds of the national poverty line (900) on monthly consumption are calculated as follows: less than TJS162 for poor; less than TJS358 for vulnerable; and less than TJS594 for middle class*.

**Maternal Mortality Rate**

According to the Demographic and Health Survey (TJ DHS, 2017) for every 100,000 live births, 32.0 deaths are from pregnancy-related causes; and the adolescent birth rate is 45 per 1,000 women of ages 15-19. According to WHO, Maternal mortality is conditioned by poor quality of services in antenatal, intrapartum, and postpartum care, lack of skilled personnel, inadequate infrastructure, and lack of means of transport especially in rural areas, and inadequate access to emergency obstetric care. The most substantial reductions were observed in Khatlon (from 31% to 15%), GBAO (from 34% to 24%), and DRS (from 30% to 18%). The largest reduction of 67 percentage points occurred among women at lower levels of education and wealth (TJ DHS, 2017)**.

**Child Mortality**

Overall, under-5 mortality declined between the 2012 and 2017. The TJ DHS surveys from 43 deaths per 1,000 live births to 33 deaths per 1,000 live births. Infant mortality decreased from 34 to 27 deaths per 1,000 live births over the same period. The risk of dying in early childhood is much greater for children under five years old living in rural areas (58 deaths/1,000 live births) than in urban areas (20 deaths/1,000 live births). Khatlon generally has the highest mortality rates (40 percent) and Dushanbe the lowest (11percent). The percentage of children who are immunized is 30 per cent: DRS, same for GB AO, and 33 per cent Sugd (TJ DHS, 2017)**.

The percentage of women with a secondary or higher education is highest in Khamtan (72%), followed by DRS (69%), GBOA has the lowest percentage of women with a secondary or higher education (51%).

School attendance for boys and girls

18. The primary school Net Attendance Rate (NAR) indicates that 83% of children age 7-10 who should be attending the primary level are doing so. The secondary school NAR shows that 87% of children age 11-17 who should be attending the secondary level are doing so. There is a slight difference in attendance rates between boys and girls (83%), while the secondary school NAR is slightly higher among girls than boys (90%). There is also little difference by region, with attendance rates being about the same in urban and rural areas (84% versus 83%). There is also little difference by region, with attendance rates being about the same in urban and rural areas (84% versus 83%). The lowest primary school NAR is in Suguh (78%) while the secondary school NAR is lowest in DRS (95%). GBOA has the highest NARs (88%) for primary schools, followed by Khamtan (86%).

Labor Force Employment and Unemployment

19. According to national statistics as reported in the Labor Market Survey, female employment rate (40.5 percent) is significantly lower than men (59.5 percent) and the unemployment percentage stands at 31.6 for women against 68.4 for male. The unemployment rate in rural areas amount to 59 percent while in urban areas it equaled to 41 percent (TjRdS, 2016)**.

20. It is also observed that employment status varies widely by region. At the time of the survey, the highest employment rate was registered in Sugd (31%), the lowest in Sughd (18%). The same trend can be seen for Tajikistan.

21. For women, employment varies depending on the socio-economic status. According to the survey results, 54% of women with a higher education are currently married or who were previously married are more likely to be employed than those who have never been married. Half of women who are divorced, separated, widowed, or single are employed (TJ DHS, 2017).

22. Women with a higher education are three times more likely to be currently employed as women with no education or only a primary education (54% versus 16%). Among women with secondary education, 39% were employed in agriculture; the rest were most likely to work in agriculture (33%); 30% were employed in professional, technical and related jobs; 27% worked as managers, officials or proprietors; 18% in skilled manual labor; 11% in unskilled manual labor, and 11% in clerical jobs. The survey reports that involvement in agricultural work has tripped among women with a higher education (TJ DHS, 2017). The proportion of women with contrast involvement in unskilled manual labor has decreased, from 45% to 11% (TJ DHS, 2017).

# Women employment in agriculture

24. According to FAD Gender Profile (2016), women’s formal employment in agriculture presents only a narrow view of women's engagement in this sector because their participation in agriculture is typically informal. Women are commonly employed as hired labourers, family members, or brigades. Women's tasks are largely restricted to field labour, such as weeding, sowing, harvesting, and transporting produce from the fields; whereas the selection of seeds, fertilizers, and plant protection materials is controlled by men. Notably, a large proportion of women working as horticulturists or market gardeners in urban areas are paid piece rates with little flexibility, women generally receive very low pay or only in-kind payments (FAO, 2016).

25. Women's labour is concentrated at peak times, such as the harvest season, and during the winter months their income-earning opportunities are more restricted. Most of them work on small plots of land, which are typically on household plots and residential lands (cotton farms) to earn extra income.

According to the Demographic and Health Survey (TDHS, 2017): women age 15–19 were more likely to have worked in agriculture (64%) than women in older age groups. Women working in agriculture are more likely to be engaged in agricultural occupations. Women in rural areas result to be much more likely involved in agricultural occupations than women in urban areas (44% versus 2%).

Agricultural workers in the regions of Khyber Pakhtoonkhwa, Balochistan (51%) and Sindh (30%) than among women in other regions (13% or less).

Approximately one-third of women work on a farm with an annual income generation of less ($25–$53) and are in the lowest two wealth quintiles (49%-54%). as compared with 2% of women who have a higher education.

Results show that three-quarters (75%) of women are paid in cash only and 9% are paid in cash and in-kind. Around 1 in 7 women are not paid (13%), and 3% receive their wages in kind. The majority of women work for less than one relative (56%), one-quarter work for a family member, and 19% are self-employed. Women's earnings have increased over the years; female earnings increased from 53% in 2012 to 75% in 2017. The percentage of women engaged in agriculture who received cash only has also increased since 2012, from 5% to 47% (TDHS, 2017).

## Informal Employment (outside agriculture)

### Table 3
At the time of the Survey mentioned above (TajStat, 2016) in Tajikistan, 249,536 people were employed outside agriculture at the main work, including 189,289 men and 60,247 women. Thus, in the percentage ratio, men accounted for the majority of people employed (33.2%) outside agriculture (versus women at 27.7%).


---
**Page 130**
---

34. This is especially true for small-scale and family production businesses that need low-paid workers, such as agriculture. The outmigration of males in the agriculture sector has led to increased involvement of female labor. The result is a significant increase in women's employment in this sector. A sharp increase in women’s participation rates in the agriculture sector, either as self-employed or as agricultural wage workers, was observed between 2013 and 2016, with the percentage of women employed being economically active in rural areas (Tajtian, 2018).

### Women's employment and engagement in the Forestry Sector

Official statistics generally combine various categories of related work: agriculture, forestry, fisheries / aquaculture. As already reported, women represent 60.8% of the sector, no further disaggregation is provided by subsector. The most recent available data (2015) shows that women are less represented in the forest sector than in other sectors such as agriculture and fishing. According to the most recent available reported in the FAO Gender assessment conducted in 2016), indicates that few men, and almost no women, are contractually employed in forestry or aquaculture (FAO, 2016). Women's employment is also less represented in fisheries than in agriculture and livestock. The percentage of women working in public forest institutions. Labour market statistics for 2010 indicate that the number of people employed in the forestry sector was 1,700, of whom 200 (or 12 percent) were female employees (FAO, 2016).

36. The draft Strategy on Forest Sector Development identifies a gender imbalance in employment within the forest industry, with men representing 92 percent of employees. According to data submitted to the FAO forest resources assessment in 2015, women represent only 8% of workers employed by private companies working in public forest institutions. Labour market statistics for 2010 indicate that the number of people employed in the forestry sector was 1,700, of whom 200 (or 12 percent) were female employees (FAO, 2016).

37. According to FAO, the fact that staff are required to carry out patrol functions on foot for several days at a time (e.g., forest rangers who patrol forests for several days), suggests that this type of work is unlikely to be considered suitable for, or accessible to, women. However, according to the same source, women's almost invisibility in the sector is not due to their lack of interest but rather because they are not engaged in forest activities in other ways (FAO, 2016).

38. Rural women spend a significant amount of time on domestic tasks and for domestic food production; male household heads are usually responsible for buying firewood. Ecological organizations have noted that widespread deforestation has resulted from the lack of access to firewood. According to the same source in general rule, women in forest villages engage in the informal collection of non-timber forest products, for home consumption and for sale, and this pattern is likely to be replicated across Tajikistan. In 2013, the state of non-timber forest

The FAO gender assessment points out that women and men can play important roles in conserving forest areas by eliminating harmful agricultural practices (cropping, grazing), and using and managing the resources of forests that can be used in the long term for reforestation. Both women and men have specific knowledge about the use of forest products which should also be taken into consideration by forest management. However, because women are largely absent in the formal forestry sector (in employment and in policy positions), special attention needs to be paid to their role as well as to their gender knowledge.

41. A similar view was also shared by the representatives from Biodiversity International who met with CASP+ Design Mission (April 2021). It was explained the relevance to increase awareness, and participation of women and children in forest conservation and management activities.

42. Recommendations from analytical papers and discussion with stakeholders during the design mission suggest that the strategy on Forest Sector Development for 2015-2030 and the National Action Plan should play a key role


---
**Page 132**
---

In increasing gender-specific information about the forestry sector, including awareness events about gender and climate change.

The strategy foresees the establishment of a national supervisory board on forestery that will include the participation of the Committee on Women and Family Affairs (CWF). The strategy also foresees the implementation of follow-up from responsible entities are required to ensure that gender-related constraints are considered in all forestry activities. Gender-sensitive policy dialogues events dedicate space and time to discuss the gender and climate change.

### Women's role in conservation of Biodiversity

The sixth National Report on the Convention of Biological Diversity (CBD, 2019) strongly highlights that gender-balanced forest communities are more effective in performing all their functions (plant protection, forest regeneration, maintenance of biodiversity and distribution of forest use) than communities composed mainly by men. Women who have a better knowledge of forests are on average able to determine a greater number of plant species (trees, shrubs, grasses, herbs, flowers, fruits, leaves, bark, seeds, roots).

The report highlights that women have extensive experience in traditional knowledge and practices that are effectively used in the conservation of biodiversity. Leased forest areas and dekhkan farms have organized mini-nurseries for tree seedlings which are planted by women during most of the work. The most significant initiative implemented by women and benefiting both women themselves and contributing to sustainable environmental management is the establishment of nurseries, which has increased the distribution of local varieties in the Khadoni district of the Khatlon region (CBD, 2019).

The report stresses that despite women's prominent role in conservation of biodiversity, their participation in the design, planning and implementation of environmental policies is still low. Action is needed to ensure their participation (CBD, 2019).

### Smallholder farming in Tajikistan

Tajikistan is a landlocked low-middle-income country located in Central Asia. Given the country's mountainous geography, the total arable land area is limited to only 3 percent. The FAO publication, Small family farms country factsheet, (FAO, 2018) indicates that agriculture and livestock production remains as the key source of the population’s income as well as for Tajikistan’s economy. Agriculture accounts for about 45% of GDP according to the National Statistical Service of Tajikistan (National labour survey), but due to high out-migration rates of work-abled men the agricultural sector has become increasingly feminized, i.e., 68.3 percent of women are employed in the agricultural sector compared to 41 percent of men (FAO, 2018).


---
**Page 133**
---

48\. Smallholders in Tajikistan live on marginalised farmland, resulting in an average farm size of only 0.2 hectare. The head of a smallholder household in Tajikistan can take advantage of 10 years of education on average and leads households that represent the majority of the population (75 percent). However, this household is headed by women, yet, representing 16 percent of small family farms in Tajikistan\. The number of livestock owned by small family farms in Tajikistan is increasing. Smallholder households are more likely to own sheep and goats than cattle, small cattle and horses as the most common owned livestock. However, pasture resources are limited and overgrazing is a major problem, leading to soil erosion and over-grazing of pastures\. More than half of the small family farms in the country remain below the national poverty line\(. (FAO, 2018)\).

### Women's role in agricultural activities

49\. According to the Tajikistan Country Gender Assessment conducted by the Asian Development Bank (ADB, 2016)¹²³ women are heavily engaged throughout the entire production cycle of agriculture. They are considered as unpaid users or workers\) in agriculture formally counts only when they are registered as legal entities or farm workers\). Women are also heavily involved in unpaid family labor for agricultural activities such as planting and harvesting, tending to the home garden and securing water and food\. Although the agriculture output of kitchen gardens is not included in national statistics, Tajikistan's economy is presented without sex attribution in statistics and national reports (ADB, 2016).

50\. There is little evidence that women have become empowered through agricultural activities. Women are often seen as victims of one of the most exploitative. As explained above, women’s agricultural work is characterized by seasonal nature, low productivity, lack of access to land, poor working and break-breaking conditions, lack of access to and control over productive resources, limited participation in decision-making activities and low technical and specialized knowledge (ADB, 2016).

51\. Women are often described as lacking the appropriate skills and knowledge required, whether as farmers or managers. This hampers their ability to take on those roles that would continue participation as informal workers\). It reinforces the perception that women have no experience or knowledge of farming activities. In Tajikistan, women are not considered as farmers; rather, as breadwinners and heads of households, are farm managers regardless of whether they own land or not (ADB, 2016). Women’s agricultural work is largely restricted to field labor such as weeding, sowing, transplanting, and harvesting, which do not require decision-making, whereas the selection of seeds, fertilizers, pesticides, and other inputs are made by men. However, although both men and women are involved in livestock-raising, it is men who decide on purchase, sale and other operations linked to its management (ADB, 2016)¹²³.

Livestock husbandry is a major agricultural activity, and raising livestock is the norm for rural households (73 percent of households raise livestock, including cattle, sheep, goats, pigs, donkeys, mules, dogs, sheep, goats and poultry). There are gender differences in the extent to which households are engaged in raising livestock. Women are more likely than men to keep chickens and other small animals such as rabbits or guinea pigs. Men tend to have livestock; they tend to have cattle and poultry, possibly because dairy farming is traditionally considered a "female" work or because selling extra milk and eggs is a relatively low intensity means of supplementing household income (FAO, 2010) [4]

**55. According to FAD country assessment, gender differences in the ownership of livestock is both a reflection of differential access to the resources needed to buy or keep animals, and it also indicates the presence of gendered roles in livestock management:**  
Women are more involved with animal husbandry (tending cows and milking) and poultry farming are generally perceived as women's work.

**56. Men have greater involvement in grazing, feed production and the purchasing and sale of livestock. Women and men also have differing roles in the processing of livestock products such as meat, milk, eggs and poultry. Women are involved in shearing, transporting products to market and butchering, whereas women are involved in dairy product (FAD, 2016).**

**57. Furthermore, women play a prominent role in managing backward poultry farming:**  
The USAID report: agriculture technology and commercialization assessment of the livestock sector in Bangladesh (2013) states that in Khulna province keep poultry and an estimated one-third of households headed by women also have poultry. The scale of these household operations is small often fewer than 50 birds per household. Most of this production is for home consumption, but some is traded locally. The demand for eggs and poultry meat at the household level is mostly controlled by women (FAD, 2016).

**58. As it is the case in other areas of farming, women's success in raising livestock is dependent on their access to information and level of knowledge about running a farming enterprise, and their access to finance and to other key resources such as veterinary and extension services and training (FAD, 2016).**

### Women’s access to Land

#### 59. Access to land and tenure security remains a severe constraint for women.

Although the land reforms legally guaranteed equal rights to men and women to use land (and be conferred shares or certificates), the reality presents a different situation.

#### 60. Women's lack of access to land is underpinned by several forms of inequality.

They often lack information about their rights to land as members of collective farming households, and they are less likely than men to have the means, either financial or in terms of time resources, to undertake the registration process. As a result, women are more likely to be denied rights to land; many are “effectively excluded from the process of obtaining dhan or household land-use rights because administrators are often dismissive of women’s claims and/or their ability to pay for the registration fee.” Women are also less likely to hold lesser shares of the land that they work and are less likely to report tenure insecurity.

#### 61. According to data reported by FAO, the majority of rural households possessed land (86 percent). Male-headed households were also more likely than female-headed households to own land (92 versus 75 percent of FHs), and they tended to own larger plots of land. The average size of land plots owned by male-headed households in rural areas was 10.3 sotka, while that for women’s plots was 9 sotka (FAO, 2016).

#### 62. Considering only those households with land in rural locations, data below show that when land owned by households is combined with rented land and land rented out, female-headed households own significantly less land than male-headed households (39 sotka for female compared with 50 sotka for FH). Both households rented rural land by women; men use most of their land plots for farming, and very few rural households have land that they rent out (FAO, 2016)4.

| Land Plot Characteristics | Male-Headed Households | Female-Headed Households |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Average own land area | 15.6 | 10.2 |
| Average own land area used for farming | 9.8 | 7.3 |
| Average rented land area | 4.4 | 5.9 |
| Average rented out land area used for farming | 1.9 | 0.9 |
| Average own + rented or + rented out land area | 20.4 | 16.8 |

#### 63. In terms of the characteristics of the land plots that are registered to women, they are less likely to have rights to use “presidential land” and more likely to have rights to household plot. Furthermore, women’s land plots are generally further from irrigation facilities or other water sources than men’s plots. When women do register their land rights (after divorce or the death of a spouse), they received "the worst land plots"; at a considerable distance from irrigation facilities or with

### Women dekhan farms

A particular concern is the serious underrepresentation of women either as agricultural title holders or decision makers. Dekhan farms are the most common type of commercial enterprise in Tajikistan. They are privately-owned commercial farms that function as legal enterprises and can be based on the work of an individual, family, collective farm, or a combination of these entities collectively. Family and collective dekhan farms are managed by a head who officially holds the title to the land and makes decisions about the use of resources on the farm. The number of dekhan farms has increased annually, from a total of 30,842 in 2008 to 106,597 in 2014 (FAO, 2016)###.

Looking at the share of dekhan farms managed by women has been rising alongside their general growth. In 2014, women headed 13% of dekhan farms (up from 8% in the previous year)###. The growth in women-led dekhan farms from 2008 to 2014 is attributed to increased government support for women's involvement in dekhan farming and to register individuals who once worked on collective farms as individual farmers (FAO, 2016)###.

| Year | Men | Women |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 2008 | 93.4 | 6.6 |
| 2009 | 95.7 | 4.3 |
| 2010 | 95.4 | 4.6 |
| 2011 | 95.7 | 4.3 |
| 2012 | 92.8 | 7.2 |
| 2013 | 92.8 | 7.2 |
| 2014 | 86.0 | 13.0 |

Figure [5]: Trends in Dekhan Farm Management by Women and Men, 2008-2014 (%) Source: FAO Gender Assessment, 2016

However, the average size of female-headed farms is smaller than those managed by men. In 2014, women managed an average of 3.5 hectares of cropped land on dekhan farms###. Dekhan farms headed by females also have fewer shares in collective farms and are less likely to be registered as individual farms. Customarily, family plots and presidential lands are registered to the household head, most often a man. Furthermore, documents that stipulate the right to use collective farm land are issued in the name of the male head of the family, even when women are owners; but instead are issued in the name of the male head, most often his husband, brother, or if a woman is widowed, her eldest son (ADB, 2016)###.

The National Association of Dehkan Farms (NADF)

72. ### Women access to Agriculture inputs

There are no sex disaggregated data on the availability of key agricultural inputs to rural men and women, therefore conclusions can only be drawn from other sources.


---
**Page 140**
---

available information. In general, a lack of key inputs prevents many people, both women and men, from taking up farming as a business. FAO reports that on a survey conducted in Sughd, Khatlon and RSSs, between 73 percent and 78 percent of farmers reported that they lacked access to at least one of the four "lack" of machinery, seeds, fertilizers, chemicals or water as the main impediments to founding a dekhan farm (FAO, 2016)###.

Data also show that the average number of farming machines, for male and female farmers is lacking. However, it is known that the majority of small-scale farmers have very limited access to agricultural inputs and services, which are essential for adopting more intensive practices, such as harvesting by hand and use of traditional tools.

According to the Tajikistan Living Standards Survey administered in 2009, small households headed by women were less likely than male-headed households to be literate. Female-headed households were also less likely than male-headed households that were likely to have access to such resources (3.6 percent compared with 15.8 percent). The same survey found that according to a survey of a relatively small number of dekhan farmers, female farmers experienced more difficulties in accessing agricultural inputs and equipment, having it repaired, and seeing agricultural specialists" (FAO, 2016)###.

When female-headed households do not own farming equipment, they are able to obtain access through other means. The most common coping mechanism for female farmers is to "work their land less intensively," meaning that they use smaller amounts of fertilizer and improved seeds than their male counterparts. For example, 38 percent of HHs used fertilizers, compared with 38 percent of FHH (FAO, 2016)###.

**Women's access to Extension Services**

The lack of agricultural extension and advisory services is another major problem encountered by small-scale farmers in Tajikistan and it is of particular concern for women. Women's ability to access extension services is also constrained by factors such as distance from the service provider, limited education compared with men. Research conducted in Tajikistan on sustainable farming practices to mitigate climate-related risks found that "within families, female-headed households do not seem to benefit from this knowledge" and that "male farm heads enjoy more" (FAO, 2016)###.

**Women access to pasture and representation and decision-making in Pasture Users Associations (PUAs)**

In the livestock sector, one of the most significant issues facing women is the limited recognition of the roles that they play in raising livestock, or the ways in which their contributions are undervalued. While many women have relatively good access to land generally, it stands to reason that those who are more constrained in accessing pastures. While women are well represented in PUAs (46.7 percent of all members), they hold only 10.5 percent of the leadership positions and 23.8 percent of all management positions are held by women (31.4 percent) (FAO, 2016)###.

80. Similar estimate for woman participation is also registered in the IFAD funded Livestock and Pasture Development Project II (LPDP-II) Mid Term Review Report (September 2019). As reported in the LogFrame, all PULs boards have maintained women representation of at least 30%. This was verified during field visits and acknowledged the need for some action to strengthen the role of women and their leadership.

This has been incorporated in the CASP+ design mission and integrated into the Gender Action Plan (GAP).

## Women's access to water and representation in Water Users Association (WUAs)

82. A recent study of Women’s role in irrigated agriculture conducted in the lower Vakhsh River Basin (ADB, 2020) indicates that females comprise only 3.8% of total farmers in the region. While this is a significant improvement from past statistics not available, the gender disaggregated data for the Khatoon province [study area] indicate that of a total cropping area of 281,424 hectares used by delhan farms, women represent less than 5%.

83. According to ADB study, women as decision makers are in the minority or absent among delhan farm managers, and therefore do not participate in decisions of WUAs. Water users who [not] have access to information gathered statistics about their members and users (ADB, 2020). The study revealed that nearly 80% of male respondents stated problems with water supply, while only a small percentage of female respondents stated problems, including lack of water, bad condition of canals, poor quality of irrigation water, low yield due to drought, and unsatisfactory work by WUAs. Around 20% of female respondents experienced verbal or physical abuse when asking for water.

84. About 35% of female farmers reported that the main constraint to productivity was supply of irrigation water. Availability of good quality seeds was also important, particularly for women who were involved in seed multiplication, credit, and machinery services as important factors. About 20% of men and women cited climate change as a serious issue (ADB, 2020).

85. The study revealed that women face significant constraints and inequalities not experienced by males. Clearly, the expanded role of women needs recognition and support in practice as well as in legislation. The role of women in irrigation management is important for improving water productivity through improved crop production process but they may not receive training in participating irrigation management activities. Women’s participation in decision making processes is also important for achieving gender equality and sustainable farming. The study

## Main challenges expressed by women from FGD and proposed solutions

86. During the remote CASP+ Design Mission (April 2021) and also during project Concept Note Formulation (October 2020), the mission team met with women's groups to discuss about key issues in relation to: (i) economic activities, (ii) livelihoods, (iii) access to land and other resources for decision making bodies, (iv) access to key agriculture inputs and NRM among others.

87. The mission interview a total of 31 women through six FGD; four FGDs involved in Hamadon district and two FGDs were held in Sh. Sholim district. Sh. Sholim, Maschoh, Shartus; two FGDs were held during CN development (October 2020) they involved 5 women from Vose and Harmandor, Farkeo, Munshah, Kharabat, and Sh. Sholim districts. The participants included: producers, including women headed households, women leading dehkanan farms, women members of the local farmers' associations, and women members of the PDPFL. Below is reported a schematic summary with main challenges faced by women and solutions proposed which have been taken into account during the design.

| Districts | Production | Main challenges related to economic activities |
| --- | --- | --- |

Legal status of Women

88. In the last decade, the social gender equality framework has been expanded and strengthened through the adoption of new laws and amendments to existing laws, development of national programs, and incorporation of objectives within general policy documents. For the most part, however, such developments have focused on raising awareness for women and girls and eliminating barriers of the realization of their rights rather than equalizing opportunities and opportunities for women for men or addressing gender stereotypes.

Laws and Policies on Gender Equality

89. The Constitution of the Republic of Tajikistan recognizes international law as a component part of the national legal system10, and Tajikistan is a State Party to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW). In 2005, Tajikistan ratified CEDAW. In 2006, the National Assembly of parliament ratified the Optional Protocol to CEDAW, which allows individual women and men to file complaints with the UN Committee on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women. The Constitution also guarantees equal rights for women and gives them an additional remedy for violations of the convention. Important steps have also been taken to implement UN Security Council resolutions on women, peace, and security (1325, 1820, 1889, 1888). Article 17 of the Constitution guarantees equal rights on the basis of sex (Article 17), and principles of equality are enshrined in other laws. These include, for example, the Family Code, the Labor Code, the Land Code, the Criminal Code, the Law on Education, and the Law on Public Health.

90. The State guarantees Equal Rights and Opportunities for Men and Women, which is the only law to define the concepts of gender equality and equal rights and opportunities for men and women on the basis of sex, while distinguishing special measures to protect pregnancy and the health of women and men, and it guarantees equal rights in public authorities, civil service, and other areas.

91. Other legislative reforms are aimed at greater protection of women's rights, for example, laws combating human trafficking (2008), and domestic violence (2011). In 2007, the Law on Marriage and Family was amended to raise the legal marital age to 18 (for both women and men), which brings Tajikistan into line with international standards.

92. Since 2006, Tajikistan has adopted several policy documents, national programs, and strategies that support gender equality goals. Some were intended to implement the National Action Plan for the Elimination of Violence against Women on Combat Trafficking in Persons for 2006–2010, and the State Program for the Prevention of Domestic Violence for 2007-2015. The National Strategy for the Selection and Placement of Capable Women and Girls in Leadership Positions for 2007-2016 (adopted in 2006) aims to mitigate the barriers that prevent women from participating fully in public life.

93. The stand-alone policy, adopted in 2010, the National Strategy on Enhancing the Role of Women in the Republic of Tajikistan for 2011–2020, recognizes a lack of public awareness and understanding about gender equality issues, as well as stereotypes, and the need to implement the formal equality that exists, but it

10 Article 10, Constitution of the Republic of Tajikistan.


---
**Page 146**
---

nevertheless covers areas in which women encounter barriers: political participation, the labor market, entrepreneurship, and education. The strategy lists concrete actions for each sector, but does not establish the responsible agencies, a timeframe with milestones, sources of funding, or a monitoring plan.

The government has incorporated specific gender equality goals into national socioeconomic development strategies, beginning with the adoption of the Poverty Reduction Strategy Paper (PRSP) for 2001–2005 and the PRSPs for 2009 and 2010–2012. These strategies and the current Living Standards Improvement Strategy for 2013-2015 and National Development Strategy for 2015 all dedicate chapters to gender equality, recognizing women’s potential as a key human potential. Inclusion of gender equality targets in these strategic documents ensures that indicators, implementing agencies, and financing are also delineated.

95. Despite a solid legal and policy framework for the protection of the rights of women and girls, and extensive commitments generally, many planning measures are never realized due to insufficient implementation mechanisms, weak monitoring and evaluation systems, and lack of accountability. The Committee’s observations on the sixth periodic report of Tajikistan from the Committee on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW,2018) report the following key recommendations:

• Accelerate, with a view to its adoption, the drafting of anti-discrimination legislation in line with article 3 of the Convention. The draft law prohibits discrimination against women in line with article 1 of the Convention, covering both direct and indirect forms of discrimination. It also prohibits all forms of discrimination, including intersecting forms of discrimination;

• Strengthen capacity-building for members of the judiciary and legal professionals on how to invoke or directly apply the Convention, or interpret national legislation in accordance with its provisions;

• Apply a gender-sensitive approach in the implementation of its legislation, policies, and programs. This will help ensure that equality is promoted, gender inequalities are addressed, and disparities between women and girls are reduced.

• Intensify existing awareness-raising initiatives and provide training to enhance knowledge about the Convention among key stakeholders, including government and law enforcement officials, parliamentarians, judges, lawyers, education and health-care professionals and religious and community leaders!

National mechanisms

96. The Committee for Women’s and Family Affairs (the Women's Committee) was established in 1991, and its authority was increased in 2006, making it the central authority carrying out state policy on protecting women’s interests and rights. The Women’s Committee has a broad mandate that includes cultivating

## Women’s rights, addressing women’s socioeconomic participation, and delivering public services

97. The Women’s Committee operates regional information-consultation and crisis centers throughout the country (110 in total) and funded locally. The Chair of the Women’s Committee is responsible for coordinating all activities of the committee. In 2014, Women’s Committee activities are diverse, ranging from research to consciousness raising, advocacy on women’s rights, training, the monitoring international standard compliance, coordinating government and nongovernmental activity, and training. The Women’s Committee works with, which supports the implementation of other climate change support for labor migrants’ wages, and peace and security issues.

98. The Committee is also part of the coordinating council on Prevention of Violence against Women (PVAW) and consists of representatives from the Ministry of Labour and Social Security, Ministry of Health, Ministry of Internal Affairs, court officials, representatives of the General Prosecutor’s Office and NGOs.

### Gender-based domestic violence (GBV)

99. According to the Demographic and Health Survey Pakistan [TDHS 2017]143, 24% of women age 15–49 have experienced physical violence since age 15, and 17% experienced physical violence in the 12 months preceding the survey. Only two parameters were found to be significantly associated with the experience of spousal violence; it is reported that 31% of ever-married women have experienced physical, sexual, or emotional violence by their current or most recent partner. The prevalence rate has decreased by approximately four percentage points in the five years since the 2012 TDHS. Among ever-married women who were physically abused, 45% reported that they had sustained some form of injury; one in ten women sought help to stop the violence they experienced. Three in four women neither sought help nor told anyone about the violence.

100. In general, women’s experience of spousal physical, sexual, or emotional violence is more common for those who are younger and older age and number of children. Women who are divorced, separated, or widowed are more likely to have experienced spousal violence (47%) than those who are currently married (30%). The prevalence rate varies greatly by region, from 16% in Dushanbe to 43% in Khujand [TDHS 2017]143.

### Accidents and crimes against women

101. According to the report of the Committee on the Elimination of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) on October 18, covering the period 2013–2017, a total of 1,296 complaints of domestic violence were filed with the Ministry of Internal Affairs by district police inspectors, and 250 by specially appointed and trained inspectors for the prevention of domestic violence. The number of women who filed such a complaint, compared with 296 made against men. Only 65 criminal prosecutions were initiated under various articles of the Criminal Code. The commission reviewing the report stressed that there is a great need to increase to domestic violence

### Vulnerability to Climate Change from a gender perspective

102. Tajikistan is at significant risk from disasters triggered by natural hazards. Food security is highly susceptible to drought and transportation links are vulnerable to flooding. Climate change threatens the food security especially for those who depend on agriculture, livestock or fishing as their livelihoods. Women suffer most from this insecurity because they are often responsible for securing income from agricultural activities. In addition, women in Tajikistan have limited access to information and support. Female farmers and female-headed households are frequently among the most vulnerable in rural areas, and often have very little capacity to adapt to climate change.

103. According to the Tajikistan Country Gender Assessment (ADB, 2016) the main indicators of climate change in Tajikistan are rising temperatures, uneven rainfall, and more frequent extreme weather events such as floods, landslides, droughts or flooding and mudslides or droughts. Rural populations are especially vulnerable to climate change given their dependence on small-scale farming and natural resources for livelihoods. Women are particularly vulnerable because they prepare food, assess, participate in decision-making processes related to preparing for heavy rains that ruined crops (ADB, 2016). Similar findings were confirmed by women interviewed through Focus Group Discussions (FGDs) as the project design mission(5).

104. Households headed by females are among the poorest and “often have very limited access to coping strategies” (Oxfam, 2013). Women are also further; they will also be disproportionately impacted by staple goods scarcity. In addition to increased physical vulnerability, women face reduced access to scarce resources, women and children are at risk for illness from substandard drinking water and fuel (Oxfam, 2013).

105. Women’s lack of educational levels, lack of technical knowledge, and limited participation in decision making also impact their climate change adaptability. According to Oxfam, households headed by females that operate small farms exhibit a higher level of vulnerability than those headed by males. This is attributed by males, which may be due to lack of technical knowledge or insufficient financing (Oxfam, 2013). Women who are not able to collect water-related changes have noticeable and immediate effects on women’s options for collecting water. When water sources become scarce, those farmers who are situated further from a water system or who do not have the ability to negotiate their


---
**Page 149**
---

water rights, or who are unable to protect their water access, will lose out first (Oxfam, 2011)**.

---
**Page 150**

109. According to FAO Gender Assessment (2016) research conducted in Tajikistan on sustainable farming practices to mitigate climate-related shocks, knowledge about such practices is low among all farmers, but there are also gender differences. Women generally have less access to information and networks than men do. They mainly obtain information about sustainable practices from other farmers, but, "within villages, families, women's groups, and informal networks that male farm heads enjoy." This lack of knowledge is a key factor in reduced yields and losses.

110. In the Shohim district, for example, women report that they call upon local agronomists when they need assistance with advice about their household plots, but they also turn to neighbors or other family members for information on sustainable practices.

113. Despite the evident gender inequalities in the agriculture sector, the ADB gender assessment points out that women have great potential as agents of change. Rural women are often more innovative and resourceful than men and can provide examples and solutions that they are already engaging in to adapt to climate change. Women also tend to work on smaller plots of land and use environmentally safe practices.

111. Lessons Learned: A case study conducted by CARE international of a small climate-smart farm in the village of Khamar, a mountain community in Tajikistan, demonstrated that simple technologies that women can implement can also have a positive effect on yields. For example, women learned to use plastic film and straw mats that enabled them to start herb and vegetable seedlings in cold weather and increase the growing season. Women also learned about food preservation.

112. The farm is part of CARE's Climate Smart Agriculture (CSA) project. For example, has a component on training and disseminating good practices among women who farm at household level and own and extend their family farms through jomast agriculture. Extension workers teach farmers how to improve their knowledge of new farming practices (for example, selection of high-quality seed-pops, pest and weed control, and improved soil management) as well as techniques for better storage and food preservation), which has led to increased crop yields for women.

113. Care International. 2016. Climate Change Brief: Adaptation, Gender and Women's Empowerment Document available at: https://www.care.org/wp-content/uploads/2015/07/2016-CARE_Gender_Brief.pdf


---
**Page 151**
---

113. **Recommendations:** The ADB country gender assessment highlights that household-level interventions targeting women are crucial in order to improve their awareness of climate change adaptability. But efforts are also needed at the local level to provide training and support for women on water resource management, on adaptive and mitigative farming,” and to increase information about women’s rights with support through investment, credit, and technology. Furthermore, it is recommended that the government should consider how climate change impacts and decision-making processes on climate resilience and livelihoods (e.g., introducing and using alternative crops) need to be considered (ADB, 2016).

Policy Review: Gender and Climate Change

114. The National Strategy for Enhancing the Role of Women in the Republic of Tajikistan includes a number of gender-sensitive adaptation measures, which also aim at reducing the impact of climate change on women. The National Action Plan under the National Strategy on Activation of Women’s Role in Tajikistan for 2011-2015 is designed to ensure that women are involved in decision-making and raising awareness between Tajik women on climate change that is jointly implemented by the Ministry of Emergency Situations, the Ministry of the Emergency Situation & Civil Defence and local regional bodies.

115. In response for a Decision 23/CP.18 “Promoting gender balance” at the meeting of the Conference of the Parties to the Kyoto Protocol, Tajikistan’s representation of Parties in bodies established pursuant to the Convention or the Kyoto Protocol is reported as being very limited (if any) and/or quite limited for supporting these activities (gender and climate change). It is reiterated that further funding consideration should be given to adaptation programs and projects aimed at reducing vulnerability of women and children in rural areas in order to reduce the adverse effects of climate change on vulnerable groups. The government should also provide training, education, and climate change awareness trainings; assistance for gender capacity building within the framework of the National Strategy for Enhancing Women’s Role.

116. In the National Development Strategy of the Republic of Tajikistan for the period until 2030, issues of gender equity and climate change are coincidently addressed in the same section. The government acknowledges that the main problems for Tajikistan in recent years have been a high level of risk of natural disasters, including due to climate change, from which first, women and children suffer.

117. To create incentives for reduction impact of climate change considering gender aspects, it is suggested to develop and implement mechanisms for reducing social vulnerability due to climate change. This includes the implementation of gender-sensitive information support and training of the population in problem areas on adaptation measures. The government should also work to develop a system for implementing climate change issues, disaster prevention in strategic regional documents, and strengthening local disaster risk management capabilities.

118. In the draft National Strategy for Adaptation to Climate Change of Tajikistan until 2030 (MCC), gender issues are addressed in a cross-sectoral

area. The draft of NACC lists gaps that need to be overcome to ensure that the risks and impacts of climate change capture the gender dimension. The Strategy highlights that the main gender gap in climate change, first, is the low level of women’s participation in decision-making on climate change, lack of decision-making power to take adaptation measures11.

### 119. Gender aspect of climate change are also found in the National Communication under the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change. So far, Tajikistan has prepared three National Communications of the Republic of Tajikistan under the UN Framework Convention on Climate Change. The first National Communication was prepared in 2002, the second in 2008, and the third in 2014. In the third communication, gender equality is one of the main areas covered. It also includes assessments of women’s reproductive health vulnerability to climate change12.

### 136. It is reported that the impact of climate change does not only threaten lives and property but also affects the social and economic development of countries and increases the inequalities between women and men. Gender equality and opportunities for women are among the main areas covered in the agenda of concern covered by the UNFCCC. It is stressed that strategies on climate change and risk mitigation cannot be successful without the participation of women. Thereby, it is necessary to ensure gender equality and opportunities which affect gender equity issues in all areas including adaptation, risk mitigation, knowledge transfer, and so on.

## Project details

### Access to information and opportunities under CASP+

121. Gender mainstreaming in the project will be done with a focus on gender responsive and equitable participation for development planning and implementation, as well as ensuring participation of women and other vulnerable groups in project implementation and community representation and decision-making processes. The project will ensure that (i) gender equality is one of the main areas covered in CASP+ communication, (ii) a comprehensive participatory approach in identification of development needs with specific focus on women’s and men’s roles and responsibilities in the planning and decision making process such as PUIU committees, village organizations (VOs) community representatives, etc., (iii) gender equality is one of the main areas covered in CASP+ communication, (iv) a comprehensive participatory approach in identification of development needs with specific focus on women’s and men’s roles and responsibilities in the planning and decision making process such as PUIU committees, village organizations (VOs) community representatives, etc., (v) gender equality is one of the main areas covered in CASP+ communication, (vi) a comprehensive participatory approach in identification of development needs with specific focus on women’s and men’s roles and responsibilities in the planning and decision making process such as PUIU committees, village organizations (VOs) community representatives, etc., (vii) gender equality is one of the main areas covered in CASP+ communication.

122. One of the Project's objectives is to ensure that all project targets groups (men, women, young people, including vulnerable such as women-headed households, and other vulnerable groups) have access to adequate resources from the Project, especially in terms of information. For this reason, there is a plan to organize training sessions for all project target groups on gender equality opportunities, including awareness raising sessions with community leaders and men, on the role of women in the development process through their

representation in grassroots community organizations. Their awareness will also be raised on the benefits the community can derive from the involvement of women in these development processes.

123. The communication and mobilisation strategy will also be supported by a strong dialogue process that will help identify the main environmental challenges affecting the communities (including from a gender perspective) and propose solutions to address them. This will be done through participatory workshops under the project. This includes opportunities for Climate Sensitive Community Action Plans (CSCAPs) as well livelihood diversification through Common Interest Groups (CIGs). The women will also participate in these dialogues, besides trainings through participation in Farmers Fields Schools (FFS).

124. Access to information by women at village level largely depends on the channel they use for accessing such information. The communication and mobilisation Project will conduct separate consultations with women, using existing formal/informal women groups as villages. These meetings will be held during the project implementation period. The sessions will be conducted by community facilitators (CF) in every targeted village. The CFs will be trained on gender issues and will ensure that both men and women from very poor households (below poverty line). The consultation will be in line with women's need to accommodate proper time and location. The ToRs of communication and mobilisation activities will be developed accordingly.

125. A strong monitoring process during the information/mobilization activities will be put in place to ensure that information reaches the intended beneficiaries (including vulnerable socio-economic categories) and indicators will be gender disaggregated.


### Women's access to education, technical knowledge, and skills

126. As reported in the gender assessment women have lower level of education, technical knowledge and skill to apply climate smart agriculture practices. Women are less likely to attend trainings and they account for small percentage of training recipients.

127. An example from ADB survey (as reported in the Country Gender Assessment 2013) shows that women farmers in the region are more likely to have no specialized training than men and specifically to lack vocational training (19.3% of men as compared to 7.7% of women) and higher education (5.1% of men and 2.7% of women).

128. CASP+ remote design Mission organized a separate Focus Group Discussions (FGD) with women's participants (26) from the districts of Hamdan, Mashcho, Sharjah, and Al Ain. The women expressed their interest in increasing their knowledge about improved agriculture practices; (ii) marketing and (iii) climate smart agricultural practices. They also expressed their interest to increase production to commercial agriculture. They also show interest to increase knowledge on how to improve the quality of their produce. Women expressed their constrained by:(i) their limited agricultural technological knowledge, and (ii) lack of training relevant to areas where they could start businesses. They also highlighted that women are not involved in decision making.

129. Most of the women interviewed, had rarely received training (on agricultural techniques, financial management of their operation and climate smart agriculture practices). The women who have received training were mainly training for women (50% beneficiaries) in Farmers Fields Schools (FFS) and Common Interest Groups (CIGs).


36


---

Interests Groups (CIG) as well as trainings on climate smart resilient technologies and business trainings: farm as business, business planning, proposal writing. This is key to support preparation of business plans and access grant financing schemes.

Women's access to services and technologies provided by CASP+

130. The project has a strong focus on gender-equality. Access to services and technologies provided by the project will equally benefit men and women. Design of services and promotion of technologies are also taking into account the different needs, roles and constraints faced by men and women in their economic activities they are engaged.

131. The assessment finds that the productivity and resilience to climate change of traditional livestock production systems (where majority of women are engaged) is limited by the poor capacities of farmers on animal husbandry, in particular related to feed management and disease prevention and control. This is due to the availability of and awareness on technical innovations that could improve productivity and resilience of these systems, which are not widely disseminated. This is particularly relevant for women as engaged during the FDGs. The project will support the dissemination of these technical innovations and their adoption by smallholder farmers through a combination of demonstrations and hands-on training activities.

132. As part of the activities planned under component 2: specifically, activity 2.3.2. Support to livestock production systems, it is estimated that in 2025, women will account for 59% beneficiaries corresponding to about 2,000 women (20%). This is a significant increase compared with the current situation where only able to access the main climate resilient technologies that will be disseminated:

- New varieties and species of drought and heat resistant fodder;
- Afforestation and agroforestry techniques, in order to reduce seasonality of production and dependence on feed;
- Biogaz production (in order to reduce utilization of wood, dried cow plants and cow dung as fuel);
- Conservation agriculture;
- Husbandry of alternative livestock species, not or less dependent of pasture resources, and resilient to climate change;
- Prevention and management of animal diseases;
- Reproductive health (detection of heifers, calving and calf care, drying off management).

133. In communities where the project will support marketing and processing of milk, milk hygiene and quality improvement as well as value addition will also be addressed under this activity. Based on the livelihood analysis and the role of women in agricultural activities, it is expected that women will be particularly suitable for women. It is also expected that their presence will be particularly prominent given their strong engagement in livestock related activities. The type of proposed training and technical assistance to be provided should therefore be expressed by women during consultation through Focus Group Discussions (FDGs).

134. It is also suggested as a key recommendation in the Project Implementation manual, that the project team should ensure that women are fully involved at all levels. Furthermore, the mission acknowledges the need expressed by women during


---
**Page 155**
---

consultation to have separate trainings groups (only women FFS/groups). In line, the project will form only women FFS (where needed) and time and location for schools will take into account women's needs. FFS facilitators, will be trained to provide services and training in a way that is sensitive to women's interest far women, coupled with increased gender sensitiveness of FFS facilitators, will ensure women access to services, trainings and technologies promoted.

134. The topics for the trainings will include: climate change adaptation and conservation (contribution to climate resilience), as well as milk hygiene (needed for market access) and processing. All topics are of interest for women, however, as research shows that women are more interested in climate change than men, participants attending to their needs and this will also take into account women's need for income generation.

136. Furthermore, women will access information about climate resilient technologies and innovations that have been tested, adapted and validated by research institutions and other organizations. The project will also provide training on how to enable men and women farmers to acknowledge their benefits and feasibility, and disseminate them further popularized and tested in real farm conditions through FFS.

137. In order to allow access to field day demonstrations, field days will be organized at least once a month for 50% of the participants.

138. In addition to participation in FFS women will also be trained by CGI (Activity 3.3.1). Identify individuals and establish Common Interest Groups around climate smart technologies and business planning. The training on climate smart resilient technologies and organization of business trainings; farm as business, business planning, proposal writing to enhance their business capacity and adoption of climate smart technologies is expected to account for 50% of participants for climate resistant technologies and business planning corresponding to 120 CGI groups.

139. A Matching Grant Facility (MGF) will be established and will have MGF Window 1 (livelihood diversification for vulnerable households) and Window 2 (component 4). The project expects that a total of 10,200 households will access 110 Window 1 grants and 2,200 households will access 110 Window 2 grants.

140. Window 1 will be for grants up to $6,000 USD and women will account for 50% beneficiaries of which 20% are expected to be Wives. These grants could be for e.g. small-scale livestock production, poultry production, fish farming, vegetable production, inputs and service provision, drip irrigation, greenhouses, nurseries, beesbeet establishment, etc. The project expects that a total of 10,200 households will match the grant with an 18 percent cash contribution. Window 2 will be for CGI grants that have a minimum of $6,000 USD and maximum of $35,000 USD. Women should account for at least 30% and 30% should be women-led (corresponding to 33 women-led groups).

141. The productive areas to organize women around CGI's should be identified during the baseline survey and through a participatory diagnostic study. As for FFS the training topics, location, time, will take into account the needs of women, to ensure that they are able to participate in the trainings.

142. Component 1: Support to University Students through scholarships. The project will also offer services relevant for young women. In addition of being indirect beneficiaries (as FFS members) the project will provide direct targeted services
