For young girls (students). It is expected that the project will support universities to develop curricula specific on climate change and number of students will be enrolled and some of them will be granted a scholarship.

143. The scholarship available will be for 50 students in total (BA, MA and PhD level) and for 20 years. The objective of this scholarship programme is to increase the climate resilience of communities by enhancing the capacity of young women to manage their livelihoods through sustainable, resilient, diversified production systems. Vulnerable youth from the project areas who are interested in studying a bachelor's/master's/PhD programme (e.g., bee-keeping, agroforestry, livestock management) at any of the universities listed below will be identified. Their scholarship should cover expenses so that they can complete their studies and return to their communities with knowledge and climate resilience of production systems. During their studies, students should have access to training in sustainable livelihoods and climate change adaptation and climate resilience in local public institutions or to develop start-ups. Support for start-ups on an individual or collective basis could be provided through the project. The project will also provide support to young women in the development of a start-up programme so close coordination with them in the identification of their needs is required.

144. Once youth return to their villages, they should be involved in the preparation and implementation of the CuCAPs to share their knowledge and expertise. This is an important step for the project as it will ensure that women are involved around certain activities to manage NRM and increase their climate resilience.


### Women in decision-making

145. Women's decision-making role is limited, although it varies depending on the type of crop under consideration and if it is a household or community level. From the consultation with women in FGDs it appears that consultation among family members and joint decision-making is prevalent. This include experience with maize, sorghum, millet, cowpea, groundnut and beans in the domain of women who decide on type and quantity of crops, how much to spend on inputs such as seeds, fertilizers and pesticides.

The same apply for milk-product, although sale of animals remain in the domain of men.


146. From the consultation from remote with women through FGD, it also emerged that younger women, and particularly daughters-in-law, have few assets and little decision-making power within their households as older female household members exist. Elder women are best positioned to make decisions that are accepted by other household members.

147. Convincing women of the importance of joint decision-making within Takô households. However, due to male outmigration, women become de facto heads of the households and are responsible for plots may be left to be operated by female heads, given for use of close relatives or rented out. This is also to formalize the land rights in the name of their wives to prevent additional problems.

148. Consultation with female dehkan farmers during FGDs confirm that they make most of the production decisions alone or with the support of male family members. Women are responsible for all aspects of crop production except milk and dairy products is entirely controlled by women while decision over livestock is controlled by men.


39


---
**Page 157**
---

149. At community level most of decisions are taken by male household members, as they are represented in higher percentage in Community Based Organisations. Presence of women in village development committees (as reported from women interviewed) is compared to men and the lack of women skills to have their voice heard. This is confirmed with the views of women in decision making and as per data reported in this assessment in relation to women in PUsUs and WUAs.

### Promotion of Women Leadership under CASP+ 150. Women limited capacity to assume leadership role is often associated to cultural constraints and other factors which include:

- Loss of income due to unpaid domestic work, discrimination and gender stereotyping, where women are generally viewed to be unfit for leadership structures.
- Limited opportunity to engage full time in activities outside the home due to unequal burden of care work that falls upon them;
- Low self-confidence and inadequate leadership skills and experience as a result of the above factors.

151. In light of the above constraints which were also identified in the target areas through focus group discussions, it is recommended that women leaders and women's leaders to increase their awareness about their rights, their presence in the decision making process and their representation at all levels so they can express their opinion/voice. Specifically, the project will set quotas to ensure a minimum representation of women in decision making and representation positions.

152. In line with available data it is confirmed that women are 30% representative in PUsUs. The same strategy for quotas is also applied by the CASP project category to have at least 30% women representatives (WUsUs, VOIs). CASP will apply quotas for women as decision makers in key committees such as Pastoralists' Group Committees (PGCs), PUGs), Forest Management Groups (FMGs) and Village Organisations (VOs).

153. Women participation as registered in the IFAD funded Livestock and Pasture development project (LPP) is 20%. This is lower than the 40% reported by the findings emerged from Focus Group Discussion (FGDs) held with women groups during the baseline survey (2016) and the 35% reported in the CASP+ baseline (2017 respectively) where women interviewed (some of them members and representatives) confirmed that they are not represented on village board representatives and recognized the need to receive leadership trainings. Key notes from the consultation are available in the stakeholder consultation report . In order to ensure that women leaders and women's groups continue to perform an active role, the following activities are planned:

- Raise the awareness of women and men leaders and members of CRBs (i.e. VOIs and PUGs) on the effects of discrimination against women on the personal and interpersonal growth of individuals and the impact it has on their participation in the development of the organisation. The activity will be part of gender awareness session taking place in all targeted districts (1 day workshop).


---
**Page 158**
---

155. The awareness session will also include briefing on women’s legal status ( equality of rights) and legislation about women in representation and key position as outlined by the national gender strategy. Other gender sensitive topics to be covered are: women’s access to land, women and climate change as well as women’s access to land (among others).

156. Trainings for women leaders, targeting 6 women per village, for a total of 2,400 women leaders in the 21 districts, will be conducted by community facilitators and members of community organizations to be leaders and change agents in their organizations. They will be provided with sensitization on topics including gender relations, gender equality, gender-based violence, gender roles, gender mainstreaming, advocacy and lobbying, effective communication. The training will specifically focus on the following areas: gender analysis; gender sensitivity; conflict and communication; group management; coping with challenges/conflict resolution strategy; and personal development.

157. The module “Personal Development”, provides the trainees with tools for self- analysis, reflection and development. This module is seen to be critical because of the challenge that women face in terms of their ability to effectively participate in leadership structures if they know how to constantly check themselves for deep-seated internalization of gender stereotypes, as well as to develop a sense of self-confidence.

158. The training is planned to take place during year 1 with refresh training every year conducted by community facilitators. A specialised service provider also conducts the training in the districts. The trainings are expected to be held in the 21 districts covering 400 villages. Toll and tasks are described in the PIM.

164. The outcome of the consultation is to ensure that women’s needs and priorities are identified and that during the community prioritisation process for public and private investments, they are participating (at least 50% of workshops participants) in decision-making at all levels.

165. The gender strategy of CASP+ is informed by the Gender Assessment and recognizes that rural women in Tajikistan play a key role in the natural resource management, and that they have a high stake in both climate change adaptation and mitigation.

By promoting positive shifts in the natural resource management, CASP+ will provide a major contribution to mainstream gender empowerment, increased access to resources and opportunities for women facilitated by the state on an equal basis. Activities will contribute to: (i) increase gender awareness and dissemination of information about the importance of women’s equal access to common resources and (ii) increase women’s resilience, economic and social empowerment through access to market opportunities, credit and land.

It also reflects the understanding that women’s equal participation as active actors and agents of change in the project needs to be facilitated through a set of specific actions. These include: (i) increasing women’s awareness and skills; the gender strategy therefore aims to use every possible opportunity in the project actions to advance towards gender equality and women’s rights.

Underlying principles that key features of the gender strategy are as follows:

Women will particularly participate in the project implementation at all levels and benefit from its opportunities. A minimum of women's quotas will be set for specific activities.
- The project, through the inclusive participation of stakeholders will support the strengthening of gender mainstreaming in the policy dialogue. Gender and climate change issues will be addressed during the planning phase and gender workshops at national level involving key stakeholders engaged in policy formulation.

Women’s informed engagement in decision making processes on related matters (e.g. livelihood adaptation, natural resource management) – both at community and household levels - will be facilitated.
- Opportunities for women to participate in leadership roles, as well as their leadership and decision-making opportunities, will be identified and supported. The project will ensure that women are represented and involved at decision-making level in the executive committees of Pasture Users Unions (PUUs), Village Organisations (VOs) and Forest management groups (FMGs).
- Needs for capacity building and training on gender issues will be identified and acted upon, and all trainings will take gender issues into consideration in the modules, selection of participants, communication and mobilization channels, selection of venues and logistical issues.

# Preventing increased risks of SEAH and GBV

The engagement of women in project’s activities and their acquisition of new skills may increase the risk of exposure to sexual exploitation and abuse (SEAH), and/or gender-based violence (GBV). For prevention of SEAH and GBV, the project staff will train project-related personnel on the subject and sensitizes and mobilizes village heads/chiefs and relevant community members. The project will also engage a gender equality and social inclusion specialist who shall be focal point for advice on SEAH related matters and monitor proper implementation of any activity/mobilization measure as required.

To minimize any possible risks, the project will consider the following options during implementation:

* Increase local organizations’ engagement to work with local leaders and male household heads to promote and campaign promise for mobilization on gender equality and against gender biases and GBV.
* Conduct a needs assessment and develop action plans/strategies for consultations while finalizing and designing the various sub-programme activities. These have to include safe spaces/women-only focus groups to encourage women’s meaningful participation in all aspects of project implementation.
* Sensitize the IP as to the importance of addressing SEAH in the programme, and the need to ensure that it is not overlooked.
* Work with local government or authorities to sensitize community members on SEAH issues.
* Identify male champions where applicable to act as allies on SEAH safeguarding.
* Provide SEAH training to project stakeholders and communities.
* Provide in the community on SEAH risks, how to report them and the services available.

168. The Project Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) will be strengthened so that SEAH issues are addressed in a timely manner with survivor-centred and gender-responsive ways. Affected women and men will be able to file complaints and grievances against the project, IFAD and


---
**Page 162**
---

project executing entities (EEs) will inform communities about the GRM through culturally appropriate mechanisms, ensuring information on the mechanisms at all three levels is communicated. Principles to be followed during implementation include respect for cultural diversity and sensitivity, respect for human rights, equality, transparency, honesty, and mutual respect.

Affected women and men can make a complaint or appeal on any and all aspects of sub-activities' design and implementation. A complaint and grievance mechanism (GRM) will be established and a complaints and appeals mechanism will be developed under the project and distributed to all project communities for their information. The mechanism may be used orally, in writing (digitally or via post) in a culturally appropriate manner.

Project beneficiaries will be clearly informed of the complaint and appeal channels available to them through the project's GRM. Beneficiaries will have access to various forms of communication that are convenient to them (including local languages where suitable). The project will ensure that all project communities are fully informed of the project and GRM, and on the information on the GRM provided to ensure they can exercise their rights under the project. Further information on the project's GRM is provided in appendix 4 of Annex E (SECAP) to the Funding Proposal.

The project's social inclusion specialists will be part of stakeholder and community consultation throughout implementation, undertake regular consultations with women and men, and engage with other vulnerable groups. All groups will be reminded of the GRMs and made aware of the complaint channels available in the [project/program area], which can be accessed even if a survivor does not have access to the project/program's GRM.

The project will ensure that awareness check mitigation measures are consistently being implemented, e.g., undertake spot checks to see if concepts include clauses such as "women and men" or "all genders". The project will be using awareness-check reporting mechanisms, use monitoring visits to check whether awareness-raising materials are clearly visible and awareness-raising exercises are being delivered and engaged with.

### CASP+ Social inclusion strategy for vulnerable groups

176. Gender assessment and stakeholder consultations informed that certain groups in the rural community are socially disadvantaged, particularly poor families, women-headed households and youth, due to their weak social and economic standing.

177. The project therefore incorporates special action to ensure their participation in the project. It is expected that youth will be 30% participants in key consultative working groups and 40% of all FFSs will be youth, with at least 25% being FFSs and actively participating in CIGs (40%).

178. Youth (age=14–30) are largely unemployed, underemployed, and underpaid, and they rank among the most vulnerable groups. The current youth unemployment (working age 15-24) is at 20.9% (WB, 2020). According to the WB (2018) study on addressing challenges in Tajikistan’s labor market, women aged between 15 and 64 years old make up 37% of Tajikistan's total working age population are in the labor force. The majority of those employed young people are in agriculture, which is a seasonal occupation. Seasonal jobs in Tajikistan are seasonal or temporary, and their share has increased over time. Women and youth are the least represented in the labor force. Inactive youth (age 15–29) make up about one-third of the total youth population, which is high by international standards. While youth are more likely to be employed than inactive young people, only a small proportion of employed young people are in unpaid (informal) jobs, compared to 15 percent of adults.

For purposes, in addition to ensuring their equal access to project information and benefits, particular attention will be paid to promote their engagement in business related capacity building activities. It is expected that youth will be 40% participants in CIGs. It is also expected that 25% of common interest groups (CIG) will be youth-led. The final selection criteria for evaluation of grant proposal include priority for youth.

180. Workshops bringing together different partner groups in the Value Chain will be provided where they can share their experiences, exchange and share information with each other and discuss specific problems within the value chain. This will be done as part of PPP approach and the programme will ensure that this social category

The minutes of meetings and other related community records are duly kept as part of M&E requirements including the way potential complaints are addressed.

Part II Gender Action Plan

The Gender Action Plan has been developed in alignment with national policies and strategies to promote gender equality. The National Strategy on Enhancing the Rule of Women in the Republic of Tajikistan for 2011–2020 as well as the National Development Strategy of the Republic of Tajikistan for the period until 2030. Both State documents take into account the impact of climate change and its disproportionate effect of climate change on women. Furthermore, The GAP aligns with gender specific recommendations made by the United Nations Commission on Human Rights in the Republic of Tajikistan on the UN Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC, 2014) and the Sixth National Report on the Convention of Biological Diversity (CBD, 2019).

These policies recognize that promoting gender equality and empowering women is integral to addressing climate change. Women play multiple critical roles as educators, caretakers, practitioners, and agents of change in climate resilience the country. The GAP recognizes that women are disproportionately affected by the disproportionate effects of climate change on women and acknowledge (i) the need to enhance their capacity for decision-making; (ii) the need to increase participation in decision-making at all levels (iii) increase skills, knowledge and access to climate adaptive technologies for women working in agriculture. (iv) provide opportunities and strategies for women’s empowerment and gender equality; (v) develop policies for gender-sensitive strategies and policies of adaptation to climate change (vi) develop gender-sensitive strategies and policies of mitigation.

The GAP focuses on women's visibility and agency as farmers and more broadly climate change agents at the community, governate and national level. It recognizes that as a result of differences in socially constructed gender roles and social norms, women are disproportionately affected by the impacts of climate change differently and differentiated strategies are needed.

Consequently, CASP + adopts a two-pronged approach to mainstreaming gender in the design and implementation of its activities. The first is that all the activities undertaken by the project are gender inclusive and the second is to explore specific activities can be included to enhance women’s agency in dealing with climate change through increasing their capacity for decision-making at all levels (i.e. leadership trainings to increase women leadership skill in decision making).

Under Component 1 the project will increased general awareness and dissemination of knowledge, under Component 2 CASP + will increase women's decision-making and access to resources and services and activities in C3 will include climate change economic and social empowerment through access to knowledge, services, capital and markets.

The project is designed to deal with the specific issues that women face with respect to their roles and responsibilities at the farm level in their role on the homestead plots, backyard poultry, livestock feeding, kitchen gardens etc as well as at community level. The project will increase women’s agency to deal with climate change by ensuring that women are key participants in the decision regarding the Climate Sensitivity Action Plan (CSAP) and the Climate Change Adaptation Strategy as well as private investments from livelihood diversifications and improvements through their inclusion in the CSAP and CCA. In addition, the project recognizes that women-headed households are especially vulnerable to climate change risks and often do not have the resources to adapt to


---
**Page 170**
---

them. Thus women-headed households will be given a priority in terms of their inclusion and selection in project activities especially in participation in FFS, CIGs.

Allocated budget for activities directly supporting women under the Gender Action Plan account for USD 51 millions.

# Project: Enhancing Climate Resilience and Sustainable Development

## Overall Goal:
The overall goal of the project is to contribute to the country's NAP towards low emission sustainable development pathways and climate-adaptive agricultural production practices.

### Specific Objective:
The specific objective of the project is to increase public sector coordination and technical capacity for building climate resilience at the national level and enhancing capacity at the district and village levels for climate adaptive planning and mitigating the impact of climate change and building more resilient livelihoods, particularly in agriculture.

## Contribution to Women's Empowerment Agenda:
By promoting positive shifts in the natural resource management, CAHP presents a major opportunity to mainstream gender equality into national development strategies. The project will contribute to women’s empowerment agenda by increasing their awareness and dissemination of knowledge; increase women’s decision-making and access to common resources and increase women’s resilience, economic and social empowerment through improved livelihoods.

### Component 1: Strengthening enabling conditions for transformative climate adaptive natural resources management
#### Output 1.1: Capacities of relevant national institutions for climate-resilient natural resource management are enhanced

#### Output 1.2: Creating an enabling environment for climate adaptive, inclusive and integrated management of pasture, forestry and livestock resources is enhanced

### Gender-sensitive Budgeting
- **Budget gender sensitive (sub-activity 1.1.4):** Develop gender-sensitive curricula integrated into the CC curriculum developed by the project for schools and specialized training institutions.
    - Number of gender modules prepared: 5
    - Timeline: By end of year 1
    - Responsibilities: To develop the curricula
    - Budget: $5,000

- **Budget gender sensitive (sub-activity 1.1.2):** Provide scholarships for male and female youth in training curriculum on climate-resilient natural resources management.
    - Number of scholarships granted to end of project:
        - Female students: Target > 15 or up to 30%
